  [G1.2] Tax status stated in the report is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states on page 19: 'You have not yet triggered the MPAA' (Minimum Pension Accrual Allowance). The report asserts mpaaTriggered: false.
    File:     The case file contains policy information documents from five pension providers (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) and a Fact Find document. None of these documents contain any reference to MPAA triggering status, MPAA protection, or any indicator of whether the client has triggered the MPAA. The Fact Find document records personal details, employment status, income, and expenditure but contains no tax status information. The provider documents contain standard tax-related disclaimers and regulatory information but no client-specific MPAA status.
    The report asserts that the client 'has not yet triggered the MPAA' on page 19. This is a specific client tax status assertion. The ValueMatch check identified that the figure 19 (page number) does not match any corresponding passage figure, with the nearest being 18 in [P12], 5.3% away — however, this is a page reference, not a substantive data point. The substantive assertion about MPAA status cannot be corroborated or contradicted by the retrieved passages. No document in the case file — neither the Fact Find, the policy information documents, nor the suitability report itself — contains supporting evidence, contradictory evidence, or any related calculation that would allow comparison. The policy documents do not disclose MPAA status for any existing arrangements. The Fact Find does not record tax status, MPAA status, or lifetime allowance protection status. The absence of supporting evidence is notable: a client-specific assertion about tax status (MPAA triggering) is made without any recorded basis in the file. However, this is not a contradiction between two sources; it is an unsupported assertion. Under the decision logic, where 'the report asserts something no document supports,' this constitutes a potential concern. The guards specify that 'only flag where a figure is applied to this client' — and here the MPAA assertion is applied directly to this client's tax position. The guard on 'Generic legislative statements' does not apply, as the report is not making a generic statement; it is stating 'You have not yet triggered' (client-specific). The tax treatment asserted without a recorded tax position meets the evidence-absent criterion: 'Potential Concern: tax treatment asserted without a recorded tax position.'

  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five employment details: (1) Client is Employed as an HGV driver through an agency (page 3); (2) Client has additional income of £20 per week from a volunteer role with the Sea Cadets (page 3); (3) Client expects to retire within the next two years, inferred as intended retirement date 2027-10-14 (page 3); (4) Kim (spouse) is Employed and earns approximately £1,100 per month net (page 3); (5) Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, inferred as intended retirement date 2026-10-14 (page 3).
    File:     The fact find [P5] and [P13] evidence: Client employed as HGV Driver with employer CDH Recruitment, with net basic monthly income of £1,200.00. State pension income listed at £230.00 per month (£2,760 annually, not £11,973 as stated in report [P7]). Sea Cadets volunteer income of £20 per week is corroborated in [P15]. Kim's employment and £1,100 monthly net income is corroborated in [P7]. No retirement dates or ages are explicitly stated in the fact find documents [P1], [P5], [P11], [P13]. The planning scenarios [P3], [P9] show current age 69/65 (client/Kim as at 2025). If client is currently 69 and retires 'within the next two years' (by 2027), retirement age would be approximately 71, not 66. Kim is currently 65; if she works until age 66, retirement date would be 2026, which matches the inferred date. However, no explicit retirement age of 66 for Kim appears in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - Client intended retirement date stated as 2027-10-14 (within two years) but report quote says 'expect to retire within the next two years' from 2025, which if taken literally means by end 2027; however derived age would be ~71 not aligned with typical retirement ages
      - Kim's intended retirement date inferred as 2026-10-14 (age 66) but no explicit retirement age of 66 stated in evidence — only planning scenario shows she is currently 65
      - Report states Kim receives state pension of £11,973 per annum but fact find [P13] shows state pension income as £230 per month (£2,760 annually), a discrepancy of £9,213 per annum
      - Client's net monthly income shown as £1,200 in fact find [P13] basic income, but report states £300 per week as HGV driver [P15] which equals £1,300 per month, a discrepancy of £100 per month or £1,200 annually
    The employment status (Employed for both client and Kim) is corroborated. The occupations (HGV driver, volunteer Sea Cadets) and income from Sea Cadets (£20/week) are supported by evidence [P15]. Client's employment with CDH Recruitment is confirmed in [P5]. Kim's employment and approximate £1,100 monthly income is supported in [P7]. The intended retirement dates are inferred from the report's narrative but the evidence does not explicitly state retirement ages. The report states 'expect to retire within the next two years' which from 2025 means by 2027, but no explicit age is given. For Kim, the report quotes 'continue working until she reaches 66' and the derived date 2026-10-14 is mathematically consistent if she is currently 65 (per planning scenarios [P3], [P9]) and would reach 66 in 2026. However, the fact find does not explicitly record an intended retirement age for either party. The most significant discrepancy is the state pension income: the report [P7] asserts Kim will receive £11,973 per annum but the fact find [P13] records state pension as £230 per month (£2,760 annually). This is material to the income analysis. There is also a minor discrepancy between the client's reported weekly HGV income (£300/week = £1,300/month per [P15]) and the fact find basic monthly income (£1,200 per [P13]). The guard notes that intended retirement age in a fact find is often superseded by later file notes, but here the issue is that explicit retirement ages are not recorded in the contemporaneous fact find despite the report inferring them. The retirement dates themselves (2027 and 2026) are inferred from narrative statements about timing ('within two years', 'until she reaches 66') rather than explicit dates.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: C1 (John) HGV driver employment income £300/week net (£1,300/month equivalent); C1 volunteer income £20/week net (£86.67/month equivalent); C2 (Kim) employment income £1,100/month net; C1 state pension £230/week net (£1,000/month equivalent); C2 state pension £230/week net (£1,000/month equivalent) from age 67 starting 2026-10-14. Household expenditure £1,700/month. Total monthly income net £4,486.67. Monthly surplus £2,786.67. Retirement expenditure approximately £1,700 annually (stated as monthly equivalent of £1,700).
    File:     Fact Find [P1] and [P10] record: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00 (comprised of Basic Income £1,200.00 monthly and State Pension £230.00 monthly). Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00. Monthly Disposable Income -£288.00. Detailed expenditure breakdown totals £1,718.00. [P10] shows Basic Income employed £1,200.00 monthly, State Pension £230.00 monthly. [P17] report narrative states 'You work full-time as an HGV driver through an agency, typically earning around £300 per week net, with additional income of £20 per week from your volunteer role with the Sea Cadets. Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net. You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report derives total monthly income of £4,486.67 but Fact Find records total net monthly income of £1,430.00 — difference of £3,056.67
      - Report states John's HGV income £1,300/month (from £300/week × 52÷12) but Fact Find records Basic Income £1,200.00 monthly — difference of £100/month or 7.7%
      - Report includes Kim's employment income £1,100/month in affordability total but Fact Find does not show this income recorded in the income section shown — only John's £1,200 and state pension £230 appear in [P10]
      - Report converts state pension £230/week to £1,000/month equivalent but Fact Find shows £230/month recorded; £230/week × 52÷12 = £1,000/month but Fact Find income table lists £230.00 as monthly amount not weekly
      - Report calculates monthly surplus of £2,786.67 from £4,486.67 income minus £1,700 expenditure but Fact Find calculates monthly disposable income of -£288.00 from £1,430.00 income minus £1,718.00 expenditure — opposite sign and magnitude differ by £3,074.67
      - Report shows household expenditure £1,700/month but Fact Find shows £1,718.00 calculated total — difference of £18
      - Report states retirement expenditure 'approximately £1,700' annually on page 14 but narrative and all calculations treat it as monthly equivalent £1,700
    The Fact Find [P1] and [P10] show employment income totalling only £1,430/month net (John's basic income £1,200 + state pension £230), yielding a monthly deficit of -£288. The report asserts £4,486.67 total monthly income and a surplus of £2,786.67. The discrepancy arises from multiple factors: (1) The report's HGV income figure of £1,300/month is derived correctly from £300/week using the guard conversion (52÷12), but the Fact Find records only £1,200/month as 'Basic Income Employed'. (2) Kim's employment income of £1,100/month is stated in the report narrative [P17] as earned by 'Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net' but this income does not appear in the Fact Find income summary table [P10] which only shows two income lines. (3) The state pension is quoted as £230/week in both sources but the Fact Find table [P10] lists it as monthly £230 (treating it as monthly not weekly), whereas the report correctly converts weekly £230 to monthly equivalent of £1,000. (4) Expenditure differs marginally: £1,700 stated vs £1,718 calculated in Fact Find. The core inconsistency is that the Fact Find records total monthly income of £1,430 producing a deficit, while the report's total of £4,486.67 with surplus of £2,786.67 requires all five income sources (John HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + John state pension £1,000 + Kim employment £1,100 + Kim state pension £1,000). The Fact Find [P10] does not record John's Sea Cadets income (£20/week), Kim's employment income in that summary line, or the correct weekly-to-monthly conversion for state pension. Retirement expenditure language ('In retirement...approximately £1,700') is ambiguous whether annual or monthly; the report and all projections treat it as monthly. The withdrawal of £116,555.45 shown as 'Lump Sum Cont.' value in [P19] dated 23/07/2026 does not reconcile to any income figure. The report's derivation of £4,486.67 is arithmetically correct from its stated component figures, but those components are not all evidenced in the Fact Find summary; Kim's income and John's volunteer income are absent from [P10]'s income table.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Client (JS) is in receipt of his full State Pension of £230 per week (net basis). Partner (Kim) will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age. The report states Kim's State Pension age is 67. The report also states client receives state pension income of £11,973 per annum and Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.
    File:     [P1] intelliflo planning Baseline scenario shows STATE PENSION AGE of 66 for one party. [P5] Suitability Report states 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week' and 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'. [P9] Suitability Report confirms 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week, and Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age.' The £230 weekly figure is corroborated in [P9]. [P5] states 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67', confirming the age 67 assertion and the annual equivalent (£230 × 52 = £11,960, approximately £11,973 allowing for rounding).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Kim's State Pension age is 67, but [P1] intelliflo planning Baseline scenario shows STATE PENSION AGE of 66
    The ValueMatch check against passages found 67 not matched exactly; nearest value is 66 in [P1], representing 1.5% variance. The guard states 'State pension age and intended retirement age are routinely conflated in reports.' [P1] shows two separate age entries: one marked 66 (STATE PENSION AGE) and retirement ages of 66 and 71 for different parties. The £230 weekly amount is corroborated by [P9]. The annual figure of £11,973 reconciles with £230 × 52 = £11,960 (within rounding tolerance). However, there is a material discrepancy between the report's assertion that Kim's State Pension age is 67 and the planning document [P1] which lists STATE PENSION AGE as 66. This appears to reflect either a data entry error in one source or conflation of retirement age (intended retirement age 66) with State Pension age. The guard acknowledges this is a common issue but does not excuse the inconsistency—the discrepancy still stands and must be flagged. The £11,973 annual figure itself is internally consistent with the £230 weekly figure stated in the report and corroborated in evidence.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report contains three identified internal inconsistencies: (1) Pension fund value stated as approximately £110,000 in narrative (page 3), £106,000 in capacity for loss section (page 4), and £110,185.47 in the Existing Arrangements table; (2) Charges comparison on page 11 states Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive' but Switch Charges table shows -0.06% impact, and Standard Life text claims '0.08% more expensive' but table shows +0.26%; (3) Critical yield section shows new solution achieving £115,000 vs existing £127,000 at age 75 (decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%), which includes adviser fees despite no existing adviser fees being disclosed on current arrangements.
    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report against itself. There is no external supporting document to corroborate or contradict the internal consistency check. The report's own extracted data and tables are the sole basis for comparison.
    Discrepancies:
      - Pension fund value appears in three different forms: £110,000 (narrative), £106,000 (capacity for loss section), and £110,185.47 (Existing Arrangements table)
      - Aviva switch charge narrative states '0.06% more expensive' but Switch Charges table shows impact as -0.06% (improvement rather than increase)
      - Standard Life charge narrative states '0.08% more expensive' but Switch Charges table shows impact as +0.26% (not 0.08%)
      - Critical yield projections show new solution at £115,000 vs existing at £127,000 (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease) with adviser fees included, yet existing arrangements disclose no current adviser fees
    The report identifies three material internal inconsistencies within its own pages. The first inconsistency involves the pension fund valuation: the narrative on page 3 states 'approximately £110,000', the capacity for loss section on page 4 cites '£106,000', and the Existing Arrangements table totals £110,185.47. The variance between £106,000 and £110,185.47 is £4,185.47 or approximately 3.9%, which exceeds rounding tolerance. The second inconsistency appears in the charges comparison section (page 11): text narrative states the Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive' but the accompanying Switch Charges table records the same comparison as -0.06%, a sign reversal indicating the plan is 0.06% cheaper. For Standard Life, the narrative claims '0.08% more expensive' while the table shows +0.26%, a discrepancy of 0.18 percentage points. These are not rounding differences but material contradictions within the same section. The third inconsistency involves the critical yield analysis (page 18, Appendix): the projections compare an existing arrangement value of £127,000 against a new solution of £115,000 at age 75, a shortfall of £12,000 or 9.4%. The extraction notes indicate this calculation includes adviser charges, yet the existing arrangements table shows no adviser charges currently being paid on any of the five plans, creating an asymmetric comparison. All three inconsistencies are acknowledged by the extraction report's own flagging as internal inconsistencies. No 'as at' dates are stated for the three pension fund figures, so they cannot be reconciled as representing different valuation dates. The charges contradictions are direct and unambiguous: the same comparison cannot simultaneously show both an increase and a decrease. The adviser fee asymmetry means the critical yield comparison is not comparing like with like across existing and proposed arrangements.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the following income figures in different frequencies: (1) £300 per week net from HGV driver employment; (2) £20 per week net from volunteer role; (3) £1,100 per month net for Kim's employment; (4) £230 per week net current State Pension; (5) £230 per week net projected State Pension for Kim. The report derives monthly equivalents: £1,300 (HGV £300×52÷12), £86.67 (Sea Cadets £20×52÷12), £1,100 (stated as monthly), £1,000 (State Pension £230×52÷12), £1,000 (Kim's projected State Pension £230×52÷12). The report totals monthly net income as £4,486.67. The report asserts expenditure of £1,700 monthly net (stated twice: actual and intended).
    File:     The Fact Find [P2] shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Basic Income (Employed, CDH Recruitment) Monthly £1,200.00; State Pension Monthly £230.00. Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00 [P1]. The Fact Find income section [P2] records a total of £1,430.00 net monthly. The Fact Find [P1] records calculated total monthly household expenditure as £1,718.00. The client states a need for £1,700 net per month household income in retirement [P5, P12].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report derives HGV income as £1,300 monthly (£300/week × 52÷12); Fact Find shows Basic Income Monthly £1,200.00 — a difference of £100 or 8.3%.
      - Report states Sea Cadets £20/week; Fact Find does not separately itemise this income line in the income table, making verification impossible.
      - Report derives State Pension as £1,000 monthly (£230/week × 52÷12); Fact Find shows State Pension Monthly £230.00 — these are not comparable (one is weekly conversion, one is monthly statement); however the report's conversion of £230/week to £1,000/month is arithmetically correct (£230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000).
      - Report totals monthly net income as £4,486.67; Fact Find total net monthly income is £1,430.00 — a difference of £3,056.67 or 214%.
      - Report states expenditure of £1,700 monthly; Fact Find shows Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00 — a difference of £18.00 or 1.1%.
    Frequency conversion reconciliation: The report correctly converts weekly figures to monthly equivalents using 52÷12 (not the crude 4.33 guard). Weekly State Pension £230 properly converts to monthly £1,000 (£230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000), which matches the arithmetic guard. However, critical discrepancies exist: (1) HGV income: Report asserts £300/week, derives £1,300/month. Fact Find shows £1,200/month for 'Basic Income (Employed, CDH Recruitment)'. The £100 difference (8.3%) exceeds the 1% tolerance and does not reconcile; this is material. (2) Total monthly income: Report totals £4,486.67 by summing five income lines (£1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,000). Fact Find total is £1,430.00. This massive discrepancy of £3,056.67 (214%) suggests the report is including Kim's income (£1,100 spouse employment + £1,000 spouse State Pension = £2,100 combined), but the Fact Find does not clearly itemise spouse income in the table provided. The report's internal arithmetic is sound: £1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,000 = £4,486.67. But reconciliation with Fact Find is impossible because the Fact Find total of £1,430 appears to include only the client (John), not both household members. (3) Expenditure: Report states £1,700/month; Fact Find shows £1,718.00. The £18 difference (1.1%) is within the 1% tolerance and reconciles. (4) Sea Cadets income: Report asserts £20/week; no separate itemisation appears in Fact Find income table extract [P2], preventing verification. The income was extracted from report narrative, not independently corroborated. (5) The retirement need stated in [P5] and [P12] is £1,700 net per month, which aligns with the report's stated expenditure figure but does not explain the income total discrepancy. The most critical finding is that the report's total monthly income of £4,486.67 cannot be reconciled with the Fact Find's £1,430.00 total; this appears to be driven by different scopes (household vs client only) but the report does not explicitly state this dual-income scenario in its income derivation section, creating internal ambiguity about whose income is included in totals used for planning.

The investment horizon of 10 years stated for recommended funds conflicts with the 2-year retirement horizon, suggesting funds will continue beyond retirement, which is addressed obliquely in the narrative but not explicitly reconciled.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). Report prioritizes objective 1 (lifestyle affordability) as priority rank 1 and objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700 net monthly income) as priority rank 2, but evidence does not explicitly establish relative priority order between these two objectives—they appear co-equal in importance in fact find and file notes. Report states client 'think[s] he'll probably retire in the next 2 years' but planning document [P8] shows specific retirement dates: John retires 2027 (age 71, approximately 2 years from 2025 fact find date) and Kim retires 2026 (age 70, approximately 1 year from fact find date), which is more precise than the report's 'in 2 years' phrasing. Report details consolidation objective separately as part of objective 3 regarding 'clearer understanding of pension arrangements', but evidence [P3] and [P5] present consolidation as a primary goal alongside the retirement income need, not subordinate to understanding arrangements. 1 could not be assessed for want of a value the comparison needs (G2.4). 13 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   Three objectives stated with priority ranks 1-3: (1) Ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime; (2) Retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) Obtain a clearer understanding of pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement. All marked as client-stated (isClientStated: true). Report also details that client 'prefer[s] a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers' and 'consolidate five separate pension plans with different providers into a more streamlined approach for easier management'.
    File:     Fact Find [P3] completed 19/09/2025 records under 'Retirement Planning' category: 'Retirement Planning: Yes' with details 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' File notes [P5] record ATR discussion confirming client 'have confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period'. Intelliflo planning documents [P8] show retirement timeline: 'Retire (John) 2027— 71 /67' and 'Retire (Kim) 2026 — 70 / 66', confirming retirement within approximately 2 years. Suitability report [P2] reiterates all three objectives as agreed.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report prioritizes objective 1 (lifestyle affordability) as priority rank 1 and objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700 net monthly income) as priority rank 2, but evidence does not explicitly establish relative priority order between these two objectives—they appear co-equal in importance in fact find and file notes
      - Report states client 'think[s] he'll probably retire in the next 2 years' but planning document [P8] shows specific retirement dates: John retires 2027 (age 71, approximately 2 years from 2025 fact find date) and Kim retires 2026 (age 70, approximately 1 year from fact find date), which is more precise than the report's 'in 2 years' phrasing
      - Report details consolidation objective separately as part of objective 3 regarding 'clearer understanding of pension arrangements', but evidence [P3] and [P5] present consolidation as a primary goal alongside the retirement income need, not subordinate to understanding arrangements
    Each of the three objectives asserted in the report is corroborated by evidence. Objective 1 (continue to afford lifestyle through lifetime) is supported by capacity for loss discussion in file notes [P5] confirming client 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' and planning projections [P8] showing sustainable asset position through lifetime. Objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700 net monthly income) is directly evidenced in fact find [P3] stating 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' and planning documents [P8] showing retirement timeline and income projections. Objective 3 (clearer understanding and appropriate structuring of pensions through consolidation) is evidenced in fact find [P3] 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' and file notes [P5] discussing consolidation priority. The discrepancies identified do not undermine corroboration: the priority rank ordering is not explicitly stated in evidence but the report's ranking appears reasonable given evidence emphasis; the specific vs. approximate retirement dates are consistent in substance (both indicate ~2 year timeframe); consolidation appears both as discrete objective and component of objective 3, which is consistent with the goal's dual nature (structural and understanding-focused). The isClientStated flag is supported: all three objectives originate from client discussions recorded in fact find [P3] and file notes [P5]. Report wording 'would like' and 'prefer' in objective 3 summary reflect client agency evident in evidence. Objectives are stated with sufficient specificity to be actionable (income target £1,700 net monthly, 2-year timeframe, consolidation to specific platform). All objectives are then linked to recommendations: consolidation achieved through switching to Aviva Platform [P15]; income target addressed through investment strategy and pension drawdown planning [P8]; lifestyle maintenance supported through risk rating 5 balanced approach and capacity for loss assessment [P5]. No vulnerability indicators in evidence materially affect this check's assessment.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a retirement target age of 71 with a term of 2 years. It also states three timeHorizon values: LongTerm, MediumTerm, and MediumTerm. Life expectancy is stated as age 86 for a 69-year-old male and age 88 for a 65-year-old female, both citing Office of National Statistics data.
    File:     The evidence shows multiple retirement dates across different documents. P10 (intelliflo proposed scenario) shows retirement ages of 66 for one party (2026) and 71 for another (2027), with current ages 69 and 65 respectively. P1 and related pension switch reports show a retirement age of 75 as the standard maturity date for the pension plans being switched. P15 (Zurich Policy) states 'Selected Retirement Age was chosen and was fixed at the start of the plan' with a planned retirement age of 75, minimum age 55. P11 (Risk profile) shows an investment period of 2 years with £106,000 investment. P12 (Aviva) references decision required before 75th birthday with automatic annuity at that age. P24 (Suitability Report) confirms the same ONS life expectancy figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts single retirement target age of 71, but P10 shows two different retirement ages: 66 (for one party in 2026) and 71 (for another party in 2027)
      - Report states term of 2 years, but pension plans in evidence repeatedly reference retirement age 75 as the maturity/default retirement date (P1, P12, P15)
      - Mismatch between the 2-year investment horizon in the report/P11 and the multi-year horizon implied by projections to age 75 in the pension switch analysis (difference of approximately 4 years to age 71, or 6 years to age 75)
      - Report provides three timeHorizon classifications (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) without clear link to specific objectives or parties
    The evidence reveals a household with two parties of different ages and retirement dates. P10 clearly shows a married couple: one party aged 69 with retirement at 71 in 2027 (2-year horizon from 2025), and another aged 65 with retirement at 66 in 2026 (1-year horizon from 2025). The report's assertion of a single target age 71 and term 2 years aligns with one party but obscures the household structure. The pension switch reports (P1, P7, P2) consistently reference retirement age 75 as the maturity date for the pension plans, which conflicts with the stated 2-year term. This appears to reflect the contractual default retirement ages on the existing and proposed pension plans, as noted in the guard: 'Selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date.' The 2-year investment period in P11 and the report aligns with the proposed scenario timeline to age 71/66, suggesting the client's intended horizon differs from the plan maturity dates. However, the report does not clearly distinguish between the client's intended retirement dates (71 and 66) and the plan maturity dates (75), nor does it explicitly address both parties' horizons. The three timeHorizon values in the report are not clearly mapped to specific objectives or parties. The life expectancy figures are correctly cited from ONS data and are corroborated by P24.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two access types: (1) Emergency access: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies' with £6,000 held in liquid cash savings. (2) Regular/planned access: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' via Flexi Access Drawdown from pension funds. The report states the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' and that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.'
    File:     The financial planning documents confirm: (1) Liquid assets of £6,000 in a Cash Account (Savings) held in 2025. (2) Regular pension income projections showing stable liquid asset balances throughout retirement (ranging from £6,000 in 2025 to £4,773 in 2056 in the baseline scenario, and £6,000 in 2025 declining gradually in the proposed scenario). (3) State pension income of £11,973 per annum for JS from age 66 and for KS from age 67, plus employment income until retirement. (4) Total regular expenditure of £24,001 in first year per the baseline scenario, declining on death assumptions. (5) Cashflow projections showing no shortfall throughout lifetime to age 100 in both baseline and proposed scenarios, with liquid assets and accessible pensions always sufficient to meet required withdrawals. (6) The fact find confirms £6,000 in cash savings as at 22/09/2025. (7) Pension access options documented for each arrangement include Flexi Access Drawdown (FAD) for Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life and People's Pension; Scottish Widows limited to Full and partial UFPLS.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report mentions 'instance access funds' (likely 'instant access') but the planning documents confirm only 10-15 working day pension access timescales and standard cash account access, not immediate access.
      - The report states ad-hoc spend from pension funds should 'grow for large ad hoc spends' but the planning models show liquid assets declining gradually from £6,000 to near zero by age 102-104, limiting ad-hoc access capacity in later life.
      - The report does not explicitly state a planned withdrawal amount or schedule for ad-hoc access, while the planning documents show continuous but modest liquid asset balances insufficient for significant unplanned expenditure beyond the £6,000 emergency fund.
    The evidence fully supports the emergency access requirement: £6,000 in liquid savings is confirmed across the fact find and planning documents, and the report's assertion that the client has 'confirmed sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies' is reasonable given this amount relative to typical household emergencies and the client's stable pension income. The planned access requirement is also supported: the report correctly identifies Flexi Access Drawdown as the mechanism for regular pension access, the 10-15 working day timescale is stated in the suitability report and is a standard pension administration timeframe, and the planning documents confirm multiple pension arrangements support FAD. The cashflow projections across both baseline and proposed scenarios confirm no shortfall throughout the plan period, validating that access requirements can be met. However, the statement about 'instance access funds' appears to be imprecise language (likely meaning 'instant' or 'in-case') and the characterisation of pension funds as available for 'large ad hoc spends' while they 'keep growing' is somewhat misleading: the planning models show liquid assets depleting over time and pension drawdown is modelled only for regular retirement expenditure, not for ad-hoc needs beyond the emergency fund. The guard 'No access needs identified is a valid evidenced answer' does not apply here, as access needs have been explicitly identified. The two access types (Emergency and Regular) are both evidenced. The missing element is explicit quantification of ad-hoc access amounts or confirmation that pension access beyond regular drawdown is feasible without jeopardising the plan; this remains implicit rather than explicit in the supporting documents.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states three prioritized objectives: (1) 'ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime' (priority rank 1); (2) 'retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month' (priority rank 2); (3) 'clearer understanding of pension arrangements and streamlined approach to managing pensions' (priority rank 3). The retirement target date is 2 years from the fact find date (2025), placing retirement at 2027 when the primary client (JS, born 1956) will be age 71. The investment time horizon is stated as long-term, aligned with the client's lifetime. The financial planning models show projections to age 104/100 (2060). The report recommends switching five pension arrangements into a consolidated Aviva solution on a risk rating 5 portfolio. The critical yield analysis at mid-growth (2.95%) shows the new solution requires 4.8% annual growth to match the existing pension value at age 75, with a projected 9.4% decrease (£12,000) versus the existing arrangement at that growth rate.
    File:     The intelliflo planning Baseline scenario and Proposed scenario documents evidence the client's financial situation from 2025 through 2060. Both spouses have retirement dates: JS (primary client) retires in 2026 at age 70; KS (spouse) retires in 2027 at age 71. The Proposed scenario timeline shows 'Retire' age 66 in 2026 for KS and age 71 in 2027 for JS. The cashflow modelling shows in-year surpluses throughout the plan period (opening 2026 with £40,703 income and £23,886 surplus, declining to consistent small surpluses through 2047 and beyond). The Baseline scenario shows liquid assets and accessible pensions held stable at approximately £122,950–£123,000 from 2025 through 2047, then declining slightly in later years. No shortfalls or required withdrawals appear in the baseline model through the entire projection period to 2060. The household income target of £1,700 net per month (£20,400 annually) is implicit in the expenditure schedules shown (total regular expenditure approximately £24,001 in first year, declining with retirement). The financial plan shows the client can maintain lifestyle indefinitely with current arrangements.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report retirement target: '2 years time' from 2025 = 2027 (age 71 for JS); Baseline scenario shows retirement at 2026 (age 70 for JS) and 2027 for KS, creating ambiguity about whose retirement date the 2-year horizon addresses
      - Report states new solution projected value at mid-growth age 75: £115,000 versus existing £127,000, a 9.4% decrease; this is presented as the outcome of the recommended switch, yet the recommendation proceeds despite this material underperformance
      - Report objective 2 states 'retire in 2 years time' but Proposed scenario shows JS retirement in 2026 (age 70, approximately 1 year from fact find date 2025) and KS in 2027 (2 years), creating internal misalignment about which client and which timeline the objective addresses
      - Report recommends a consolidation strategy to gain 'clearer understanding and streamlined approach' to pensions, yet the critical yield analysis shows the recommendation will deliver materially lower capital (£12,000 or 9.4% less at age 75 mid-growth) than retention, creating tension between the administrative/clarity objective and the wealth preservation objective
    The check asks whether stated client goals are free from material internal conflict. The requirement is met at a structural level: the three objectives (lifetime affordability, retirement with specified income, consolidation) do not claim mutually exclusive resources. However, two substantive tensions emerge when report assertions are confronted with evidence and internal logic. First, the retirement timing is ambiguous: the report states 'retire in 2 years time' (implying 2027), but the Proposed scenario's timeline shows JS retiring at age 70 in 2026 and KS at 71 in 2027. The fact find is dated 19/09/2025, so 'in 2 years time' would be 2027, aligning with KS's date but not JS's. This is not stated as 'KS retires in 2 years; JS in 1 year', leaving the primary objective ambiguous. Second, and more materially, the report asserts objective 2 (retire with specified income) and objective 3 (consolidation and clarity), and recommends a switch to achieve the consolidation objective. Yet the critical yield analysis shows the recommended switch will deliver £12,000 (9.4%) less capital at age 75 under mid-growth assumptions than retention. The financial planning model (Baseline scenario) shows the client maintains positive surpluses throughout retirement even with the existing arrangements; switching materially reduces the capital available to support lifetime affordability (objective 1) without visible evidence that the household's stated income target (£1,700/month net, approximately £20,400 annually) requires the switch or that administrative streamlining justifies the capital reduction. The report does not explain why the 9.4% projected reduction serves the stated objectives, nor does it address whether administrative consolidation outweighs the material wealth reduction. This is a tension between stated prioritization (lifetime affordability ranks first) and the recommendation's outcome (materially lower capital). The evidence shows the existing arrangements already support the stated lifestyle and income targets; the recommendation trades existing adequacy for consolidation with a known capital shortfall. This falls short of full alignment but does not render the recommendation entirely incoherent, as the client may rationally prefer simplicity. However, the objective is stated without explicit trade-off acknowledgment.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report links all five recommendations to three objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) via the linkedObjectiveIds field. The recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio plan specifies: transfer value £110,185 as at 2025-10-14; planned withdrawal type UFPLS starting 2027-10-14; selected retirement age 71; projected values at age 75 under low/mid/high growth scenarios (£97,600/£115,000/£135,000 in real terms); death benefits payable to nominated beneficiaries tax-free before age 75, taxed at marginal rate after. The fund recommendation is stated as 'aligned with your stated attitude to investment risk' and 'based on your retirement objectives in isolation as you requested'.
    File:     P1 records income/expenditure goal and retirement need of £1,700 net per month income in retirement. P10 states client receives state pension income of £11,973 per annum and expects annual expenditure of approximately £1,700 in retirement. P8 shows baseline regular expenditure of £24,001 annually in first year, with total regular income of £41,813. P10 notes that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.' The financial plan in P10 shows cashflow runs to age 100 with no projected shortfall. P6 and P7 detail access options including flexi-access drawdown available from age 55 (57 from April 2028), with up to 25% tax-free lump sum. P3 and P16 explain flexi-access drawdown mechanism. P21 and P23 show Zurich retirement date as age 75; P22 notes Aviva plan accessed via income drawdown from age 55 (57 from 2028). No specific evidence identifies which objectives are OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 or their definitions. No evidence explicitly links access date, term, or withdrawal strategy to each specific objective.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report links all recommendations to OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 but evidence does not identify or define what these objectives are
      - Report states selected retirement age 71 but projections quoted in report are stated 'at age 75' - age mismatch between selected retirement age and projection horizon
      - Report states withdrawal planned to start 2027-10-14 (age 55 for client aged 69 in 2025) but retirement age is stated as 71 - withdrawal start predates selected retirement by 16 years
      - Report specifies UFPLS withdrawal type but also states 'prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' - unclear which mechanism is intended
      - Retirement need stated as £1,700 per month (£20,400 annually) but P8 shows first-year expenditure modelled at £24,001 - discrepancy in retirement expenditure figure
      - Report projects fund value at age 75 (£115,000 mid-growth) but selected retirement age is 71 - no projection given for actual selected retirement age
    The requirement tests whether recommendations are appropriate for stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes. The report asserts that all five recommendations are linked to three objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) but the evidence pack contains no definition or identification of what these three objectives actually are. The guard against boilerplate states that a recommendation linked only by generic language ('this meets your objectives') is not sufficient - a specific objective must be named. Here, the report names the objectives by ID only, without evidence of their content or how each recommendation specifically addresses each one. The evidence shows retirement needs (£1,700 monthly net income required) and a financial plan indicating state pension will cover bills with pension available for ad-hoc spending. However, the connection between this stated need and the specific recommendation of Aviva Personal Pension linked to OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3 is not evidenced. Additionally, there is a material inconsistency: the report states selected retirement age as 71 but provides projections only at age 75, and specifies a planned withdrawal starting 2027-10-14 (age 55) which is 16 years before the stated retirement age of 71. This creates uncertainty about the actual term and access strategy. The flexibility to access from age 55 is documented in product literature (P6, P7), but the report does not explain why access at 55 aligns with objectives when retirement is targeted at age 71. The withdrawal mechanism is also unclear - stated as UFPLS but also described as flexi-access drawdown, with product literature (P16, P22) confirming both are available but the report not specifying which is intended for this client's circumstances. The discrepancy between modelled retirement expenditure (£24,001 annually, P8) and stated retirement need (£1,700 monthly = £20,400 annually) further undermines clarity of goal appropriateness. The report states recommendations serve 'retirement objectives' but without identifying those objectives or demonstrating how the specific product, term, access mechanism and withdrawal strategy are tailored to them, the link is not supported by evidence beyond assertion.

The policy research document [P23] shows objectives were not explicitly linked to specific recommendations at the time of research ('Which objective/goal is this achieving?' is blank), which creates weak justification: the recommendations are not explicitly tied to named objectives. However, the file notes [P1] and Fact Find [P5-P6] provide substantive context showing why consolidation and risk rating 5 funds serve the retirement goal. No assumption stated in the report is contradicted by evidence; all material assumptions appear in the disclosed Cashflow Assumptions section or ATR section. The 2.95% mid-growth rate conflicts slightly with the report's quote stating '2.94%' in the provenance, but this is a minor discrepancy in rounding and the assumption is disclosed. Discrepancies around the 10-year horizon and current negative cash flow do not render the objectives unsupported—they reflect the planning structure (immediate drawdown to bridge shortfall, investment growth for future needs)—but the report does not explicitly reconcile the tension between a 2-year retirement date and 10-year investment term, leaving the suitability of a risk rating 5 strategy for imminent retirement access under-explained.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client wants to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month. The report models retirement income of £1,700 net per month. The cashflow assumptions section states: '0% of any in-year surplus will be saved within the plan - all surplus income is spent.' The report notes: 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.' The report projects income and expenditure to age 100, incorporating state pension income of £11,973 per annum for the client and £11,973 per annum for Kim from age 67. Employment income of £1,100 per month from Kim is included. Annual expenditure in retirement is stated as approximately £1,700.
    File:     The Fact Find (Test 1) records under Retirement Planning: 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The Policy Research document states under Income/Expenditure Goal and Retirement: 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement.' The Fact Find shows total net monthly income of £1,430 (John HGV £1,200 + State Pension £230) and Kim employment income of £1,100 per month, totalling household income available of £2,300 net per month. Current monthly expenditure is calculated at £1,718. The client is shown to currently have a disposable income deficit of -£288 per month before retirement adjustments. The Fact Find notes no expected changes to income or expenditure in the foreseeable future.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states annual retirement expenditure of approximately £1,700 (£1,700 net per month or £20,400 per annum), but the Fact Find shows calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718, which would equate to £20,616 per annum—a discrepancy of £216 per annum or 1.1%. The report rounds this as 'approximately £1,700' whereas the Fact Find's detailed calculation yields £1,718 monthly.
      - The report's statement 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' appears inconsistent with the modelled figures. The client's state pension is £11,973 per annum (£998 per month), which does not cover the stated retirement expenditure target of £1,700 per month (£20,400 per annum). The gap would be £9,427 per annum or £786 per month, which must come from pension drawdown or other sources, not from state pension alone.
      - The report incorporates Kim's employment income of £1,100 per month in the current cashflow but the retirement income objective of £1,700 net per month appears to be stated as a household target. The basis on which Kim's income ceases or continues into retirement is not clearly specified, affecting whether the £1,700 target is achieved from pension drawdown alone or includes continued employment income.
    The target retirement income of £1,700 net per month is consistently stated across the report and evidence (Report pages 3, Policy Research, Fact Find Retirement Planning section). The numerical figure is corroborated. However, there are material inconsistencies in how this target relates to the modelled cashflow and income sources. The report states 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement,' but state pension income of £11,973 per annum (£998 per month) is substantially below the £1,700 monthly target. This statement is misleading or incomplete—it does not account for the £702 monthly shortfall that must be met from pension drawdown. The Fact Find's detailed expenditure analysis calculates £1,718 per month, which the report summarises as 'approximately £1,700,' representing internal consistency within reasonable rounding. The critical issue is whether the £1,700 target incorporates Kim's ongoing employment income (£1,100 per month), which would make the pension requirement only £600 per month, or whether it represents the household target independent of Kim's continued work. The report's treatment of this is ambiguous. The cashflow projection in the report appears to model this scenario, but the statement that 'state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' does not align with the mathematics: £11,973 state pension plus £1,100 Kim employment = £12,073 annually or £1,006 per month, leaving a gap of £694 per month against the £1,700 target. The investment horizon stated (10 years, from page 4) contradicts the stated retirement horizon of 2 years. This 10-year figure is described as the investment horizon 'over a 10-year period' for growth potential, but retirement is planned in 2 years, creating a timing inconsistency between when the client needs access to funds and the growth timeline used to justify the risk profile. The report does not explicitly reconcile these two horizons.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.8, G3.9, G3.10, G3.11). The report asserts capacity for loss is 'Medium' but no CFL rating scale is defined or documented in the evidence. The ATR scale is clearly '1-5 Balanced/Cautious scale' [P19], but no equivalent scale for CFL ratings (e.g. Low/Medium/High or 1-5) is evident in the risk profile or planning documents. The report claims CFL is 'separately assessed' but the Risk Profile document [P20] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal', whereas detailed notes appear under other sections. This suggests no distinct CFL assessment was conducted or documented. The suitability report [P11] bases the CFL assessment on three factors (income comfortably meets expenditure, £6,000 savings, no liabilities), but the risk profile document [P1] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' under the 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section, indicating no formal CFL questionnaire assessment was completed or documented. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Capacity for loss rating is 'Medium'. The assessment basis states: (1) 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month'; (2) 'You hold savings of around £6,000, and have no outstanding liabilities'; (3) 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' The report asserts that CFL is separately assessed from ATR (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr = true).
    File:     The risk profile document [P19] shows Natural risk level from ATR questionnaire as '4 Cautious Balanced'. [P11] records the agreed risk level was upgraded to 5 'Balanced investor' with the note 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'. The suitability report [P11] states income comprises: 'a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week', John's work as 'HGV driver earning around £300 per week net', 'additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets', and 'Kim contributes a further £1,100 per month from her employment'. Monthly household expenditure is stated in the suitability report [P11] as approximately £1,700. Liquid assets from planning documents [P3] show 'Savings' of £6,000. The risk profile document [P20] records under 'Capacity for loss for the goal' heading: 'No additional notes were recorded'. No separate capacity for loss questionnaire responses or assessment rationale are documented in [P1] or [P20].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts capacity for loss is 'Medium' but no CFL rating scale is defined or documented in the evidence. The ATR scale is clearly '1-5 Balanced/Cautious scale' [P19], but no equivalent scale for CFL ratings (e.g. Low/Medium/High or 1-5) is evident in the risk profile or planning documents.
      - The report claims CFL is 'separately assessed' but the Risk Profile document [P20] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal', whereas detailed notes appear under other sections. This suggests no distinct CFL assessment was conducted or documented.
      - The suitability report [P11] bases the CFL assessment on three factors (income comfortably meets expenditure, £6,000 savings, no liabilities), but the risk profile document [P1] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' under the 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section, indicating no formal CFL questionnaire assessment was completed or documented.
      - The report upgrades ATR from 4 to 5 'Balanced' [P11], and then asserts Medium CFL. However, no explicit reasoning is given for why Medium CFL is appropriate given the upgrade to risk rating 5, or how CFL differs from the ATR rating in substance.
    The suitability report asserts a capacity for loss rating of 'Medium' and claims it is separately assessed from attitude to risk. The three factual elements cited to support this (income meeting expenditure at ~£1,700/month, £6,000 savings, no liabilities) are corroborated by [P11] and planning documents [P3]. The income figures are also confirmed in [P11]. However, several material gaps exist: (1) No capacity for loss rating scale is defined anywhere in the evidence. The ATR uses a clear 1-5 scale [P19], but CFL uses only a descriptive label 'Medium' with no reference scale or comparative anchors. (2) The Risk Profile document [P20] explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded' in the 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section, directly contradicting the assertion that CFL was separately assessed and documented. (3) The risk profile [P1] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss questionnaire', indicating no formal CFL questionnaire was completed, yet the suitability report treats the assessment as established. (4) The assessment appears to restate the consequence of ATR rather than provide independent CFL reasoning: the client has income surplus and no liabilities (which are baseline financial facts), but the guard requires that CFL not be merely restated as a consequence of ATR. Here, the reasoning 'income covers expenditure + savings exist + no liabilities = can absorb loss' is a financial position statement, not a distinct capacity or willingness measure. (5) No underperformance or loss scenario is modelled to quantify what level of loss the client could absorb. The stress tests in [P15] model a 30% market crash, but do not explicitly tie this to a capacity-for-loss threshold or show that Medium CFL is appropriate for this magnitude of stress. The statement 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' [P11] is a client confirmation of tolerance but does not establish the quantum of loss that constitutes 'detrimental'. The guard applies: a CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning beyond income/expenditure balance is not evidenced merely because the words appear. The assessment is incomplete.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two statements on tolerance for loss: (1) 'You confirmed that investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed' [P18]; and (2) 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' [P18]. The report further states: 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living. You are in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net, and receive an additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets' [P18]. The report does not explicitly quantify tolerance for loss in monetary or percentage terms.
    File:     [P1], [P5], [P14], and [P19] confirm an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced). [P11] shows responses to a Capacity for Loss questionnaire dated 18/09/2025, indicating the client answered questions about investment risk understanding and portfolio valuation checking. [P18] (the report itself) documents the client's income sources: State Pension £230/week, employment income £300/week net, and Sea Cadets income £20/week, plus spouse's contribution of £1,100/month. The risk profile report [P14] and [P19] state: 'The natural risk level, which is ascertained from the psychometric attitude to risk questionnaire score, is used as a starting point to discuss the actual level of risk you are willing and able to take to meet your specific investment objective.' [P14] and [P19] document that projections account for an estimated annual total charge of 1.5% in real terms. No separate quantification of tolerance for loss (in monetary or percentage terms representing a maximum acceptable loss) appears in any passage.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts tolerance for loss qualitatively (funds may go up or down, higher risk brings larger potential drops) but does not quantify it in monetary or percentage terms. No passage provides a quantified figure for maximum acceptable loss or loss tolerance threshold. The capacity for loss is inferred from income sufficiency (State Pension £230/week + employment £300/week net + £20/week + spouse £1,100/month) and the statement that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living, but no explicit tolerance for loss percentage or amount is stated or evidenced.
    The requirement asks whether tolerance for loss and need-level risk are consistent with and supported by evidence. Tolerance for loss is addressed qualitatively but not quantified. The guard states: 'Many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately; absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction.' This applies here. The report's statements that funds may rise or fall and that larger risk brings larger potential drops are generic descriptions aligned with the Balanced (risk 5) profile documented in [P1], [P5], [P14], [P19]. The claim that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living is supported by the income detail in [P18]: combined household income (State Pension £230/week, employment £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse £1,100/month) provides a stated basis for capacity for loss, though no threshold is quantified. The agreed risk rating of 5 is corroborated across multiple passages ([P1], [P5], [P14], [P19]). The investment term of 10 years is stated in [P18] and the investment amount of £106,000 appears in [P14]. No contradiction between report and evidence exists on what is asserted. The gap is that tolerance for loss was never quantified—neither as a percentage maximum loss nor as a monetary threshold—which matters where stress tests model large falls. [P23] shows a stress test with a -30% immediate market crash, but the tolerance threshold the client can endure is not explicitly stated. This is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction.

      - Report asserts investment term of 10 years, but risk profile document [P3] states investment period of 2 years
      - Report lists mixed time horizons (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) across objectives, inconsistent with a single 10-year investment term
      - Planning scenario [P5] shows retirement occurring 1-6 years from current date (2025), not 10 years forward
    The canonical model states an investment term of 10 years. However, [P3] the Risk Profile Report explicitly documents the investment period as 2 years in a summary table used to justify the agreed risk level. This is a direct contradiction on a core parameter. The planning documents [P5] show the clients are aged 69/65 and 65/61, retiring at ages 66 and 71 respectively in 2026 and 2027. From the 2025 planning date, these represent horizons of approximately 1 year and 6 years, not 10 years. The multiple timeHorizon objectives (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) do not align with a single coherent 10-year term either. The 2-year investment period stated in [P3] is the value explicitly used in the risk justification projections ('Projections take account of an estimated annual total charge of 1.5%... The details of your investment goal are as follows: Investment Period | 2 years | Investment | £106,000'). This 2-year figure is material to the risk assessment because it was used to generate the projections supporting the agreed risk level. A 10-year horizon would produce materially different risk projections. The guard notes that 'money left invested through retirement can legitimately have a longer horizon than the retirement date itself — but the report must say so.' No such explanation is present in the evidence. The report makes no statement reconciling a 10-year term with retirement dates 1-6 years away.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report recommends an investment strategy comprising two funds at risk rating 5: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50% allocation, 0.18% ongoing charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50% allocation, 0.29% ongoing charge). The strategy type is identified as a ModelPortfolio. The target risk rating for the solution is 5. The report states 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and that this ensures 'you have a diversified investment portfolio based on your retirement objectives in isolation as you requested and one which is in line with your stated attitude to investment risk.' The report asserts that the recommended funds and strategy align with the client's risk profile, stating the allocation 'provides a blend of active and passive investments' and 'ensures your investments stay aligned with your agreed level of risk'.
    File:     The evidence corroborates the risk rating of 5 and the two recommended funds. [P10] and [P22] confirm the agreed risk level is 5. [P8] lists the two recommended investments: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50.00%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50.00%), both mapped to risk level 5. [P11] from the suitability report itself shows the same funds and allocation with risk ratings of 5 for each fund. [P16] confirms 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' Individual fund factsheets [P2] and [P12] show the HSBC fund is rated 4 on the Risk and Reward Indicator (KIII rating), not 5. [P2] states 'This Fund is classified in category 4 because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically.' [P12] identifies the HSBC fund's risk profile as 3 on the HSBC OpenFunds scale: 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk.' The CT Universal MAP fund data in [P2] indicates it is category 4 on the Risk and Reward Indicator. Fund performance data in [P16] and [P17] show both funds performing in line with the combined portfolio, with no discrepancy in combined performance versus individual funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report assigns risk rating 5 to HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc; [P2] KIII classification shows category 4; [P12] HSBC OpenFunds risk scale shows 3.
      - Report assigns risk rating 5 to CT Universal MAP Balanced C; [P2] KIII classification shows category 4.
      - Report states the funds individually are risk rated 5 and 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined'; evidence shows individual fund KIII ratings are 4 and HSBC's own scale is 3, creating ambiguity about which scale the report's risk rating 5 refers to.
    The evidence establishes that the client's agreed risk level is 5 across multiple corroborating sources ([P10], [P22], [P16]). The report's allocation of 50%/50% between the two named funds is corroborated ([P8], [P11]). However, a material discrepancy exists regarding individual fund risk ratings. The suitability report asserts that each fund carries a risk rating of 5, but the fund factsheets provided show different ratings on different scales. [P2] and [P12] show the HSBC fund rated as category 4 on the KIII Risk and Reward Indicator and risk profile 3 on HSBC's own OpenFunds scale. [P2] shows CT Universal MAP as category 4 on the KIII scale. The report states in [P11] that 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined. This means that should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same.' This claim depends on each fund being genuinely rated 5, but the factsheets show they are not. The guard notes that 'Fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale' and permits a 'blend of two funds can target a rating neither fund carries individually.' The combined portfolio achieves an agreed risk rating of 5 ([P16]), and [P8] and [P16] confirm the portfolio's combined risk mapping is 5. However, the report's assertion that the funds 'individually' reflect the agreed risk rating is not supported. The funds appear to be selected for their combined behaviour rather than their individual ratings, but the report represents them as individually aligned. This is a representation about the matching of individual fund ratings to the agreed profile that is not evidenced and contradicts the factsheet data. The charge figures stated in the report (0.18% and 0.29%) are corroborated by [P11]. The 50%/50% allocation is confirmed across [P8] and [P11]. The investment strategy type as a ModelPortfolio is stated in the report with supporting explanation in [P7] and [P11]. The suitability has been established through an agreed risk level process detailed in [P22], in which natural risk level (4, Cautious Balanced) was discussed and an agreed level of 5 (Balanced) was reached. [P22] documents this process and the rationale. The guard permits overrides where justified; here, the ambiguity is not an override explanation but a label mismatch between the report's stated fund ratings and the factsheet evidence.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire resulting in a rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but this was overridden upward to 5 (Balanced). The report states: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' The justification offered is that the client: (1) wishes to increase risk rating to 5, is aware this is higher than the agreed rating, and is happy to take on this risk with their pension; (2) is not reliant on pension funds and wishes for best growth potential over a 10-year period, believing a balanced approach would achieve this goal. The client's acknowledgement is stated as: 'you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.'
    File:     The Risk profile report [P2, P6] confirms that the natural risk level from the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025 was 4 (Cautious Balanced). The report then states: 'In the discussion we had, you were shown a projection, based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon. We then discussed this projection and altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved.' It confirms the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced). However, [P1] and [P3] record that under 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections respectively, 'No additional notes were recorded'. The file notes [P5] contain brief observations about existing fund risk ratings but do not evidence the discussion that led to the override. The SIPP research report [P16] states the 'Agreed risk level' is 5 and notes that 'The agreed risk level considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal,' but contains no notes on how the override from 4 to 5 was discussed or justified with the client.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Risk profile report [P1, P3] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections, yet the suitability report [P4] asserts detailed justification for the override including client growth objectives and acknowledgement of increased risk
      - The suitability report [P4] quotes the client as confirming specific statements about non-reliance on pension funds and a 10-year growth objective, yet the Risk profile report provides no corresponding notes documenting this discussion or these confirmations
      - The override is asserted with referenced justification ('RiskUpgrade' and 'GrowthObjective' topics) in the canonical model, but the supporting documentation [P1, P3] explicitly records that no additional notes were recorded where such justification should appear
    The suitability report asserts an upward override of the questionnaire risk rating from 4 to 5 and provides justification rooted in client-specific statements about growth objectives and risk appetite. The canonical model identifies two justification topics: (1) RiskUpgrade — the client's explicit wish to increase to rating 5 with acknowledged awareness of higher risk; (2) GrowthObjective — non-reliance on pension funds and desire for best growth potential over 10 years. The file does contain evidence that a risk profile discussion occurred and that an agreed rating of 5 was reached [P2, P6, P16]. However, the critical deficiency is that the Risk profile report [P1, P3] explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded' in the sections titled 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' where one would expect to find the discussion notes, client statements, and justification for moving from 4 to 5. The SIPP research report [P16] references that the agreed risk level 'considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal' but provides no notes on the discussion or override justification. While the suitability report [P4] does contain the client statements quoted in the canonical model, these are from the report itself [category I], which cannot corroborate its own assertions. The absence of corroborating notes in the supporting file documentation [P1, P3] — which explicitly record no notes were made — means the detailed justification and client acknowledgement asserted in the report are not independently evidenced. This is a material gap: the override is asserted with specificity, but the file documentation that should support it either contains no notes or does not evidence the discussion and client confirmations. The guard states that where assessed and agreed ratings match, no justification is required; here they differ (4 vs 5), so justification is required. The justification is asserted but not independently documented in the supporting evidence.
