  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a target risk rating of 5 for the recommended investment strategy. The client's agreed risk rating is 5 (upgraded from an initial questionnaire rating of 4). The report states: 'You are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'. The capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium'. The recommended solution is an Aviva Pension Portfolio with a balanced investment strategy. Existing arrangements are rated at risk levels 9, 4, 2, 7, and 4 (Standard Life rated).
    File:     The evidence confirms an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced). P1, P2, and P3 from the SIPP research report and Risk profile report detail how the agreed risk level was reached through discussion considering 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return'. P5 from the suitability report confirms the client completed a questionnaire with an initial rating of 4, which was upgraded to 5 by agreement, and that the client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5 - Balanced investor'. The client's natural risk level from the questionnaire is stated as 4 (Cautious Balanced) in P3. The capacity for loss assessment in P5 notes the client is 'aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living'. The recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C) are both described as Balanced (risk level 5) portfolios in P1 and P6.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts existing arrangement risk ratings of 9, 4, 2, 7, and 4, but these are stated only in the report itself (P5, page 5: 'Risk Level 9', 'Risk Level 4', 'Risk Level 2', 'Risk Level 7', 'Risk Level 4 (Standard life rated)') with no corresponding evidence in the case file documents about what these existing arrangements' actual risk ratings are or what scale is being used.
      - The existing arrangement risk ratings are on an unknown scale ('scaleName': 'Unknown' for most entries), making them incomparable to the client's agreed risk rating of 5, which is on the Balanced 1-5 scale documented in the evidence.
      - The report provides no explicit mapping or explanation of how existing arrangements rated 9, 4, 2, 7 on an unknown scale relate to the client's agreed rating of 5 on the 1-5 Balanced scale.
    The requirement tests whether the recommended strategy's risk rating exceeds the client's agreed risk tolerance and capacity for loss. The report asserts a recommended strategy risk rating of 5 (Balanced), matching the client's agreed risk rating of 5. This alignment can be substantiated from the evidence: P1 and P2 confirm the agreed risk level is 5 Balanced, and P5 (the report itself, but as factual record of what was discussed and agreed) confirms the client agreed to rating 5. The two recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc at 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C at 50%, per P1) are both explicitly positioned as Balanced (risk level 5) investments. The capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium' in the canonical model, and the evidence in P5 shows the client confirmed 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living', supporting a medium capacity. The client's financial position (full state pension, ongoing employment income, and household income of approximately £1,100 per month household contribution) supports this medium capacity assessment. The strategy therefore does not exceed the agreed risk rating or capacity for loss on a like-for-like comparison. However, the report lists existing arrangements with risk ratings of 9, 4, 2, 7, and 4, but provides no evidence these are on the same scale as the agreed rating of 5, nor does it explain how these are consistent with or relevant to the client's agreed profile. The extraction metadata notes ambiguity about whether these existing arrangement ratings are on the same scale. Since the report itself states these ratings but the supporting evidence provides no corroborating documentation of what these existing arrangements' actual risk profiles are or what rating scale applies to them, the claim about existing arrangements' risk alignment remains unsupported. The guard allows that silence about a retained plan's risk level is a gap; a claim of alignment that the plan's own rating contradicts is a finding. Here the report asserts existing arrangements have specific risk levels but provides no evidence the case file contains documentation of those arrangements' actual risk ratings on any stated scale. This is a gap in support, not a contradiction of the recommended strategy itself. The recommended strategy (5 Balanced) remains aligned with the agreed profile (5 Balanced) and capacity for loss (Medium).

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an agreed risk rating of 5 on the Dynamic Planner 1-10 scale, labeled 'Balanced'. The questionnaire initially assessed the client at rating 4, but the report states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' The label and description for rating 5 are consistently stated as 'Balanced' with the description: 'As a Balanced investor, you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk.'
    File:     The Risk profile report [P1] and Risk profile report [P12] both confirm an agreed risk level of 5, labeled 'Balanced' with identical description text. The SIPP research report [P2] and [P6] confirm agreed risk level 5 'Balanced' with the same description. The Pension switch report [P9] states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5'. File notes [P10] record 'Aviva current risk – 2' and 'Zurich current risk – 7', indicating existing arrangements at different risk levels. The questionnaire responses in [P15] show the initial assessment led to a Cautious Balanced profile (rating 4), with responses indicating caution-oriented investment approach. The KIIDs [P17] show funds rated at category 4 on a 1-7 scale, which is a different scale and not directly comparable to the 1-10 scale used for the agreed profile.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts agreed risk rating 5 on 1-10 scale; evidence confirms this consistently across multiple documents
      - Report asserts initial questionnaire rating 4; evidence confirms initial assessment at 4 from questionnaire responses [P15]
      - Report asserts override from 4 to 5 with client agreement; evidence supports this through statements in risk profile documents
      - Report uses 'Balanced' label for rating 5; evidence consistently uses 'Balanced' label in [P1], [P2], [P6], [P12]
      - File notes [P10] show existing arrangements at risk ratings 2 (Aviva), 7 (Zurich), and 4 (Standard Life), which differ from the agreed rating 5, but this reflects existing arrangements not conflicts in the agreed profile itself
      - KIIDs [P17] reference funds rated category 4 on a 1-7 scale; this is a different scale system (1-7 KIID categories vs 1-10 Dynamic Planner scale) and not a conflict per se
    The agreed risk rating of 5 on the Dynamic Planner 1-10 scale is consistently described as 'Balanced' across all supporting documents. The questionnaire initially assessed the client at 4, which represented a Cautious Balanced profile, and the agreed upgrade to 5 (to Balanced) is explained and supported by client acknowledgement in the report. The override from questionnaire rating 4 to agreed rating 5 is an expected feature of the suitability process where the initial assessment is refined through discussion, and this is explicitly documented and evidenced. The descriptions associated with rating 5 are consistent: all state the same definition of 'Balanced investor' without contradiction. Existing arrangements show varying risk levels (2, 4, 7) but these relate to current holdings, not to conflicting definitions of the agreed profile itself. The KIIDs reference funds on a 1-7 KIID risk/reward indicator scale, which is a different standardised scale and does not represent a conflict with the 1-10 Dynamic Planner scale used for the client's agreed profile—the guard explicitly permits comparison across different scales where descriptions align. No conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels are present for the agreed rating 5 profile itself.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts an agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced) on the Dynamic Planner 1-10 scale. The client's natural questionnaire score was 4, upgraded to 5 by agreement. Capacity for loss is rated as Medium, based on surplus income (£1,700/month household expenditure comfortably met), liquid savings of £6,000, no liabilities, and confirmation that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living. Investment term is 10 years. The recommended strategy comprises two funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). The portfolio is described as aligned with the client's risk profile both individually and combined, with the stated rationale that it provides diversification, a blend of active/passive management, simplicity, competitive charges, strong peer performance, and governance oversight. The strategy targets a risk rating of 5 on a Balanced scale with a benchmark of IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares TR in GB.
    File:     The evidence supports the agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced) and the Medium capacity for loss assessment. P15 [D] confirms the natural risk level questionnaire completion on 18/09/2025 with a 4 rating, and the agreed upgrade to 5 through discussion. P6, P12 [G] and P15 [D] all corroborate the risk rating 5 and Balanced profile description. P4, P10, P12 [G] confirm both recommended funds and their 50/50 allocation. P4 [G] shows HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C with performance data (3-year: 32.52%, 5-year: 40.06%, 10-year: 122.95%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C with comparable performance (discrete 2021-2024 showing 12.02% combined, -9.83%, 10.68%, 11.05%). P11 [G] confirms ongoing costs of 0.18% for HSBC and 0.29% for CT Universal MAP. P9 [H] (KIID for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio) states the fund is rated as 3 on the fund's own Risk and Reward Indicator (where 1 is lower risk, 5 is higher risk), and notes a recommended minimum holding period of 5 years or more. The fund aim is to provide 'growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of 5 years or more.' P1 [H] (another KIID) shows the fund rated 4 on its Risk and Reward Indicator, with a note that 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' The capacity for loss basis is documented in the report itself (P16 [I]) citing surplus income, savings, and lack of liabilities, consistent with the report's narrative sections.
    Discrepancies:
      - Fund risk ratings: The report states both recommended funds carry a risk rating of 5, but the HSBC fund KIID (P9 [H]) identifies the fund as rated 3 on its Risk and Reward Indicator, not 5. The CT Universal MAP fund's individual KIID was not located in the evidence pack.
      - Fund minimum holding period: P9 [H] (HSBC KIID) states 'The Fund aims to provide growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of 5 years or more' and P1 [H] notes 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' The client's investment term is 10 years, which exceeds the minimum, so this is not a mismatch in outcome but the funds' documentation shows a 5-year-plus holding period, not a 10-year design horizon.
      - Scale comparison: The report's agreed risk rating of 5 is on the Dynamic Planner 1-10 scale (Balanced label). The HSBC fund's KIID risk rating of 3 is on the fund's own Risk and Reward Indicator (1-7 scale). The guard notes these scales do not map directly and should be used for corroboration, not value matching.
    The portfolio alignment can be assessed on the available evidence. The client's agreed risk rating is corroborated: the report states 5 (Balanced), the risk profile report (P15 [D]) confirms the questionnaire natural level was 4 and agrees to 5, and the SIPP research report (P6, P12 [G]) validates the agreed level as 5 Balanced. The capacity for loss (Medium) is supported by the stated factors: surplus income, liquid savings, and stated resilience to underperformance. The 10-year investment term is documented. The recommended funds and allocations (50% HSBC, 50% CT Universal MAP) are evidenced in P10, P12 [G] and P4 [G]. The report claims both funds have a risk rating of 5 and are aligned with the agreed profile. However, a material discrepancy arises on the fund risk ratings: the HSBC KIID (P9 [H]) identifies the fund as rated 3 on its Risk and Reward Indicator (1-7 scale), not 5. The report states the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and that they 'are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk.' The guard for this check acknowledges that a fund's SRRI (Synthetic Risk and Reward Indicator) or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's, and should be used as corroboration, not value matching. Applied here, the HSBC fund's internal rating of 3 (on its 1-7 scale) cannot be directly compared to the client's agreed rating of 5 (on a 1-10 Dynamic Planner scale). Nonetheless, the HSBC fund's KIID describes it as 'Balanced' and designed for investors with 'a degree of understanding of financial matters' seeking 'a combination of investments with differing levels of risk,' which aligns in descriptor with the client's Balanced profile. The fund's 3-year volatility of 7.12% (P8 [H]) sits within a peer group ranging from 4.14% (lowest) to 10.87% (highest), positioning it in the moderate-risk band. No evidence was found that shows the CT Universal MAP fund's risk rating or indicator, so full corroboration of both funds cannot be completed. The investment term of 10 years exceeds the HSBC fund's documented minimum holding period of 5 years, so no mismatch on term arises. The report's claim that the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' is partially supported: the descriptor (Balanced) aligns, the term is adequate, but the fund-level risk rating evidence for HSBC shows internal rating 3 (not 5), and evidence for CT Universal MAP is absent. The report does not explain or reconcile the difference between the agreed client risk rating (5 on 1-10) and the fund's internal rating (3 on 1-7), nor does it address why funds designed as Balanced (typically moderate-risk) are presented as rated 5 to meet a client's agreed risk rating of 5. This creates ambiguity on whether the portfolio's risk exposure is truly aligned with the client's agreed profile or whether the report conflates different rating scales without explanation. Given the guard that allows fund indicators to be used for corroboration rather than direct value matching, and given that the descriptor Balanced aligns, a potential concern arises from the unexplained discrepancy in ratings and the missing evidence for one of the two recommended funds.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Questionnaire risk rating was 4, upgraded to agreed rating of 5 on the Dynamic Planner 1-10 scale (range 1-10, label 'Balanced'). (2) Capacity for loss is 'Medium', based on surplus income (£1,700/month expenditure met), liquid savings (£6,000), and no liabilities. (3) Client confirmed underperformance would not affect day-to-day living. (4) Investment term is 10 years. (5) Recommended strategy: two risk-rated balanced funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%), each rated risk 5, total allocation 100%. (6) Strategy rationale: funds reflect risk profile, provide diversification and blend of active/passive management, charges are competitive, funds have performed well, part of CIP with quarterly oversight. (7) Override: questionnaire rating 4 increased to 5; justified by client growth objective over 10-year term, non-reliance on pension funds, desire for best growth potential, and client acknowledgement that this is higher than questionnaire rating and they are happy to take it.
    File:     Evidence corroborates: (1) Questionnaire completed 18/09/2025, natural risk level scored 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); agreed risk level following discussion was 5 ('Balanced') per [P1] and [P8]. Scale is Dynamic Planner 1-10. Description of rating 5 in [P1] and [P8] matches report assertion. (2) Capacity for loss: [P1] references capacity for loss questionnaire with no additional notes recorded post-discussion [P4]. [P17] (suitability report itself) states client confirmed underperformance would not affect day-to-day living, holds savings of £6,000, has no liabilities, receives State Pension and employment income. [P8] uses investment figure of £106,000 for projections and notes 'estimated annual total charge of 1.5%'. (3) Investment term: [P8] shows investment period of '2 years' in projections table, contradicting the 10-year term asserted in the report. (4) Recommended funds: [P5] and [P2] confirm CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), each rated risk 5. (5) Fund ratings [P9]: HSBC cumulative performance data provided; CT Universal MAP performance data provided. (6) Alternative fund analysis [P7] notes: Aviva current risk 2, Zurich current risk 7, Standard Life current risk 4. [P10] explains why alternatives were rejected: Aviva only has risk 4 fund (does not meet ATR of 5), Zurich lacks risk 5 multi-asset funds (blend used), Standard Life funds more expensive. (7) Cost data [P12] shows Aviva custom charge structure but no specific ongoing charges stated for new recommended funds in cost sections. Charge figures in report (0.18% HSBC, 0.29% CT MAP) stated in [P18] but full justification for 'competitive' not evidenced in detail.
    Discrepancies:
      - Investment term: Report asserts 10-year investment period in override justification ('wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period'); [P8] Risk profile report shows Investment Period of '2 years' in the projections table used for capacity for loss assessment.
      - Capacity for loss assessment basis: Report states medium capacity for loss is 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' but [P4] notes 'No additional notes were recorded' for capacity for loss questionnaire discussion, leaving the reasoning for medium rating unexplained in supporting documentation.
      - Risk profile description label inconsistency: Report text uses 'Balanced' as label for rating 5, but questionnaire response in [P16] shows client answered 'Cautious' to investment approach question, with responses indicating cautious positioning (e.g. 'More cautious' compared to others, 'Slight risk of capital loss' acceptable). The gap between questionnaire responses (cautious tone) and final rating 5 (Balanced/non-cautious) is not bridged by explicit discussion notes.
    The comparison can be performed. Corroboration on most elements is present: questionnaire rating 4 upgraded to rating 5 on the Dynamic Planner 1-10 scale is confirmed across [P1], [P8], [P17]. The override is documented with client acknowledgement. Both recommended funds are confirmed as risk 5 and are evidenced in [P5] and [P2]. The capacity for loss basis (surplus income, savings, no liabilities) is asserted in the report and corroborated by [P17] which repeats it verbatim. However, three material discrepancies emerge: First, investment term contradiction: The override justification in the suitability report rests on a '10-year period' growth objective, but [P8] shows the risk profile report used a '2 years' investment period when calculating projections for capacity for loss assessment. This is material because investment term is a primary determinant of capacity for loss and risk suitability. The 2-year term contradicts the 10-year term used to justify the risk 5 upgrade. Second, capacity for loss reasoning gap: The report asserts capacity for loss is 'Medium', 'consistent with' the agreed risk 5 rating. But [P4] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the capacity for loss questionnaire discussion section, and [P1] does not detail how medium capacity for loss was derived from the three stated factors (income, savings, liabilities). The assertion that medium CFL is 'consistent with' risk 5 is stated but not explained — the mechanics of that consistency are absent. Third, risk profile override bridging: The questionnaire responses in [P16] show a cautious tone ('Cautious' self-description, 'More cautious' vs peers, anxiety threshold). The jump from questionnaire rating 4 (Cautious Balanced label in [P8]) to agreed rating 5 (Balanced, non-cautious label) is justified by client's growth objective and non-reliance on funds. However, [P3] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the agreed risk level discussion. The override documentation in the report ([P17]) includes client acknowledgement, but no discussion notes in [P1]/[P3] explain how the adviser and client moved from questionnaire result to the higher rating, or why the questionnaire responses (cautious) were judged not to 'fully reflect' risk appetite. The report asserts the override was evidenced; [P3] shows no substantive notes. Standard boilerplate risk warnings in the report are not unsupported statements about this client — they are generic. The guard permits gap-finding where support is uncontroversial and consistent; here, the 10-year vs 2-year term contradiction and the absence of reasoning for medium CFL being consistent with risk 5 are neither uncontroversial nor consistent.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report retains one arrangement (People's Pension, Arrangement 1) which holds 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' rated at risk level 9. The agreed client risk rating is 5. The report states: 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement' and 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' No alignment claim or override justification is made for the fund held within this retained arrangement.
    File:     [P4] confirms an agreed risk level of 5 based on the risk profile assessment. [P9] and [P10] corroborate this agreed risk level as 5 for the investment recommendation. [P1] and [P9] evidence risk level 9 exists as a category. The retained arrangement (People's Pension) is not addressed in any supporting document regarding fund suitability or risk alignment. No document evidences why a risk level 9 fund would be appropriate for a client with an agreed risk level of 5, nor does any document provide a rationale, override justification or client acknowledgement for retaining an out-of-profile fund.
    Discrepancies:
      - The retained arrangement holds a fund rated at risk level 9, which exceeds the agreed client risk rating of 5 by 4 levels.
      - The report provides a rationale for retention (ongoing employer contributions) but makes no statement about the alignment, suitability or risk profile of the fund held within the retained arrangement.
      - The rationale given for retention addresses the arrangement itself, not the investment risk profile of its contents.
    The report asserts that the People's Pension arrangement will be retained, citing employer contributions as the rationale. However, this rationale addresses why the arrangement should not be transferred away, not whether the fund inside it aligns with the agreed risk profile. The fund is stated as 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' with a risk level of 9. The client's agreed risk rating is confirmed as 5 across multiple independent sources ([P4], [P9], [P10]). A retained arrangement carries the same alignment duty as a recommended one; retention does not override the requirement that funds held must match the agreed risk profile. The report does not claim the fund aligns with the profile, does not provide any override justification, and does not acknowledge any mismatch. The guard notes that a rationale for retaining a plan (such as employer contribution) explains retention but does not answer for the fund inside it. Where the report gives one and says nothing about the fund's risk level, that is the finding. Here, the fund rating (9) materially exceeds the agreed rating (5), and no justification, explanation or client acknowledgement is present to support this gap. The comparison can be performed: both the retained fund's risk level and the agreed client risk level are clearly stated on the same scale.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report characterises client as having 'Moderate' knowledge suitable for 'Balanced' investor approach; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' when directly asked about investing experience and understanding. Report states client has 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'; questionnaire shows no investment experience outside workplace pension in past 5 years and 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks, shares, unit trusts, and pension planning. Report references client understanding of risk and returns; questionnaire simultaneously records 'No understanding / knowledge' of how these products work and their associated risks. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client has 'Moderate' knowledge and experience. The report states: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' It further notes the client 'understand[s] that you may need to take some risk to meet your investment goals' and references experience 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' but states the client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed.'
    File:     The Risk Profile questionnaire (P1, P10) completed on 18/09/2025 records the client's responses to direct questions about investment knowledge and experience: 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' Response: 'No understanding / knowledge'. For stocks and shares: 'No understanding / knowledge'. For Unit Trusts: 'No understanding / knowledge'. For pension planning: 'No understanding / knowledge'. For Current Accounts/Savings: 'Very little understanding / knowledge'. The questionnaire records no investment experience in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, or pension products within the last 5 years. The fact find (P7) shows Cash savings of £6,000 as the only non-pension holding. File notes (P3) record 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss sections.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report characterises client as having 'Moderate' knowledge suitable for 'Balanced' investor approach; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' when directly asked about investing experience and understanding
      - Report states client has 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'; questionnaire shows no investment experience outside workplace pension in past 5 years and 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks, shares, unit trusts, and pension planning
      - Report references client understanding of risk and returns; questionnaire simultaneously records 'No understanding / knowledge' of how these products work and their associated risks
      - Questionnaire records limited knowledge only for basic savings products ('Very little understanding / knowledge' of Current Accounts/Savings); report elevates this to 'Moderate' level suitable for complex balanced portfolios
    The questionnaire responses are direct, structured assessments administered on 18/09/2025, the same date as the adviser meeting. They represent formal answers to explicit questions about the client's self-assessed knowledge and experience, not casual remarks. The questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' as discrete, formally captured responses to: (1) overall investing experience and understanding, (2) stocks and shares understanding, (3) Unit Trusts understanding, and (4) pension planning understanding. The report's reference to the client having 'some experience' and 'a degree of understanding of financial matters' is unsupported by these assessments. The fact find (P7, P8) evidences only basic cash savings (£6,000) and no securities or investment experience beyond existing pension arrangements. The adviser's file notes (P3) record 'No additional notes were recorded' under both Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss discussion sections, indicating no offsetting discussion or explanation was documented that might reconcile the questionnaire's stark assessment with the report's claim of moderate knowledge. The report does acknowledge the client's experience is 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' (P15), but then positions the client as suitable for a Risk 5 Balanced portfolio requiring active investment decision-making and understanding of mixed asset funds without evidencing how this limited, formally-assessed 'no understanding' baseline supports that suitability. The report's language mirrors the description text for a Balanced investor (P2, P12), which assumes 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' — a baseline the questionnaire explicitly contradicts. The discrepancy is material: the questionnaire is the formal knowledge assessment; the report's characterisation of 'Moderate' knowledge is not corroborated by it and is directly contradicted by multiple 'No understanding / knowledge' entries on the same questionnaire.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client has: (1) previously taken out cash savings products [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice, page 4: 'You confirmed that you have previously taken out cash savings products']; (2) NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by themselves [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice, page 4: 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself']; (3) held group pensions for some time [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice, page 4: 'You have however held your group pensions for some time'].
    File:     The Risk Profile questionnaire [P1, D] records the client's investor experience as: (1) Cash savings: 'Yes, by myself' to the question 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?'; (2) Understanding of stocks and shares: 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work...?'; (3) Stocks & shares investment in last 5 years: 'No' to 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?'; (4) Pension planning understanding: 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works...?'; (5) Pension investment in last 5 years: 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?'. Existing arrangements [P18, B] show: People's Pension (£6,601.55), Scottish Widows (£211.32), Aviva (£3,744.36), Zurich (£103,439.24), Standard Life (£3,002.00) — five pension schemes held. The fact find [P15, P17, B] records current employment as HGV Driver and cash savings of £6,000. Risk assessment [P14, D] shows natural risk level was 4 (Cautious Balanced) upgraded to 5 (Balanced) by agreement; notes record 'No additional notes were recorded' on investor experience [P7, D].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states client has 'held group pensions for some time' but questionnaire [P1] records 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' and states 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning. This is internally inconsistent: holding pensions suggests recent experience, yet questionnaire denies both recent investment and any understanding.
      - Report's characterization of group pension holdings as evidence of knowledge/experience is undermined by questionnaire responses showing zero understanding of 'pension planning and income in retirement'.
      - Existing arrangements show five pension schemes (total £116,998.47) held by the client, but the Risk Profile questionnaire at [P1] shows response 'No' to pension investment in last 5 years and 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning, creating tension between asset holdings and stated knowledge level.
    The report asserts the client has held group pensions for some time as evidence of experience. However, the Risk Profile questionnaire [P1, D] — a direct investor-experience assessment — records the client's pension planning knowledge as 'No understanding / knowledge' and response of 'No' to pension investment in the last 5 years. The guard states 'holding a workplace pension is passive experience; a report distinguishing it from self-directed investing is being accurate, not inconsistent' and 'plans held do not prove understanding — do not treat plan ownership as evidence of knowledge.' The report does not rely on plan ownership alone; it explicitly cites 'held your group pensions for some time' as lived experience ('You have however held...'). Yet the questionnaire denies both recent investment activity (No to past 5 years) and understanding (explicitly 'No understanding / knowledge'). This is not the guard; it is a material contradiction. A client can passively hold workplace pensions while having no understanding of them. The questionnaire response 'No understanding / knowledge' to pension planning is the material finding on knowledge for a pension recommendation. The report's narrative about group pension holdings does not resolve the questionnaire finding that the client records zero understanding of pension planning. The existing arrangements confirm holdings, but the Risk Profile [P1] at investor-experience questions records them as not meeting the 'yes by myself' threshold and denies understanding. The report's statement about 'held your group pensions for some time' is an assertion about experience that the evidence does not support in the form the report presents it. On cash savings, the report and questionnaire align: both confirm prior experience. On stocks & shares, both confirm no self-directed investment. The tension is entirely on pensions: the report treats holdings as evidence of experience and understanding, but the questionnaire (the direct evidence of knowledge) records no understanding and no recent investment activity. This is a material inconsistency between the report's narrative characterization and the investor-experience questionnaire.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended product is 'Aviva Personal Pension with Flexi Access Drawdown' and characterizes it as 'Complex' with seven complexity drivers: (1) Multiple fund choice (6,900+ funds available); (2) Flexible drawdown options requiring tax planning decisions; (3) Pension freedoms and income withdrawal choices; (4) Death benefit nomination and beneficiary designation; (5) Lifetime Allowance and Annual Allowance considerations; (6) Investment platform requiring active management; (7) Tax implications of withdrawals at different ages (before and after age 75). The extraction note states complexity was 'inferred from product features described' and that these contrast 'with client's limited investment experience.' The report states [P4, I] 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension' and recommends the product as 'the most suitable solution given your circumstances and retirement objectives' [P17, I].
    File:     The case file contains extensive product literature describing Flexi Access Drawdown features [P1, H]: multiple withdrawal options (lump sum, drawdown, annuity combinations), single drawdown, Self-Select phased income drawdown with tax-planning tailoring, capped drawdown alternatives, and rules on withdrawals before and after age 75. Terms and conditions [P7, P11, P15, H] detail adviser charges, fund manager charges, taxation treatment, and restrictions on complex instruments. Key Features [P20, P22, H] describe income drawdown risks, currency risk, property investment timing, and tax position variability. KIIDs [P21, P23, P24, H] document fund-level charges and risks including derivative risk, credit risk, emerging markets risk, and liquidity risk. Fund factsheets [P8, P9, H] show specific funds with ongoing charges (0.29%) and risk profiles. A Policy Research table [P6, P12, H] confirms Flexi Access Drawdown is available ('Yes') and Full and partial UFPLS available ('Yes'). However, the file contains NO explicit record of the client's stated knowledge level, investment experience, previous holdings, or pension experience prior to or supporting the characterization 'limited investment experience' referenced in the extraction note.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report characterizes the client as having 'limited investment experience' in the extraction note, but no fact-find data, knowledge assessment, experience record, or prior holdings documentation appears in the case file to corroborate or specify what that limited experience comprises.
      - The report asserts the product is suitable given the client's 'circumstances and retirement objectives' but the case file provides extensive product complexity documentation (Key Features, KIIDs, Terms and Conditions) without corresponding documentation of the client's stated knowledge level, demonstrated understanding, or assessment of comprehension of these complex features.
      - The report lists seven specific complexity drivers but provides no evidence in the file that the client was questioned about, explained, or demonstrated comprehension of features such as Self-Select phased income drawdown tax planning, Annual Allowance (£60,000 limit) and carry-forward provisions, Lifetime Allowance replacement (Lump Sum Allowance £268,275; Lump Sum and Death Benefit Allowance £1,073,100), or tax treatment variance at age 75 (tax-free death benefits before 75; taxable at beneficiary rate after 75).
      - The report states 'You understand the pension freedoms' [P4, I] but the case file contains no client statement, questionnaire response, adviser notes, or record of explanation confirming the client's actual understanding of the specific freedoms available (single drawdown, phased drawdown, annuity purchase, combined options).
    The report recommends a complex product (Flexi Access Drawdown personal pension) and asserts in the extraction metadata that this is suitable despite the client's 'limited investment experience.' However, the supporting evidence is one-sided. The file contains comprehensive product literature describing the complexity (multiple fund choices, tax planning requirements, withdrawal options with differing tax treatment before and after 75, Annual and Lifetime Allowance rules, beneficiary designation, platform management). It also contains a single statement attributed to the client: 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' [P4, I]. No other client-side evidence appears: no knowledge questionnaire, no fact-find recording investment experience, no existing holdings list, no record of adviser questions testing understanding, no written confirmation of comprehension. The extraction note itself identifies the tension ('limited investment experience' versus complex product recommendation) but the canonical model provides no supporting documentation from the fact-find, knowledge assessment, or adviser records. The comparison can be performed because the product complexity is documented in detail; what is missing is the client knowledge side. The file demonstrates that Flexi Access Drawdown is a complex product with multiple withdrawal mechanics, tax-timing decisions, allowance monitoring requirements, and investment platform management responsibilities. Against this, the sole evidence of the client's readiness is the single assertion 'You understand the pension freedoms.' No grading of knowledge level, no record of previous pension or investment experience, no documented explanation of the specific complex features, and no sign-off confirming comprehension appear in the case file. The recommendation may be sound, but the suitability rationale is incomplete: it lacks the client knowledge documentation necessary to demonstrate that complexity was proportionate to demonstrated understanding.

  Potential Concern — severity High

      - The report states 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' (P5), but recommends two actively managed multi-asset funds requiring understanding of diversified asset allocation, active management, rebalancing, and model portfolio mechanics (P4, P11).

The guard states that where a risk-profile description carries preconditions the file contradicts, the description is being used as evidence of knowledge it does not establish. Here, the report has applied a description designed for investors with foundational investment knowledge to a client the questionnaire explicitly records as having no understanding of stocks and shares or pension planning. The discrepancy is not explained by any guard; it reflects a material misstatement of the client's knowledge level relative to the documented evidence.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report states 'combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month' but Fact Find shows income of £1,430.00 and expenditure of £1,718.00, creating a monthly deficit of £288.00, not a comfortable surplus. The expenditure figure cited (£1,700) is close to but distinct from the recorded expenditure of £1,718.00. Report asserts affordability of £1,101.85 annual ongoing commitment (1% adviser fee), but current monthly cashflow shows a deficit of £288, meaning £3,456 annual shortfall before any investment contribution or adviser fee is factored in. The initial commitment of £3,305.55 is described as paid 'directly by the pension schemes on transfer', not from client income, which is a one-off transfer event. However, the ongoing £1,101.85 annual commitment would need to be paid from available funds or income surplus, and the Fact Find shows no surplus to support it. 24 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial commitment: £3,305.55 (3% adviser fee on amount invested). Ongoing commitment: £1,101.85 per annum (1% per annum on current values and expected contributions, minus initial fee). The report asserts affordability is supported by: combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; savings of around £6,000 held; no outstanding liabilities; medium capacity for loss consistent with Risk Rating 5; potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living.
    File:     Fact Find shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Total Monthly Disposable Income £(288.00) — a deficit of £288 per month. The Fact Find records no expected changes to expenditure in foreseeable future. Savings are confirmed at £6,000. No liabilities are recorded. Planning projections in both baseline and proposed scenarios show liquid assets of approximately £122,000-£123,000 in near term (baseline) or £159,000-£180,000 (proposed scenario) by later retirement years, with annual surpluses maintained through the projection period after retirement income begins.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 'combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month' but Fact Find shows income of £1,430.00 and expenditure of £1,718.00, creating a monthly deficit of £288.00, not a comfortable surplus. The expenditure figure cited (£1,700) is close to but distinct from the recorded expenditure of £1,718.00.
      - Report asserts affordability of £1,101.85 annual ongoing commitment (1% adviser fee), but current monthly cashflow shows a deficit of £288, meaning £3,456 annual shortfall before any investment contribution or adviser fee is factored in.
      - The initial commitment of £3,305.55 is described as paid 'directly by the pension schemes on transfer', not from client income, which is a one-off transfer event. However, the ongoing £1,101.85 annual commitment would need to be paid from available funds or income surplus, and the Fact Find shows no surplus to support it.
    The guards specify that (1) fees taken from the fund reduce the investment, not the client's income — do not test them against monthly surplus, and (2) a one-off transfer of existing pension money is not a new affordability commitment. Applying these guards: The initial fee of £3,305.55 is a one-off deduction from transfer value and should not be tested against monthly income/expenditure. The ongoing adviser fee of £1,101.85 per annum, however, represents a recurring commitment that must be affordable from available cashflow. The Fact Find establishes a baseline monthly deficit of £288 (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718). The report's assertion that 'combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure' contradicts the Fact Find data which shows expenditure exceeding income by £288 monthly. The report cites 'approximately £1,700' as expenditure, which is internally inconsistent with its own affordability claim, since even at £1,700 the income of £1,430 would not 'comfortably' meet it. The planning scenarios show substantial liquid assets and positive annual surpluses in retirement, which would support the ongoing fee, but these are projections contingent on investment performance and future pension drawdowns, not current affordability from employment income. The recommendation does not quantify how the current monthly deficit is to be resolved, nor does it evidence that the £1,101.85 annual ongoing charge will be affordable from the stated income position. The report's basis for affordability relies on capacity for loss (a medium capacity, appropriate to Risk Rating 5) and assertion that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living, but this does not address the arithmetic contradiction between stated income and expenditure. The initial fee is protected by the guard; the ongoing fee creates an unresolved affordability question given the baseline monthly deficit in the Fact Find.

    Discrepancies:
      - Report states monthly income £4,486.67; Fact Find states £1,430.00 - difference of £3,056.67 (214% higher in report)
      - Report derives monthly income from four sources (John HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + State Pension £1,000 + Kim £1,100); Fact Find shows Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00 which does not reconcile to reported components
      - Report states monthly expenditure £1,700; Fact Find states £1,718.00 - difference of £18 (1% lower in report)
      - Report calculates monthly surplus £2,786.67 (4,486.67 - 1,700); Fact Find shows monthly disposable income (£288.00) deficit (1,430.00 - 1,718.00)
      - Report income derivation includes State Pension £1,000/month (£12,000/year); Fact Find shows State Pension £230/month (£2,760/year) from 22/09/2025
      - Report income includes Kim employment £1,100/month; Fact Find shows Kim 'Basic Income - Employed CDH Recruitment' £1,200 monthly but also shows State Pension entry £230 monthly for Kim from 22/09/2025, with total income £1,430
      - Intelliflo scenarios show first-year combined income £41,813 (State Pension £11,973 + Employment £16,640 + Kim employment £13,200); this equals approximately £3,484/month, not £4,486.67 reported
    Critical mismatch between report's income and expenditure figures and supporting evidence. The report states net monthly household income of £4,486.67 by summing: John HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + State Pension £1,000 + Kim £1,100. However, the Fact Find (P2) documents Total Net Monthly Income as only £1,430.00, and itemises this as: John employment (from tables not fully shown but context suggests around £1,200) and Kim employment £1,200, with additional State Pension line showing £230 monthly. Intelliflo planning documents (P14, P16) confirm first-year household income of £41,813 annually, which is £3,484.50 monthly - substantially below the £4,486.67 reported. The report's derivation adds State Pension £1,000/month (£12,000/year), but Fact Find and Intelliflo both show State Pension as £11,973 annually (£998/month), and this is shown as John's only state pension currently received, with Kim's state pension deferred to age 67. The report appears to have double-counted or misallocated income components. The monthly expenditure discrepancy is minor (£1,700 vs £1,718), within rounding, and the Intelliflo scenarios corroborate £1,700 as the stated retirement expenditure target (P6). Critically, the Fact Find shows current monthly disposable income of (£288.00) - a deficit - yet the report asserts a surplus of £2,786.67. This is not reconcilable by inflation assumptions or growth rates; it is a fundamental input error. The cashflow scenarios in Intelliflo (P11, P14) show positive liquid assets throughout because they use the correct income and expenditure figures (approximately £3,484 monthly income, £2,000 annual expenditure stated as £24,001/12 = £2,000), yielding a sustainable surplus. However, these scenarios do not appear to have been generated with the income figures the report asserts. The report's claim that 'state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' is supported by the evidence (P6), but is predicated on the lower actual income figures in the Intelliflo model, not the inflated income the report states. The affordability conclusion relies on income figures that are not evidenced and contradict the documented cashflow inputs. No vulnerability indicators are noted in the evidence that would materially affect this check.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an emergency fund of £6,000 held as at 2025-10-14, stated as actual and confirmed sufficient. The report states: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' and 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' The report identifies two access needs: emergency access funded from liquid cash savings, and regular pension access via Flexi Access Drawdown (10-15 working days). No quantified emergency fund requirement is stated in the extracted model.
    File:     The Fact Find [P6] records £6,000.00 cash savings as at 22/09/2025, matching the report's emergency fund figure. Cashflow projections in the proposed scenario [P7] show liquid assets declining from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027, continuing to decline through the projection period. By 2056 [P8], liquid assets fall to zero with an in-year deficit of £2,930. The baseline scenario [P16] shows similar erosion: liquid assets fall from £5,912 in 2027 to £5,573 by 2035. Income and expenditure show an ongoing monthly deficit of -£288 [P14]: total net monthly income £1,430 against total monthly expenditure £1,718. Fact Find records total gross annual earnings of £29,840 (employment income £16,640 + £13,200) with no mention of a required emergency fund amount or minimum threshold.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the £6,000 emergency fund as 'confirmed sufficient' but provides no quantified requirement against which sufficiency can be measured.
      - Cashflow projections show liquid assets declining materially over the projection period, from £6,000 to near-zero by 2056 in the proposed scenario and below £6,000 by 2026 in both scenarios, raising questions about whether emergency reserves are being depleted by the recommendation.
      - The Fact Find shows current monthly disposable income of -£288 (expenditure exceeds income), yet the report does not explicitly address how ongoing affordability interacts with emergency fund sufficiency.
      - The report references 'instance access funds' but does not quantify how much liquid cash is required to remain available for emergencies as ongoing contributions and withdrawals proceed.
    The emergency fund of £6,000 held as at September 2025 is corroborated by the Fact Find [P6]. However, the report's assertion that this amount is 'confirmed sufficient' cannot be verified because no quantified requirement is stated. The guard 'An asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified' applies here: the report claims sufficiency but offers no basis—no target emergency fund size, no ratio to monthly expenditure, no analysis of foreseeable needs. The Fact Find shows monthly expenditure of £1,718 and monthly income of £1,430, creating an ongoing deficit of £288 per month. Over a year, this would consume approximately £3,456 in emergency reserves if covered from the £6,000 cash holding. The cashflow projections show liquid assets declining materially in both scenarios, falling below the opening £6,000 within 12 months (proposed scenario shows £5,956 by 2026; baseline similarly shows £5,912 by 2027). By 2056 in the proposed scenario, liquid assets reach zero with a deficiency of £2,930. The second guard—'Cash held may be earmarked elsewhere in the file'—requires checking whether the £6,000 is reserved for emergencies or available for other purposes. The projections [P7, P8, P16] treat liquid assets as part of the total available funds and as subject to drawdown to cover shortfalls, implying the cash is not earmarked exclusively for emergencies but forms part of the overall retirement cashflow. The report states the client 'is comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds,' but this articulates comfort rather than a suitability finding based on quantified need. The terms and conditions [P11] indicate that if there is insufficient cash to pay income, the adviser can disinvest proportionately from assets. This means the emergency fund could be reduced if needed to fund drawdown, conflicting with its protected status as emergency reserves. The report does not address this tension. No evidence explicitly quantifies what emergency needs the client anticipates or what coverage is required. The affordability analysis in the Fact Find [P14] shows a monthly deficit before any contributions or withdrawals, yet the suitability report does not explain how the client will sustain contributions to the recommended plan while maintaining the emergency fund, or how planned withdrawal income will interact with emergency liquidity needs.

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the recommendation is affordable based on: (1) an initial commitment of £3,305.55 representing a 3% advice fee on amounts invested; (2) ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 annually (1% per annum on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee); (3) combined income that 'comfortably meets' household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (4) savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (5) a derived monthly surplus of £2,786.67 (stated as 4,486.67 minus 1,700); (6) medium capacity for loss consistent with risk rating 5; and (7) confirmation that potential underperformance would not impact day-to-day living.     The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the cornerstone figure used to calculate affordability—the monthly income of £4,486.67—does not appear in any evidence provided.   [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs will be funded from two sources: (1) Liquid cash savings, and (2) Pension funds via Flexi Access Drawdown. The report does not quantify an emergency fund shortfall or assess whether emergency reserves are adequate or would be depleted by the recommendation.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Opening liquid assets of £6,000 in cash savings (Savings account, Cash Account), which the planning scenario identifies as 'LIQUIDATED 1ST' in the proposed scenario [P1]. (2) The proposed scenario projects liquid assets declining from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), £5,869 (2028), £5,825 (2029) and continuing to decline annually [P10]. (3) Surplus Account balance begins at £0 and remains £0 throughout the baseline and proposed scenarios, meaning no surplus is accumulated [P8], [P9], [P10]. (4) The Baseline scenario shows 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' of 0 in every year from 2026 onwards, with the proposed scenario similarly showing 0 surplus in every year [P4], [P12]. (5) Income sources total approximately £30,840 p/a initially (John: £15,600 + £1,040 = £16,640; Kim: £13,200), reducing to £24,181 in 2027 when Kim retires [P1], [P5]. (6) Regular expenditure is £20,616 p/a [P10]. (7) The planning document shows State Pension of £11,973 p/a (inflated by 2.5%) from age 66 for each party [P1], [P5]. (8) No dedicated emergency fund is quantified anywhere in the case file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report identifies liquidity needs as being funded from liquid cash savings and Flexi Access Drawdown, but does not quantify what constitutes an adequate emergency fund or assess whether £6,000 is sufficient for the household's needs.
      - The planning scenario shows liquid assets declining annually from £6,000 with no replenishment (Surplus Account remains £0 throughout), yet the report does not address whether this depletion pattern is acceptable or discuss what happens when liquid assets approach zero.
      - No explicit statement in the report confirms that the £6,000 emergency fund is adequate, retained, or protected from depletion; the extraction shows /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall is absent, indicating the report is silent on this assessment.
    The requirement tests whether affordability is supported by evidence that emergency funds are adequate, retained, and accessible when needed. Three elements must be present: (1) quantification of emergency fund adequacy; (2) evidence it is not depleted; (3) confirmation of access when required.
    
    On quantification: The case file evidences £6,000 in liquid savings (Cash Account) [P1], [P9]. However, the report does not define what emergency fund provision is appropriate for this household or explain why £6,000 is sufficient. Expenditure is £20,616 p/a [P10], making the £6,000 represent approximately 3.5 weeks of spending—a conventional emergency fund guideline, but one the report does not articulate or justify.
    
    On depletion: The liquidation order in the proposed scenario identifies the £6,000 savings as 'LIQUIDATED 1ST' [P1]. The asset statement shows liquid assets declining from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), and continuing annually [P10]. This is consistent with modest drawdown against ongoing expenditure. Critically, the Surplus Account remains at £0 throughout (no surpluses are being saved) [P8], [P10], so no new emergency buffer is being built. The report does not comment on whether this trajectory is sustainable or at what point liquid reserves become inadequate.
    
    On access: The report asserts that liquidity needs are funded from 'Liquid cash savings' and 'Pension funds via Flexi Access Drawdown.' The Flexi Access Drawdown wrapper permits withdrawal at any time (subject to tax and HMRC rules), so pension access is not constrained. The £6,000 cash account is immediately accessible. The guard 'A transfer between pension wrappers does not deplete an emergency fund — only new money from cash does' applies: the recommendation consolidates existing pension arrangements into a new Aviva pension via transfer; this does not consume the emergency fund. However, the guard 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it' also applies: the evidence shows the £6,000 is being gradually spent down with no replenishment mechanism, so it is not being 'retained.'
    
    Weakness: The report is silent on whether the £6,000 is intended as an emergency fund, what emergency fund size would be appropriate, whether the declining trajectory is acceptable, and at what point (if any) the household would face liquidity stress. An affordability assessment that does not quantify or comment on emergency fund adequacy fails to demonstrate support for the claim that the recommendation is affordable from a financial resilience perspective. The evidence of the planning scenario is consistent and shows cash being drawn down gradually; the problem is not contradictory data but absence of the report's own reasoning to justify that this is appropriate.
    
    No vulnerability overlay is evident from the evidence pack that would materially affect this assessment.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report recommends a Personal Pension wrapper with planned withdrawals via UFPLS (Uncrystallised Funds Pension Lump Sum) starting 2027-10-14. The client is stated to be age 71 at retirement (planned for 2027) with a preference for 'flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown'. The report states 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension' and notes that benefits can be drawn from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028). The baseline scenario shows retirement planned for 2027 when the client will be age 71. The proposed scenario shows the same retirement age of 71 in 2027.
    File:     The Aviva Policy Info [P3] states: 'Moving to income drawdown isn't possible after age 75. But once funds have been moved to income drawdown, the money can remain invested for the rest of life.' The Key Features [P4] confirm: 'You can't usually make any withdrawals before you reach the normal minimum pension age' (currently 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028). The People's Pension policy [P2] allows access at minimum pension age and confirms flexibility for those over that age. The Terms and Conditions [P7] define Income Drawdown and [P14] sets out retirement options including flexi-access drawdown. The intelliflo planning scenarios [P12] [P19] show retirement planned for 2027 when the client (Kim) will be 71 years old, with a separate client (John) retiring at age 66 in 2026. The fact find [P21] shows £6,000 in liquid savings. The planning baseline [P12] and proposed [P19] scenarios both show total assets of £122,997 with £6,000 in liquid assets and £116,997 in pensions. There is no explicit evidence of a specific access need or required withdrawal date tied to a financial emergency or liquidity requirement before age 75.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts planned withdrawals beginning 2027-10-14 when the client will be age 71, which falls within the standard access window. However, no specific access need, emergency fund depletion scenario, or liquidity requirement is evidenced in the file that would trigger these withdrawals at that date. The planning scenarios show retirement at 71 but do not quantify or justify why withdrawals must commence in October 2027 rather than being deferred to a later date or taken on an ad-hoc basis.
      - The report states the client will be at the selected retirement age of 71 in 2027, which is within the permitted drawdown window (55-75 under current rules, 57-75 from April 2028). No contradiction exists on technical access. However, the affordability analysis does not evidence that ongoing contributions cannot continue, nor does it evidence a specific liquidity need that necessitates the withdrawal schedule from that date.
      - The file does not evidence what funds will be withdrawn, at what frequency, or in what amounts. The report cites 'flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' as the preference but does not quantify the income to be drawn or relate it to evidenced expenditure or cashflow needs.
      - The fact find [P21] records £6,000 in liquid savings and notes 'It is desirable that a greater priority be given to the repayment / reduction of the levels of your debt prior to making an investment or committing to a regular premium.' However, no liabilities section is completed, and no emergency fund adequacy assessment is evidenced against the stated expenditure or the recommended withdrawal plan.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Specific access need date or liquidity requirement necessitating withdrawals from 2027-10-14, Quantified withdrawal amounts and frequency under the planned UFPLS drawdown, Evidence of ongoing contribution commitments that would cease at age 71, Emergency fund adequacy assessment: whether £6,000 liquid savings is sufficient given planned expenditure and withdrawal strategy, Affordability cashflow showing how income needs from 2027 onwards will be met, and how withdrawals will sustain that income relative to the remaining fund value at age 75
    The guard states that pension access restrictions are irrelevant where the client is already over the minimum pension age. The client will be 71 at retirement in 2027, which is above the current minimum pension age of 55 (and will remain above 57 from April 2028). Therefore, technical access via UFPLS is not restricted by pension rules. However, the requirement [G5.6] is titled 'Liquidity mismatch with access needs' and asks whether planned withdrawals are compatible with the wrapper and access date. The core issue is not whether access is *possible* but whether it is *affordable* and *evidenced*. The report states planned withdrawals from 2027 but provides no evidence: (1) quantifying the withdrawal amounts or frequency; (2) showing that ongoing contributions will cease at that point; (3) demonstrating that an emergency fund has been established and is adequate; or (4) linking the withdrawal schedule to evidenced financial need or cashflow requirements. The planning scenarios show a baseline and proposed scenario with total assets of £122,997, of which £6,000 is liquid (savings) and £116,997 is in pensions. The baseline scenario cashflow graph [P24] indicates expenditure throughout the plan but does not evidence a specific need for pension drawdown to commence in October 2027 or thereafter. Without quantified withdrawal amounts, frequency, and a demonstrated link to either (a) cessation of contributions or (b) evidenced expenditure need or emergency draw, the affordability cannot be assessed. The report asserts flexibility and planned drawdown but does not support the assertion with the financial inputs necessary to establish that the recommendation is affordable and appropriate. This is a Weak Justification issue: the recommendation is made but the supporting affordability analysis is absent or unexplained.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

      - Report does not disclose whether State Pension forecasts, entitlements, or qualifying years are on file; Fact Find [P5] has a 'State Pension Entitlement' section with no populated fields for Basic State Pension, Additional State Pension, Pension Credit, or Spouse's Pension.
      - Intelliflo baseline [P8] derives State Pension income from combined regular income £41,813 minus disclosed employment income £29,840, implying ~£11,973 State Pension annually, but this is a derived inference not an explicit statement.
    The report records two State Pension income entries but does not state their amounts, frequencies, or precise start dates in the extracted fields. The Fact Find [P5] shows a State Pension Entitlement section completed with no values entered, indicating the adviser did not capture or did not have access to State Pension forecast information. The affordability case presented in the Intelliflo scenarios [P2], [P8]-[P10] relies on State Pension income continuing as part of regular income to maintain cashflow surplus, but the report does not evidence the basis for assuming this income (no BR19 projection, no statement of qualifying years, no verification of entitlement). For clients aged 65 and 69 in 2025 with State Pension Age 66, one client should already be receiving State Pension or imminently eligible; the Fact Find confirms current receipt of £230 monthly but does not explain whether this is interim, reduced, or final entitlement. The absence of gross income figures in the report's canonical model prevents verification against the Fact Find's net figures and internal cross-check with the Intelliflo derivations.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Kim's retirement at age 66 in 2026 with cessation of employment income of £1,100 net monthly; (2) John's retirement at age 71 in 2027 with cessation of employment income of £1,300 net monthly; (3) Inheritance Tax treatment change from 6 April 2027 affecting pensions; (4) Emergency liquidity needs met from liquid cash savings, with regular pension access taking 10-15 working days. The report states 'You expect to retire within the next two years' and 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully.' On expenditure, the Fact Find shows 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No' and total monthly expenditure of £1,718.
    File:     The Fact Find [P14] records total net monthly income of £1,430 (Basic Income £1,200 + State Pension £230) and asks 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future?' with response 'No'. Current monthly expenditure totals £1,718. The intelliflo planning documents [P2, P5, P12] contain a timeline showing: 2026 — 70/66 'Retire (Kim)'; 2027 — 71/67 'Retire (John)'; 2060 — 100/96 'Illness (John)' and 'Death (John)'; 2060 — 104/100 'Illness (Kim)' and 'Death (Kim)'. The cashflow analysis [P5] shows 'Required Withdrawal' patterns with accessible pensions declining modestly over time (£142,239 at age 83/79 down to £154,757 by age 89/85). Liquid assets remain stable at approximately £5,200-£5,400 throughout the projection period. The planning documents do not explicitly quantify the income loss from Kim's retirement (£1,100 net) or John's retirement (£1,300 net) as separate line items in the annual cashflow tables.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Fact Find records 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future? No' [P14] but the report asserts retirement income changes of £1,100 monthly (Kim) and £1,300 monthly (John) with defined dates. The Fact Find response contradicts the report's identified foreseeable changes.
      - The report states planned expenditure changes are absent ('Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No') but does not reconcile this with the income loss of £2,400 net monthly from both retirements, which would create a material affordability gap given current monthly surplus of -£288 (expenditure £1,718 exceeds income £1,430).
      - The intelliflo planning timeline [P12] marks retirement events at specific ages (Kim age 66 in 2026, John age 71 in 2027) but the annual cashflow tables in [P5] do not separately itemize the income impact of these retirements in the years 2026-2027 to show how the household transitions from £1,430 current income to post-retirement income sources (State Pension and pension drawdown).
    A consistency requirement compares the suitability report's assertions against the evidence in the file. The report identifies two retirement dates with quantified income losses (Kim £1,100 net/month in 2026, John £1,300 net/month in 2027), yet the Fact Find explicitly states 'No' to foreseeable income changes. This is an internal contradiction within the file itself, not a discrepancy between report and evidence: both appear in category [B] and [I]. However, the requirement is whether the affordability is 'fully supported by, and consistent with, the evidence provided.' The Fact Find records no expected expenditure changes and a current monthly deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718). When the report's identified retirement income losses are applied (totalling £2,400 net per month), the household would face a monthly shortfall of approximately £2,688 once both retirements occur. The planning scenario [P5] shows this is managed through pension drawdown and managed via the 'Required Withdrawal' mechanism in the cashflow analysis, but the report does not explicitly reconcile the current deficit position or explain how ongoing £1,718 monthly expenditure is afforded pre-retirement when net income is only £1,430. The planning documents project stable liquid assets (circa £5,200-£5,400) and growing accessible pensions throughout retirement, suggesting the model assumes the current deficit is bridged by pension draws, but this bridge mechanism is not detailed in the narrative affordability assessment. The report asserts emergency liquidity is 'retained sufficient' but does not quantify this reserve, and the planning shows liquid assets of only £6,000 initially. The guardian notes that 'expenditure already inside the monthly budget must not be counted a second time as a future one-off,' but the current £1,718 monthly expenditure is already unaffordable from the stated £1,430 income, so the transition to retirement creates a consistency issue: either current expenditure must be reduced or current income must be higher than stated. The gap is not explained in the affordability commentary.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.8, G6.9). Rationale text items 6 and 12 are identical: 'Your existing funds are invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range. We would not recommend remaining invested in these types of funds.'. Rationale text items 7 and 13 are identical: 'The receiving scheme provides more flexible retirement options, ensuring you can access your pension savings in a way that best suits your needs, including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies.'. The report links all 13 rationale items to identical objective sets OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3, but no document in the case file defines what these objectives are or how they map to specific recommendations. 12 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

      - Rationale text items 7 and 13 are identical: 'The receiving scheme provides more flexible retirement options, ensuring you can access your pension savings in a way that best suits your needs, including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies.'
      - The report links all 13 rationale items to identical objective sets OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3, but no document in the case file defines what these objectives are or how they map to specific recommendations.
      - The extraction metadata notes ambiguity in risk rating: client questionnaire rated 4 but agreed rating was 5.
