Second, no passage in the case file defines the objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 or explains how they differ or which recommendation targets which — all five recommendations reference the same three objectives, making it impossible to verify that each rationale is tailored to its own specific recommendation. Third, the extraction metadata flags internal inconsistencies: pension values stated as £110,000 in narrative, £106,000 in capacity-for-loss section, and £110,185.47 in the arrangements table; charge calculations imply fund values that match neither total; and performance chart labels are reversed between sections. These inconsistencies undermine confidence in the precision of the rationale. Fourth, while P4 documents the overall strategy ('consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning'), it does not state which objective each arrangement recommendation serves or why each specific fund/provider choice is suitable. The rationale for Aviva switch states 'lifestyling funds' are unsuitable and need not be recommended for internal switch because blended funds underperform — but the critical yield section (flagged in extraction metadata) shows the new solution would achieve £115,000 versus existing £127,000 at age 75 (a 9.4% decrease), a material disadvantage not explained in the rationale. The rationale asserts consolidation and flexibility as drivers but does not address why consolidation into a SIPP with lower projected value meets the stated objective better than retaining existing arrangements. The evidence shows client wants to understand and consolidate pensions (P4, P1), but the suitability report does not articulate which of OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 maps to consolidation, flexibility, or control, nor does it explain the trade-offs (e.g., lower projected value, new adviser fees). The linked objectives are therefore abstract and unsupported by the case file.

Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.'
    File:     [P5] states the objective: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' [P14] records: 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement.' [P15] lists reasons for recommendations as: Aviva—'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich—'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension—'Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows—(truncated, no detail provided); Standard Life—(truncated, no detail provided). The policy research document shows 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column is blank for most recommendations, indicating the link between recommendation and objective was not explicitly documented.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 13 instances of client-specific rationale but policy research [P15] shows blank cells in 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column for most recommendations
      - Report claims recommendations explain 'how and why' they meet needs but policy research rationales are brief and generic: 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' without client-specific linkage
      - Policy research shows truncated or missing justifications for Scottish Widows and Standard Life recommendations
      - No evidence in file of alternatives considered for any recommendation, despite requirement to justify rejected alternatives
    The report asserts client-specific rationale on every recommendation (13 instances, all true), but the supporting evidence reveals a material gap between assertion and documentation. The fact find correctly captures the client's objectives: consolidation for simplicity, two-year retirement horizon, £1,700 net monthly income need, and ATR 5. The policy research [P15] demonstrates that at the time of recommendation creation, the adviser's working notes show generic justifications ('expensive', 'no adviser servicing', 'lifestyle') without explicit linking to the client's specific circumstances. Most critically, the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank, indicating the adviser did not document how each recommendation addresses the stated consolidation objective. The rationale provided—'expensive', 'no adviser servicing'—is standardised language that could apply to any client, not client-specific explanation of why consolidation into specific products meets this client's need for simplification and retirement income. The assertion of 13 instances of client-specific rationale cannot be reconciled with policy research notes that show blank objective linkages and generic justifications. This represents internal inconsistency: the canonical model claims personalisation that the supporting documentation does not evidence. The requirement demands 'client-specific rationale naming an objective' and 'personalised language demonstrating consideration of individual circumstances'; the evidence shows objective-naming fields blank and reasons stated in generic terms. No alternatives are documented as considered and rejected. The guard regarding standard regulatory wording does not apply here; this concerns substantive rationale, not boilerplate protections.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents five alternatives considered: (1) Switch to Global Investments fund within People's Pension — rejected because it has not performed as strongly as current selection and consolidation is more suitable; (2) Leave pension pot to grow until retirement — considered but rejected implicitly; (3) Leave existing Aviva pension untouched — rejected because lifestyling funds automatically reduce risk outside agreed risk range of 5; (4) Switch investment funds within existing Aviva pension — rejected because it does not address consolidation objective and existing arrangement does not allow adviser management; (5) Leave existing Zurich pension untouched — rejected because current holdings use blend of risk 7 and risk 2 funds lacking proper multi-asset fund at risk level 5, and single asset type exposure reduces diversification. The report also states alternatives for other arrangements: People's Pension to be retained; Scottish Widows to be encashed; Standard Life to be switched. The report claims these rejections are client-specific and linked to objectives, particularly OBJ3 (consolidation and retirement readiness).
    File:     The research documents (Pension switch report and file notes) identify alternatives evaluated: (P22) File notes record that Scottish Widows was 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing'; Aviva alternative 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR. Discounted switch'; Zurich 'do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used...Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types...blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally. For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch. The main priority for the client is to consolidate'; Standard Life 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'. (P1, P2, P3, P5, P10, P14) Multiple pension switch reports show comparative analysis between existing and new solutions with critical yield calculations showing switching produces material decreases in projected value (e.g., P5 mid-growth: existing £127,000 vs new £115,000, a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease). (P7, P8) SIPP research report indicates 'No filters were applied', 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', 'No Product DNA analysis was carried out', and 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection. (P16, P18) Suitability report discusses stakeholder pensions as alternative, stating 'I am recommending a plan which is not a stakeholder pension plan as this is more suitable for your circumstances because the solution is more cost effective' but does not cite specific charge comparison supporting this claim in the same section.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims switch to Aviva produces consolidation and better alignment with risk 5, but pension switch reports (P5) at mid-growth show existing pensions projected to £127,000 versus new solution £115,000—a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%, contradicting implicit claim that new solution improves outcomes
      - Report states rejection of Aviva in-situ switch is because 'Existing arrangement does not allow adviser management', but file notes (P22) indicate the real reason was 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR'—the adviser management constraint is mentioned but appears secondary to the fund risk rating mismatch
      - Report rejects Zurich pension by stating it uses 'blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund' lacking 'proper multi-asset fund at your agreed risk level', but file notes (P22) add that 'blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally'—performance comparison is mentioned in research but the report's rejection rationale emphasizes risk profile and diversification, not performance
      - Report states for Aviva: 'This fund has not performed as strongly as your current selection' regarding Global Investments alternative, but the comparison document (P22) does not appear to contain a performance comparison between Aviva Global Investments and the recommended solution; the claim rests on research not retrieved or not presented with evidence
      - Report presents stakeholder pension as alternative (P16) stating 'I am recommending a plan which is not a stakeholder pension plan as this is more suitable for your circumstances because the solution is more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension', but no charge comparison between stakeholder and recommended Aviva Platform solution is evident in the charge sections or research documents provided
      - Report presents 'Leave the pension pot to grow until retirement' as alternative with statement 'If you were to leave this pot to grow until you retire, you could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due', but does not explain why this was rejected or why consolidation is preferable despite potential tax advantage
      - SIPP research (P7, P8) shows 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection despite report claiming Aviva Platform was chosen as superior to alternatives—the research report indicates no comparative justification was documented for platform selection
    The report presents alternatives with stated rejection reasons, but the evidence reveals material gaps and inconsistencies. (1) Performance claims lack support: The report rejects Global Investments by stating it 'has not performed as strongly as your current selection', but the passage containing this claim (P22, file notes) does not present a performance comparison; the rejection is asserted without the evidence base. (2) Risk-profile rejections are partially supported: The rejection of Zurich and in-situ Aviva switches on grounds of risk mismatch to level 5 is corroborated by P22, which confirms Zurich lacks risk 5 funds and Aviva's only multi-asset option is risk rated 4. However, file notes also cite performance comparison ('blend of funds have not performed as well') which the report does not foreground, suggesting the research identifies multiple reasons but the report presents only one rationale. (3) Consolidation rationale insufficiently justified: The report emphasizes consolidation as the primary objective (OBJ3) and rejects alternatives partly on grounds they do not achieve consolidation. However, the pension switch reports (P5) demonstrate that the recommended solution produces a material reduction in projected value (9.4% decrease at mid-growth) compared to retaining the existing pensions. The report does not explain why consolidation—which carries a measurable cost in foregone growth—outweighs the client's apparent financial objectives of maximizing retirement funds. This is a weak justification: the report states consolidation is 'more suitable' and represents the 'primary objective', but does not quantify the trade-off or explain why the financial cost is acceptable. (4) Stakeholder pension alternative insufficiently evaluated: The report claims the recommended solution is 'more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' but provides no charge comparison data for stakeholder products. The MiFID II disclosure (P13) shows aggregate fund costs of 0.37%, which falls below the 1.5% stakeholder pension maximum, but no stakeholder product was explicitly costed against the recommendation. (5) Platform selection lacks documented justification: The SIPP research (P7, P8) contains no comparative analysis ('No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', 'No additional justification text has been entered'), yet the report presents Aviva Platform as the suitable choice. The research output does not show why Aviva was preferred over alternatives such as Elevate (Aberdeen) or ARC (Aegon), which are listed in the shortlist (P7) but given no evaluation. (6) Leave-to-grow option not addressed: The report mentions a tax advantage ('you could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due') but does not explain why this alternative was rejected despite its tax efficiency. This is a potential concern: a client-specific advantage is identified but not reconciled with the consolidation recommendation. (7) Adviser management constraint: The report states existing Aviva does not 'allow adviser management', but this is presented as a constraint limiting options rather than a reason to consolidate—it does not explain why adviser management is necessary to meet the client's stated objectives. The analysis of alternatives shows rejection reasons are stated but several lack evidentiary support in the research documents, and material trade-offs (particularly the reduced projected value from switching) are not explained or reconciled with the recommendation.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report identifies 5 existing arrangements: People's Pension (Retain), Scottish Widows (Encash), Aviva (Switch), Zurich (Switch), and Standard Life (Switch). Each arrangement has an adviceAction recorded and supporting rationale marked as client-specific. The rationales reference the client's circumstances: employer contributions for People's Pension; small pots rules for Scottish Widows; lifestyling fund misalignment with risk profile for Aviva and Zurich; and lifestyling funds, investment choice, retirement flexibility, and adviser charging for Standard Life.
    File:     The evidence pack contains product research documents (P3, P5), policy information statements (P7, P9, P4), and a policy research table (P3, P5, P12) that identify the 5 arrangements by name, policy number, start date, plan type, fund value, and valuation date. P21 (Suitability Report extract) lists retirement options and fund availability for each arrangement and notes 'Current workplace scheme' under People's Pension and 'Retain and Encash' under Scottish Widows. P23 (Suitability Report extract) provides detailed rationale for Standard Life switch covering costs, lifestyling, investment choice, retirement flexibility, adviser charging, and monitoring. However, the evidence pack contains no independent corroboration of the rationales offered for Aviva and Zurich switches. The extraction metadata reports that /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaresLost is 'PresentButUnparseable', indicating critical replacement analysis detail may not have been fully extracted.
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life switch rationale asserts 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (P23) but P23 also states 'Lower Costs' as a reason for switching, creating an internal contradiction about whether costs favour the switch or the existing arrangement
      - The report's narrative on charges shows inconsistency: P23 text states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive' but the charge comparison context suggests the switch to Aviva may not deliver the cost saving claimed
      - Aviva and Zurich switching rationales are extracted as client-specific but cite only generic factors (lifestyling risk misalignment, investment choice, adviser charging) without engagement-specific detail differentiating these two arrangements or explaining why switching each specifically serves the client's stated objectives
      - Scottish Widows advice action is listed as 'Encash' in the canonical model but P21 context suggests this may be conditional ('Retain and Encash'), creating ambiguity about the actual recommended outcome
    The report provides a stated recommended outcome and at least one rationale for each of the 5 existing arrangements. However, the quality and specificity of the rationales varies materially. People's Pension (Retain) and Scottish Widows (Encash) are supported by straightforward, client-specific reasoning tied to employer contributions and small pots rules respectively. Standard Life (Switch) has detailed, multi-faceted rationale in P23, but contains an internal inconsistency: the narrative claims lower costs as a reason for switching while simultaneously asserting Standard Life is 0.08% more expensive—this undermines the coherence of the recommendation and contradicts the stated basis ('Lower Costs'). Aviva and Zurich (both Switch) are supported by rationales that invoke generic factors (lifestyling risk drift, investment choice, adviser charging) that could apply to either arrangement or indeed to other clients, without clear client-specific justification for why each specific switch serves this client's stated objectives. The extraction metadata notes that critical replacement analysis is 'PresentButUnparseable', suggesting material detail may exist in the report but was not successfully extracted, leaving this assessment unable to verify whether the full rationale is client-specific or whether alternatives were properly considered. The rationales are recorded as client-specific in the canonical model, but the evidence provided does not fully corroborate this characterisation for Aviva and Zurich. The cost contradiction in the Standard Life section is particularly material because it goes to the logical coherence of the recommendation itself: if the new scheme is more expensive, the primary stated justification for switching is inverted. No passage from the evidence pack independently confirms the cost basis for any of the switches.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report contains five recommendations with the following actions: (1) Retain People's Pension (£6,601.55); (2) Encash Scottish Widows pension plan (£211.32); (3) Switch Aviva pension (£3,744.36) to new Aviva Platform; (4) Switch Zurich pension (£103,439.24) to new Aviva Platform; (5) Switch Standard Life pension (£3,002) to new Aviva Platform. All five recommendations are marked as 'Recommended' in adviceStatus. For the two non-switching recommendations (Retain, Encash), willAdviserImplement is false and responsibleParty is 'Client'. For the three switching recommendations, willAdviserImplement is true and responsibleParty is 'Adviser'. Each recommendation includes an intendedOutcome: Retain aims to 'Preserve employer contributions and current fund performance while maintaining workplace pension benefits'; Encash aims to 'Remove a small, inefficient pension arrangement and redirect the funds to enhance overall financial position'; the three switches aim to consolidate arrangements into 'a single platform that better aligns with your risk profile, offers greater investment flexibility, and enables ongoing adviser oversight' (with minor wording variations). Implementation details state an expected timescale of '10-15 working days', cancellation rights of 30 days, and next steps including out-of-market risk disclosure (boilerplate), access timescale acknowledgement (client-specific), and a recommendation to complete beneficiaries nomination form (client-specific).
    File:     The evidence pack contains no contradictory statements about whether the recommendations are recommended, who will implement them, or the intended outcomes. The Aviva Terms and Conditions ([P1], [P4], [P5], [P6], [P8]) establish the technical framework for how charges are deducted, how adviser roles function, how income drawdown operates, and how client contact and instructions are processed via advisers, but these describe operational mechanics rather than comment on the specific recommendations or their status. The Risk profile report ([P12], [P15]) states 'The agreed risk level considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal' and describes a Balanced risk profile ([1] notation), but does not address recommendation status or implementation responsibility. No evidence document directly addresses whether the five recommendations are 'Recommended', who will execute each change, or what the intended outcomes are.
    The report clearly states for each of the five recommendations: (1) its action type (Retain, Encash, or Switch); (2) that it is 'Recommended' (adviceStatus); (3) whether the adviser will implement it (willAdviserImplement: true for switches, false for retain/encash); (4) who is responsible (Adviser for switches, Client for retain/encash); and (5) the intended outcome for each. The implementation section provides an expected timescale ('10-15 working days'), describes cancellation rights, and includes next steps. For the change recommendations (the three switches), the report explicitly states the adviser will implement them. For the non-change recommendations (retain and encash), the report explicitly states the client is responsible and the adviser will not implement. The intended outcomes are stated individually for each recommendation, linking each to the client's circumstances (e.g., consolidation for better risk alignment, removal of inefficient small arrangement). The boilerplate text on out-of-market risk is generic; however, it is supplemented by two client-specific statements: acknowledgement that the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' and a recommendation to complete the beneficiaries nomination form. The evidence pack does not contradict any of these statements. The Aviva terms establish that adviser charges and implementation steps are handled via the adviser role, consistent with the report's assignment of responsibility.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report specifies implementation details across multiple sections: (1) Solution-level: expectedTimescale is '10-15 working days'; outOfMarketRisk is disclosed; cancellationRights are detailed; nextSteps include three client-specific items covering out-of-market risk, access timescale, and beneficiaries nomination. (2) Recommendation-level: Five recommendations are listed with actions (Retain, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch); three recommendations have expectedTimescale stated as 'To be arranged following client acceptance; typically 10-15 working days for disinvestment and reinvestment'; responsibleParty is specified as 'Client' for first two and 'Adviser' for last three. The report states implementation will occur 'following client acceptance' but does not specify distinct timescales for each action or clearly delineate when each party's responsibility commences and ends.
    File:     The evidence provided comprises provider terms and conditions (Aviva, Scottish Widows, Standard Life), fund factsheets, key features documents, and planning scenarios. The documents establish general operational timescales: Aviva Terms state that for Income Drawdown cancellation/changes, the adviser must request at least '10 working days before' the next payment date [P1]. For fund closures, providers 'will, where possible, notify Your Adviser or You and request reinvestment instructions 30 days in advance' [P3]. For withdrawal transactions, 'Any instruction to withdraw Your Investment will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day' [P7]. Scottish Widows advises that 'Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing advisor charging' [P10]. However, no evidence in the file specifies the timescale for completing a pension transfer (switching), encashment, or retention actions, nor does it specify by whom each specific action will be arranged or executed in this case.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states expectedTimescale of '10-15 working days' at solution level; file provides general operational standards (10 working days for cancellation requests [P1], 30 days advance notice for fund closure [P3], next business day for withdrawals [P7]) but these are provider service standards, not timescales for executing the switches and encashment recommended
      - Report specifies responsibleParty as 'Adviser' for three switch recommendations but file evidence does not clarify whether the adviser or the pension provider will execute the actual disinvestment and reinvestment, or whether both parties have sequential responsibilities
      - Report states actions will occur 'following client acceptance' but does not specify when client acceptance is required, by what date it must be obtained, or what constitutes acceptance in this case
      - Report recommends completion of beneficiaries nomination 'after the transfer process is begun' [stated in nextSteps] but does not specify a deadline by which this must be completed or confirm this aligns with provider processes
      - Report does not differentiate between: when the adviser will submit instructions, when the provider will execute them, when the client must take any action, or when the entire process will be complete
    The requirement asks whether recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where material to suitability. The report does address each element in isolation but does not integrate them into a clear, sequential implementation plan. (1) What action: Five discrete actions are named (Retain, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch). This is clear. (2) When: The report provides a single timescale ('10-15 working days') applied generically to all switches and encashment, stating it covers 'administration, disinvestment and banking processes' and will occur 'following client acceptance'. However, it does not specify when acceptance must be given, when the adviser will submit the instruction, or when each phase (disinvestment, reinvestment, completion) will occur. For encashment of People's Pension (recommendation 2), no timescale is stated at all at recommendation level. (3) By whom: The report assigns responsibleParty as 'Client' for the first two recommendations (Retain, Encash) and 'Adviser' for the three switches. However, this is ambiguous: does 'Client' mean the client alone executes encashment, or does the client instruct the adviser who then executes it? Does 'Adviser' mean the adviser submits instructions to the provider, or that the adviser is solely responsible for execution? The file evidence establishes that providers require adviser involvement (e.g., 'Your Adviser will need to do so' for cancellation [P1]) and Scottish Widows explicitly states plans do not support ongoing adviser charging [P10], but this does not clarify who holds responsibility in this specific case. (4) Materiality: Timing is material because the report identifies out-of-market risk—the period between disinvestment and reinvestment creates exposure to market movement [asserted in nextSteps]. The report itself acknowledges 'whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market'. If this risk is material enough to disclose, then the precise timing of each phase (when old funds are sold, when new funds are purchased) is material to suitability. The report does not provide this precision. (5) The report does provide client-specific context: it states the client 'is comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' regarding the 10-15 working day access timescale. This demonstrates the adviser has assessed suitability against the client's liquidity position. However, this does not remedy the absence of sequential, phase-specific timescales and responsibility clarification for the switches themselves. (6) The beneficiaries nomination instruction ('I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun') establishes that a step occurs after transfer commencement but provides no deadline. This is a low-level omission because the materiality of this step to suitability is unclear, but it is still an action without a defined completion timeframe. (7) False-positive guards: 'Naming the adviser firm rather than an individual satisfies by whom'—the report does not name the adviser firm or an individual; it uses the generic term 'Adviser'. 'A timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale'—the report does not quote provider service standards; it uses its own figure of '10-15 working days', which may or may not align with provider timescales. 'Timing is material where the client is out of the market or working to a deadline'—the report explicitly discloses out-of-market risk, making timing material. None of these guards explain the gaps identified. The overall assessment: the report provides a single, generic timescale applied to all recommendations, assigns responsibility generically, and does not sequence the steps or clarify which party acts in which phase. Where out-of-market risk is disclosed as material to suitability, the absence of phase-specific timing and clarified responsibility allocation represents a Potential Concern.
      · Terms and conditions (1).md [H] ([P1])
        "If You want to cancel Your regular income payments once they have started, then Your Adviser will need to do so at least 10 working days before Your existing regular income payment date."
      · Terms and conditions (1).md [H] ([P3])
        "In the event that a Fund manager closes a Fund, or We are unable to continue administering Investments in a Fund and this affects You, We will, where possible, notify Your Adviser or You and request reinvestment instructions 30 days in advance."
      · Terms and conditions (1).md [H] ([P7])
        "Any instruction to withdraw Your Investment will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day."
      · Scottish Widows Policy Info.md [E] ([P10])
        "Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission."

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three ancillary matters: (1) A recommendation that the client 'complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided after the transfer process is begun' (page 16), marked as client-specific and recommended. (2) A statement that the client 'are comfortable with the concept' of 10-15 working days access timescale for funds, marked as client-specific and reported. (3) The canonical model shows /parties/clients[]/estatePlanning/hasWill = true, /parties/clients[]/estatePlanning/isWillUpToDate = false, and /parties/clients[]/estatePlanning/isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = false.
    File:     The Fact Find (P1, P2, P3, P5) records the client's personal details, marital status (Married), spouse details including age 65, and family information. However, there is no section in the Fact Find marked 'Estate Planning' with answers to will status, will currency, or Power of Attorney status. The file does record at P3 that the client is married with a spouse aged 65 living with them. The Fact Find form at P8 shows a 'Retirement Risk Replay' section but does not show completed estate planning questions. No estate planning section appears to have been completed or recorded in the available documents.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client has a will (hasWill=true) but Fact Find contains no estate planning section and no will status is recorded in any retrieved document.
      - Report asserts client's will is not up to date (isWillUpToDate=false) but no estate planning section exists in the Fact Find to evidence this assessment.
      - Report asserts client does not have Power of Attorney (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=false) but no estate planning section in Fact Find records this status.
    The requirement tests whether ancillary recommendations about beneficiary nomination, will and Power of Attorney are stated with an owner and consistent with file records. The report recommends completion of a beneficiary nomination form, which is a clear recommendation with the client as the responsible party (implementation will be after transfer process). This is client-specific and the owner is identified. However, the report's canonical model asserts three estate planning facts (hasWill=true, isWillUpToDate=false, isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=false) that are not evidenced anywhere in the retrieved case file. The Fact Find document does not contain any completed estate planning section. The only estate planning data in the canonical model appears to be unsupported by any file record. The beneficiary nomination recommendation itself is properly stated with clear ownership (client to complete the form provided), but the background estate planning assertions lack file support. Under the guard that 'Assertion absent is not a finding on its own — a case need not raise estate planning,' the lack of an estate planning section in the Fact Find is not itself a defect. However, the canonical model explicitly asserts three estate planning facts (will status, whether updated, and Power of Attorney status) that contradict the silence in the file: the file contains no estate planning section whatsoever, yet the report's extracted data claims these facts. This is a data inconsistency between the canonical model and file records.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report states total ongoing charge for recommended plan as 0.44% (Platform 0.21% + Investment Management 0.22%), but the Aviva Illustration (P8) lists investment charges of 0.29% and 0.18% with a weighted average of 0.23%, not a combined 0.22% investment management charge. The arithmetic reconciliation between the report's stated layers (0.21% + 0.22% = 0.43%, not 0.44% as totalled) and the illustration's presentation is unclear. Report discloses fund charges separately (0.18% and 0.29%) but does not aggregate these with the platform and investment management layers into a single all-in charge figure; instead it lists them separately, making cumulative impact difficult for a client to extract at a glance. The SIPP research report (P10) shows total aggregated fund costs for CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc of 0.55% including transaction costs of 0.13%, but the suitability report (P3) excludes transaction costs, creating a presentation discrepancy between what the fund documentation states as the total cost and what the report discloses. 29 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following cost layers for the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension: Platform charge 0.21% (£231.39 pa), Investment Management charge 0.22% (£253.43 pa), and two fund charges: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 0.18%, and CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 0.29%. Total ongoing charge stated as 0.44% of fund value pa. The report states an initial advice fee of 3% of amount invested (£3,305.55 with current transfer values). The report explicitly excludes transaction costs of underlying funds, stating they are variable and subject to change and are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures presented net of all charges. The report discloses an aggregated total and states the illustration contains information regarding cumulative effect of charges on investment returns.
    File:     The Aviva Illustration (P8) confirms the Aviva charge at 0.21% and lists investment-related charges for CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc at 0.29% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 0.18%, with a weighted average charge of 0.23%. The illustration shows these are taken from the cash account or by disinvestment if insufficient cash. The Aviva Key Features document (P6) confirms adviser charges are agreed with the financial adviser and deducted from the pension portfolio, and that the illustration shows all charges to be paid. The KIID for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (P1) confirms an ongoing charge of 0.18% with no entry or exit charges. The KIID (P2) confirms ongoing charges exclude costs of buying or selling assets for the fund. The Zurich Policy Info (P7) shows the existing Zurich arrangement has an annual management charge of 0.75% (0.93% as stated in the report). The Pension Switch Report (P23) for Zurich shows investment charges of 0.50% and a platform charge of £2.12 per regular contribution. The SIPP research report (P10) shows total aggregated fund costs for CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc at 0.55% (comprising ongoing costs 0.24%, transaction costs 0.13%). The Standard Life Policy Info shows fund charges of 1.0% with a discount of -0.5%. The Illustration—new (P18) for Aviva shows the structure of charges taken from cash account or through disinvestment.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total ongoing charge for recommended plan as 0.44% (Platform 0.21% + Investment Management 0.22%), but the Aviva Illustration (P8) lists investment charges of 0.29% and 0.18% with a weighted average of 0.23%, not a combined 0.22% investment management charge. The arithmetic reconciliation between the report's stated layers (0.21% + 0.22% = 0.43%, not 0.44% as totalled) and the illustration's presentation is unclear.
      - Report discloses fund charges separately (0.18% and 0.29%) but does not aggregate these with the platform and investment management layers into a single all-in charge figure; instead it lists them separately, making cumulative impact difficult for a client to extract at a glance.
      - The SIPP research report (P10) shows total aggregated fund costs for CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc of 0.55% including transaction costs of 0.13%, but the suitability report (P3) excludes transaction costs, creating a presentation discrepancy between what the fund documentation states as the total cost and what the report discloses.
      - The report's stated Investment Management charge of 0.22% does not clearly reconcile to any single document layer; the Aviva Illustration shows investment charges but labels them by fund name (0.18% and 0.29%) rather than as a single 'Investment Management' layer.
      - Standard Life existing arrangement shows fund charges of 1.0% with a -0.5% discount (net 0.5%), but the Pension Switch Report (P23) states investment charges at 0.50% for the existing Standard Life arrangement, creating ambiguity about which charge figure applies.
    The report discloses material cost components: platform charge (0.21%), investment management layer (0.22%), fund charges (0.18% and 0.29%), initial advice fee (3% or £3,305.55), and transaction costs are explicitly excluded with reasons stated. The guard on transaction costs exclusion is satisfied: the report states they are variable, subject to change, and already reflected in fund performance net of all charges. This is a valid disclosure of an exclusion.
    
    However, three material issues arise:
    
    1. Arithmetic reconciliation: The report states Platform 0.21% + Investment Management 0.22% = 0.44% total ongoing charge, but 0.21% + 0.22% = 0.43%, not 0.44%. The discrepancy of 0.01% is small but arithmetically inconsistent with the stated figures.
    
    2. All-in charge presentation: The report lists platform, investment management, and fund charges separately but never provides a single aggregated all-in cost figure. While the report states "aggregatedTotalDisclosed: true" in the extraction, inspection of the actual report narrative (P3) shows charges broken into a table with Platform/Investment Management/Total Charge columns but no single line stating "your total all-in ongoing cost is X%". The cumulative effect is addressed by reference to "your personal illustration" but the report itself does not show the cumulative figure in the narrative or summary.
    
    3. Coherence with evidence: The SIPP research report (P10) clearly states total aggregated fund costs of 0.55% for CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (0.24% ongoing + 0.13% transaction costs + 0.00% one-off + 0.00% incidental). The suitability report discloses only the fund's 0.29% ongoing charge and excludes transaction costs. While the exclusion is disclosed, the absence of any reference to the SIPP research report's comprehensive cost breakdown—which is in the case file and available to the adviser—means the client does not see that independent research has identified higher total costs (0.55%) than the report acknowledges (0.29% fund + 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment = 0.72% all-in without transaction costs; 0.72% + 0.13% transaction = 0.85% if transaction costs are included). This is material because the report states transaction costs are "already accounted for in the fund's performance figures" but the SIPP research report quantifies them separately.
    
    4. Consistency across existing and recommended: The report's Pension Switch Charges table (P3) compares existing and recommended charges but uses different bases or labels for some arrangements. For Standard Life, the text states the plan is "0.08% more expensive" but the Pension Switch Report (P23) shows investment charges at 0.50% and Platform at £2.12 per contribution, and the Standard Life Policy Info (P20) shows fund charges of 1.0% net of a 0.5% discount. The reconciliation between these figures is not clear in the report.
    
    The report satisfies the disclosure requirement in broad outline (platform, investment, fund, initial advice, and transaction costs are all addressed), but the all-in cost aggregation is delegated to the illustration without a clear summary statement in the report itself, and the arithmetic on the stated components does not reconcile to the claimed total. The SIPP research report evidence in the file (P10) contains more granular cost data than the suitability report discloses, raising a question about completeness of research support.
    
    None of the discrepancies relate to hidden cost layers; rather, they are presentation and reconciliation issues. The suitability report does identify the major cost components but does not provide a single, clear, arithmetically consistent all-in figure in the report narrative itself.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested (£3,305.55 with current transfer values), paid by the provider as a one-off charge, as stated in the Client Agreement. It also asserts an ongoing fee of 1% per annum (£1,101.85 for the first year based on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee), paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. The report states these charges are 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement'.
    File:     The Client Agreement [P12] states: 'For investments, we arrange and oversee on your behalf, there are two principal fees. A fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews which we will do on your behalf.' The standard fee schedule [P13] specifies: 'There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on' for initial advice on pensions and investments, with a minimum fee of £1,350. The same document states: 'For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1%' [P3]. The Client Agreement confirms fees are set out 'in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation' [P12]. The agreement specifies that 'This fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly' [P3], and that fees may be deducted from investments/pensions [P3, P13].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the initial fee is £3,305.55 'with current transfer values', but this figure cannot be verified against the stated basis. The derived figure computed from the canonical model shows the five arrangements total £116,998.47, which at 3% would yield £3,509.95, not £3,305.55. The discrepancy suggests the fee is calculated on a different basis or fund value than stated.
      - The report states the ongoing fee of £1,101.85 is 'based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee', but the Client Agreement and standard fee schedule do not reference expected contributions as a basis for ongoing fees. The ongoing fee basis is stated in the Client Agreement as 1% of fund value for reviews, not as a calculation involving expected contributions.
      - The report's calculation of the ongoing fee (£1,101.85) cannot be arithmetically verified. If 1% is applied to the total current arrangement value of £116,998.47, the result would be £1,169.98, not £1,101.85. The report does not explain the basis for the reduced figure.
    The report correctly states the percentage rates (3% initial, 1% ongoing) which are corroborated by the Client Agreement [P12], TFAS standard fee schedule [P13], and the suitability report itself [P5]. The description of services provided for these fees is stated in the report and corroborated by the Client Agreement [P3, P5]. The method of payment (via the Aviva Platform) is confirmed in [P3]. However, there is a material inconsistency in the monetary amounts stated. The initial fee of £3,305.55 is stated in the report [P9] and confirmed in the Client Agreement [P12] verbatim as a figure that 'may fluctuate depending on transfer values'. The report acknowledges this variability but does not explain why £3,305.55 is the amount at 'current transfer values' when the five arrangements total £116,998.47. At 3% of that total, the initial fee should be approximately £3,509.95. The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 'for the first year' is similarly stated in the report [P5] but the arithmetic does not reconcile with the stated basis. The Client Agreement and fee schedule confirm both the percentage rates and the principle that fees are based on fund values, but do not provide a derived total to cross-check against the specific monetary amounts the report claims. The guard that 'monetary amounts must be that percentage of the stated investment' cannot be satisfied because the report's monetary figures do not equal the stated percentages applied to any single verifiable fund value in the evidence. The report's caveat that figures 'may fluctuate depending on transfer values' does not resolve this: the report must state the basis on which it arrived at the specific figures it asserts, and that basis is absent or unclear.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures in a table on page 18 (Appendix): Existing arrangements show 0.3% RIY at all three growth rates (low, mid, high). Recommended arrangement shows 2.1% RIY at low growth (0.00%), 2.2% at mid growth (2.95%), and 2.3% at high growth (5.88%). The report also states projected values at age 75: Existing £108,000 (low), £127,000 (mid), £150,000 (high); Recommended £97,600 (low), £115,000 (mid), £135,000 (high). Critical yields are stated as 1.9% (low), 4.8% (mid), 7.8% (high) for the recommended solution. Effects of switching are stated as -£10,400 or -9.6% (low), -£12,000 or -9.4% (mid), -£15,000 or -10.0% (high).
    File:     The suitability report table [P1] shows identical figures: RIY of 0.3% for existing at all rates, 2.1%/2.2%/2.3% for recommended; projected values £108,000/£127,000/£150,000 (existing) and £97,600/£115,000/£135,000 (recommended); critical yields 1.9%/4.8%/7.8%; and effects of switching -£10,400/-£12,000/-£15,000 or -9.6%/-9.4%/-10.0%. The provider illustration [P3] for the new solution shows projections at 1.9% (low, after inflation), 2.9% (mid, after inflation), and 5.9% (high, after inflation), yielding £97,500/£115,000/£135,000 respectively. The illustration also states 'All charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £115,000.00 - this is the same as reducing the investment growth after inflation from 2.9% to 0.8%. This is a reduction in the growth rate of 2.1%.' The pension switch report [P21] shows existing Standard Life plan with RIY 0.52% and projected maturity value £3,440 at mid growth 2.98%. The switch report [P23] shows for Standard Life only comparison: existing RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 0.6% at mid growth; and projected values existing £127,000, new solution £125,000 at mid growth (2.95%).
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report RIY for existing arrangement is stated as 0.3% at all growth rates; however, the pension switch report [P21] shows Standard Life (one of the existing pensions being consolidated) with investment charge 0.52%, implying a higher RIY than 0.3% for that component. The switch report [P23] alternative comparison for Standard Life alone shows existing RIY 0.3% but new solution RIY 0.6%, creating internal inconsistency about whether 0.3% is correct for the existing Standard Life plan.
      - The arithmetic of the effect of switching percentages does not reconcile with the absolute values stated. At mid growth: £127,000 existing vs £115,000 recommended gives a difference of £12,000. As a percentage of the existing fund value: £12,000 / £127,000 = 9.45%, which rounds to 9.4% (as stated). However, at low growth: £108,000 vs £97,600 = £10,400 difference; £10,400 / £108,000 = 9.63%, which rounds to 9.6% (as stated). At high growth: £150,000 vs £135,000 = £15,000; £15,000 / £150,000 = 10.0% (as stated). The percentages are arithmetically sound when checked against the projected values shown.
      - The provider illustration [P3] shows a 'Reduction in Yield' concept in real terms (adjusted for inflation) stating the cumulative charge impact reduces growth from 2.9% to 0.8%, a reduction of 2.1%, which aligns with the suitability report's stated RIY of 2.1% for the recommended solution at mid growth. However, the suitability report describes RIY as 'of growth rate' while the illustration describes it in absolute percentage point terms relative to the baseline 2.9% growth rate.
      - The existing arrangement RIY of 0.3% stated uniformly across all three growth rates is suspiciously flat and does not align with the detailed charge figures extracted: the Aviva existing arrangement shows 0.50% annual recurring charge, Zurich shows 0.93%, and Standard Life shows 0.52%, yet all are collapsed into a single 0.3% RIY figure in the suitability report.
      - The suitability report states on page 11 that for Standard Life, 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the switch charges table comparison shows the impact as +0.26% for Standard Life, creating a direct contradiction regarding which direction charges move and by how much.
    The suitability report presents RIY figures and associated projections in [P1], which is the report itself (category I). These cannot be used to corroborate the report's own assertions; they reflect internal consistency only. Cross-checking against external evidence: The provider illustration [P3] for the new solution corroborates the projected value of £115,000 at mid growth (2.9% after inflation) and confirms the cumulative charge impact. However, the illustration expresses charges in absolute percentage points (growth reduced from 2.9% to 0.8%, a 2.1% reduction), while the report's RIY table describes the same concept as '2.1% of growth rate,' creating ambiguity about whether the figures truly align. The pension switch report [P21], [P23] provide independent evidence on individual arrangements. For Standard Life alone [P23], the existing RIY is shown as 0.3% and new solution as 0.6%, both at mid growth—this aligns with the suitability report's treatment of the consolidated existing arrangement at 0.3%. However, the detailed charge data in [P21] for Standard Life shows an investment charge of 0.52%, which does not obviously translate to an RIY of 0.3% without knowing the fund value. The arithmetic checking above confirmed: projected value differences translate correctly to the stated percentage effects of switching. The core discrepancy is the uniform 0.3% RIY for existing arrangements across all three growth rates, which appears oversimplified given the range of underlying charges (0.50% to 0.93%) and the switch report's alternative comparison showing 0.6% RIY for the new solution relative to 0.3% for existing on the same plan. This pattern suggests the 0.3% figure may be a proxy or average applied to the consolidated position without clear explanation. The suitability report also contains the internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction metadata: page 11 text claims Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive' but the accompanying switch charges table shows impact of +0.26%, a threefold discrepancy that undermines confidence in the charge comparison layer. The cumulative impact—the central purpose of this check—is explicitly presented in tabular form with RIY, projected values, critical yields, and effects of switching all aligned. The figures internally cohere arithmetically. However, the evidence for the existing arrangement RIY of 0.3% across all rates is not independently supported by the charge data cited in the supporting documents, and one explicit contradiction appears on the same page (0.08% vs +0.26% for Standard Life). The lack of granular breakdown of how the 0.3% existing RIY was derived from the five underlying pensions leaves a material gap in the evidential chain.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P19)
        "Plan: Aviva Platform Personal Pension. Platform: 0.21%. Investment Management: 0.22%. Total Charge: 0.44%."

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts a like-for-like cost comparison basis with -0.24% (pa) or -£264 total difference, stating 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans.' Per-arrangement impacts are: EA3 (Aviva) 0.50% existing, -0.06% impact (£18.72 → -£2.25); EA4 (Zurich) 0.93% existing, -0.49% impact (£961.98 → -£506.85); EA5 (Standard Life) 0.52% existing, -0.08% impact (£186.19 → -£268.94). The report does not disclose adviser fees in the cost comparison table. However, the report separately states an initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) and notes in Critical Yield analysis that 'including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year.' The comparison table appears to exclude adviser fees, while critical yield projections include them. Recommendation benefits are stated as 'consolidation, get a better handle on pensions, ready for retirement with all options' but no explicit connection between cost differential and these benefits is articulated in the cost section.
    File:     Provider documents confirm individual charge rates: People's Pension 0.50% + £4.50pa [P1]; Scottish Widows 0.19% + 0.10% platform [P1]; Aviva 0.50% [P1]; Zurich 0.18% fund charge [P6]; Standard Life 0.52% [P1]. Zurich policy illustration [P15] shows £106,000 retirement value at age 70. The Pension switch report [P13] shows existing pension projected at £141,000 at age 75 vs. new solution £138,000, with critical yield of 6.3% and effect of switching of £3,000 or 2.1% decrease. Suitability report Critical Yield table [P21] shows at mid-growth 2.95%: existing £127,000 vs. new solution £115,000, difference £12,000 or 9.4% decrease, with critical yield 4.8%. Research report [P24] shows CT Universal MAP fund total costs 0.37% (ongoing 0.24%, transaction 0.13%). File notes [P18] state Standard Life funds 'within same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' but no numeric comparison of SL vs. Aviva charges is provided. No provider documentation confirms the per-arrangement charge differentials (e.g., Aviva vs. Zurich -0.49%, or Standard Life -0.08%).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states EA4 (Zurich) existing charge 0.93%, but provider evidence shows Zurich fund charge 0.18% [P6] and Zurich illustration references charges of ¾% (0.75%) in policy info [P2], creating a 0.18% or 0.75% vs. 0.93% discrepancy.
      - Report states EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge 0.52%, confirmed in [P1] as 0.52%, but no provider illustration for Standard Life is available to verify this or to support the claimed -0.08% impact and -£268.94 saving.
      - Report states overall comparison difference -0.24% pa and -£264, but the arithmetic from per-arrangement figures is not transparent: EA3 -£2.25 + EA4 -£506.85 + EA5 -£268.94 = -£778.04, not -£264. The implied total fund value for -0.24% to equal -£264 would be £110,000, but the sum of current values is £116,998.47 [P1 table].
      - Report comparison table quotes state EA3 impact -0.06%, EA4 impact -0.49%, EA5 impact -0.08%, but the provenance quote for EA5 in the canonical model states '0.18% / £186.19 / 0.26% / £268.94', which shows 0.26% (not -0.08%) and £268.94 (not -£268.94 as an impact).
      - Report states recommended Aviva plan charges 0.44% total (platform 0.21% + investment 0.22% [P3]), but no provider documentation is supplied confirming Aviva charges; the Aviva platform personal pension is not documented in the evidence pack.
      - Critical yield analysis [P21] projects new solution at mid-growth as £115,000 vs. existing £127,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%, and states critical yield 4.8% is required. This contradicts the cost comparison assertion that the recommended plan is 0.24% cheaper when cumulative projections show substantial underperformance requiring material growth to recover.
      - Charge arithmetic mismatch: EA4 0.93% of £103,439.24 = £961.78, reported as £961.98 (difference £0.20, immaterial rounding); EA5 0.52% of £35,805.77 would equal £186.19 [derived], but the existing arrangement value for EA5 (Standard Life) is stated as £3,002 in [P1], implying the £35,805.77 figure is incorrect or EA5's fund value is misstated.
      - Report identifies five existing arrangements totalling £116,998.47, but the cost comparison appears to aggregate only three (EA3, EA4, EA5), omitting EA1 (People's Pension £6,601.55) and EA2 (Scottish Widows £211.32). No explanation is given for why only three are included in the impact comparison.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Aviva personal pension platform charges and fund charges—no provider documentation supplied to corroborate the 0.44% total (0.21% platform, 0.22% investment management) stated in [P3]., Standard Life (EA5) current charge basis—the report states 0.52%, which appears in [P1], but no provider illustration or key features document is in the evidence pack to confirm this rate or the claimed -0.08% improvement., Zurich (EA4) charge reconciliation—provider documentation shows 0.18% fund charge [P6] or ¾% (0.75%) [P2], but report asserts 0.93%, creating a 0.18%-to-0.93% or 0.75%-to-0.93% gap., Adviser fee basis in comparison—the report states 3% initial fee (£3,305.55) and mentions it in critical yield, but the cost comparison table does not clearly state whether adviser fees are included or excluded in the -0.24% differential., Aggregation methodology—no explanation of why five arrangements exist but only three are included in per-arrangement cost impact analysis, and how the aggregate -0.24% is derived from those three., Basis of fund values used for charge calculation—the report uses £3,744.36 (Aviva current value) and £103,439.24 (Zurich current value) from [P1], but recomputation against stated percentages does not reconcile to stated monetary charges in all cases.
    The cost comparison cannot be reliably performed because of internal arithmetic inconsistencies and missing provider evidence. First, internal contradiction: the per-arrangement savings (EA3 -£2.25, EA4 -£506.85, EA5 -£268.94) sum to -£778.04, not the reported aggregate -£264. This indicates either the per-arrangement figures or the aggregate is incorrect, or a fund value used for calculation has not been disclosed. Second, charge source mismatch: Zurich (EA4) is reported as 0.93% but provider documentation [P6] shows 0.18% and [P2] suggests ¾% (0.75%), a material discrepancy. Third, missing documentation: no Aviva personal pension key features or illustration document is supplied to verify the 0.44% claimed for the recommended plan. Without this, the comparison basis cannot be validated. Fourth, adviser fees treatment: the comparison table appears to exclude adviser fees, but the critical yield analysis includes them. The note that 'charges quoted exclude transaction costs' [P3] is transparent, but the exclusion of adviser fees from the cost comparison table while including them in critical yield creates two different cost universes, making the headline -0.24% cheaper claim potentially misleading. Fifth, arrangement selection: only three of five existing arrangements are included in the per-arrangement table; the reason why People's Pension (EA1) and Scottish Widows (EA2) are omitted is not stated. Sixth, the canonical model provenance quote for EA5 states '0.26% / £268.94', suggesting a cost, not a saving; the extraction may have parsed a directional sign incorrectly. Seventh, critical yield projections [P21] show the new solution at £115,000 vs. existing £127,000 at mid-growth, a 9.4% underperformance, which is inconsistent with the assertion that the plan is 0.24% cheaper—the cost advantage is outweighed by projected underperformance requiring a 4.8% critical yield. The report does connect the recommendation to consolidation and retirement readiness benefits in [P20] and [P18], but these are not set against the cost differential or reconciled with the underperformance shown in projections. The rule that a comparison on a different basis (with/without adviser fees) is legitimate applies here: the comparison table excludes adviser fees, but the suitability report must then separately disclose the impact of adviser fees on overall value. The 3% fee is disclosed [P23] and critical yield includes it [P21], so this is partially met, but the headline cost comparison message (-0.24% cheaper) does not account for the £3,305.55 initial fee or ongoing adviser remuneration, creating a material gap between the simple cost message and the full cost picture.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts seven main benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs - 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension, meaning more of your savings remain invested for growth rather than being lost to charges.' (2) Lifestyling Risk - funds automatically reduce risk as retirement approaches, moving outside agreed risk range. (3) Greater Investment Choice - wider range of options better suited to risk profile and retirement goals. (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation - new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly. (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments - ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required. (6) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility - more flexible retirement options and tax-efficient withdrawal strategies. (7) Provider Strength and Fund Choice - Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM; over 6,900 funds available. The report states 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings.' The recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, 0.29% charge), giving a blended charge of 0.235%. An initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 is deducted from the new solution. An ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00% is stated to be applied. The report references a critical yield and projected values at retirement showing effects of switching.
    File:     The evidence contains multiple charge discrepancies and contradictions. The Switch Charges comparison table [P14] shows: for existing pension at mid-growth (2.95%), projected value £127,000; for new solution, projected value £125,000, representing a £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. The critical yield required for the new solution is 0.3% at low growth and 3.2% at mid growth. At high growth (5.88%), the existing pension projects £150,000 versus new solution £147,000, a £3,000 or 2.0% decrease. The fund factsheets [P3, P5, P7] show performance data for the recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc: 5-year 44.73%; HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C: 5-year 40.06%). The report's performance chart [P1, P9] shows labels with apparent inconsistencies: first section labels 'A - Aviva' and 'D - Zurich', but second volatility section labels show 'D - Aviva' and 'A - Zurich', suggesting reversed labelling. The existing Aviva holding (My Future Focus) shows 15.58% 5-year cumulative return [P1], while the Zurich Managed fund shows 44.32% [P1, P9]. The derived figures show: OngoingCharge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71, which matches neither arrangement value nor the total £116,998.47. The annual charge difference of £268.94 at 0.26% implies Zurich's current value of £103,438.46. Research literature [P3, P12] confirms Aviva as recommended provider. The pension switch report [P14] explicitly states the effect of switching results in lower projected outcomes: at mid-growth the new solution underperforms by £2,000 (1.6%), at high growth by £3,000 (2.0%).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'Lower Costs' and that the receiving scheme has 'lower overall cost structure', but the pension switch report [P14] shows projected values at retirement are LOWER for the new solution at all growth rates: £2,000 decrease (1.6%) at mid-growth and £3,000 decrease (2.0%) at high-growth
      - Report claims recommended Aviva funds have 'demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings', but the performance chart [P1, P9] shows My Future Focus (existing Aviva) at 15.58% cumulative return vs recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio at approximately 37.25% (3-year) or 42.40% (per volatility chart), making this statement internally consistent but the comparison with Zurich (44.32%) shows recommended funds do NOT outperform existing Zurich holdings
      - Performance chart labelling inconsistency: first chart labels show 'A - Aviva / D - Zurich' but volatility chart labels show 'D - Aviva / A - Zurich', reversing the key
      - Fund values used in charge calculations do not reconcile: OngoingCharge £231.39 at 0.21% implies £110,185.71 but reported total is £116,998.47; discrepancy of £6,812.76
      - Report states ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% is included in projections [P14], but this charge is substantially higher than typical industry standards and the benefit of this cost to the client is not explained in relation to improved outcomes, particularly where projected values are lower
      - Pension switch report [P14] shows critical yield requirements (0.3% to 6.2% depending on growth scenario) but the report does not explain whether these yields are achievable given the fund choices and market conditions
      - Report asserts 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit, but evidence shows existing arrangements already access multiple fund choices (50 funds available in existing Stakeholder/Personal plans per [P10])
      - Report cites provider strength and 6,900+ fund availability but does not reconcile how this benefits THIS client specifically given the client's stated objective of simplicity ('You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' [P8])
    The critical issue is internal contradiction between the report's assertion that the recommendation offers 'Lower Costs' and the evidence in the pension switch analysis itself. The report's own comparison table [P14] demonstrates that at all modelled growth rates, the new solution produces LOWER projected retirement values than remaining in existing arrangements. At mid-growth (2.95%), the new solution underperforms by £2,000 (1.6%); at high growth (5.88%), by £3,000 (2.0%). This directly contradicts the 'Lower Costs' benefit assertion. The report acknowledges in the same switch analysis that an initial adviser charge of £3,305 is deducted and an ongoing 1.00% annual adviser charge applies, yet does not quantify or justify how these material additional costs (1.00% per annum is materially higher than the product charges of 0.18-0.29%) result in net benefit. The performance comparison claim that recommended Aviva funds have 'demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' is supported (My Future Focus 15.58% vs Aviva Risk 5 portfolio ~37-42%), but the report does NOT disclose that the existing Zurich holdings (44.32%) significantly outperform the recommended allocation. The report frames this by noting Zurich funds are 'higher-risk' and the recommended funds have 'reduced volatility', but does not adequately explain why a client with Risk Rating 5 (highest) should accept lower projected outcomes and lower historical performance. The benefit statements (Lifestyling, Investment Choice, Flexibility, Monitoring) are generic and not client-specific in their justification, particularly given the client's stated objective of simplicity. The charge calculations contain mathematical inconsistencies: the derived OngoingCharge figures do not reconcile with stated arrangement values. The fundamental fairness claim—that benefits justify costs—fails because the report's own projections show costs exceed benefits in terms of retirement outcome. The guard permits rejection of assertions regarding outperformance without supporting performance analysis; here the performance data exists but contradicts the assertion, and the adviser cost is not separately justified by superior outcomes.
