  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts a required outperformance of 4.24% of fund value per annum to justify the recommendation, and states a charge difference of -0.24% of fund value per annum, indicating the recommended solution is cheaper overall.
    File:     No supporting documents are provided to evidence the basis for the required outperformance figure, the benefits justifying that threshold, or the charge comparison calculation. This requirement compares the report against itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The required outperformance of 4.24% pa is stated but the report provides no explanation of what benefits are expected to justify this threshold
      - The charge difference of -0.24% pa suggests cost improvement, but the internal inconsistencies noted in the extraction (conflicting charge figures for Aviva at -0.06% vs 0.50% existing and 0.44% recommended, and Standard Life at 0.08% vs +0.26%) create doubt about the accuracy of this comparison
      - The derived figure of £268.94 annual charge difference at 0.26% implies a fund value of £103,438.46 (Zurich's current value), yet the -0.24% figure appears to apply to a different or aggregated fund value that is not clearly identified
    The report makes two quantitative assertions about cost and value: a required outperformance of 4.24% pa and a net charge improvement of -0.24% pa. The method 'RangeMatch' and guard state that higher cost is not automatically poor value and the comparison should test stated benefits. However, the report does not articulate what specific benefits justify the 4.24% outperformance threshold or explain how those benefits were assessed. The charge comparison of -0.24% is presented without reconciliation against the multiple conflicting charge figures noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies. The Standard Life arrangement shows a stated savings of 0.08% in text but a +0.26% impact in the switch charges table—a material contradiction the report does not resolve. The derived figures confirm that ongoing charge calculations are arithmetically inconsistent with stated fund values, undermining confidence in the -0.24% aggregated comparison. The report's assertion that costs are justified by benefits cannot be tested because: (1) the benefits are not articulated; (2) the charge figures are internally inconsistent; (3) no alternative costs or benefit comparisons are provided. The charge reduction of 0.24% pa, if accurate, is modest; a client would need to rely on the articulated benefits to assess whether the recommendation is good value. Those benefits are absent from the evidence.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends switching from three existing pensions (Aviva plan, Zurich pension, Standard Life pension) to a new solution on Aviva Platform. The report states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' for the Aviva and Zurich switches. For Standard Life, it states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. The report presents charge comparisons showing existing annual recurring charges versus recommended charges, with impact figures in both percentage and monetary terms. For Aviva switch: existing 0.50%, recommended 0.44%, impact -0.06%. For Standard Life: existing 0.59%, recommended 0.44%, impact stated as -0.15% but the table shows +0.26%. For Zurich: existing 0.93%, recommended 0.44%, impact -0.49%. The new solution charges are stated as Platform 0.21% (£231.39) plus Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81).
    File:     The pension switch report [P1] shows existing pension value £110,185.60 versus new solution day one value £106,880.60, resulting in immediate shortfall of £3,305.00. Charge comparison table [P2] shows: Aviva existing 0.50% (£3,744.36 implied), new solution 0.6% (£3,430), Zurich 0.29% (£103,438.71 implied), Standard Life 0.59% (implied from context). The switch report projects maturity values at age 75: for Zurich comparison [P2], existing pension £102,000 (low growth) versus new solution £100,000, showing £2,000 or 2.0% decrease at low growth; at mid-growth (2.95%), existing £115,000 versus new £113,000, showing £2,000 or 1.7% decrease. The report notes transaction costs are excluded [P8] as 'variable and subject to change based on factors such as market conditions'. File notes [P18] indicate alternatives were researched: Scottish Widows discounted for 'no servicing and reduced flexibility'; Aviva alternative fund discounted as 'only mixed asset fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR'; Zurich alternative discounted because funds 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally'; Standard Life discounted as 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'. No cost comparison tables are provided for rejected alternatives in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life charge impact stated as -0.15% in text but table shows +0.26%
      - Aviva existing charge shown as 0.50% in switch comparison but calculated at 0.59% in retention scenario [P2]
      - New solution charge stated as 0.6% in some tables [P2, P5] but 0.44% in suitability report [P8]
      - Projected maturity values show lower outcomes for new solution at low and mid-growth rates [P2] despite cost advantage claims
      - Immediate shortfall of £3,305.00 on day one following switch [P1] not addressed in benefits narrative
      - File notes indicate Standard Life funds are 'more expensive' than Aviva but monetary comparison not provided in evidence
      - No comparative cost analysis provided for rejected alternatives (Scottish Widows, internal Aviva switch, internal Zurich switch)
    The report asserts that alternatives provide better cost or benefit, but the evidence reveals material inconsistencies and incomplete comparisons. First, the internal charge inconsistency: Standard Life is described as 'more expensive' but the impact figure contradicts this—text claims 0.08% more expensive yet the table shows +0.26% impact. Second, the maturity value projections at [P2] demonstrate that switching to the new solution produces lower projected outcomes at both low growth (£2,000 or 2.0% decrease for Zurich comparison) and mid-growth rates (£2,000 or 1.7% decrease), which directly contradicts the 'lower costs lead to better outcomes' narrative. The new solution also creates an immediate £3,305 shortfall on day one [P1], which is not reconciled with the long-term benefit claims. Third, the report states the new solution is 0.44% but [P2] shows it as 0.6% in the charge comparison tables—a material inconsistency. Fourth, file notes [P18] show three alternatives were researched and rejected, but the evidence provides no cost comparison data for two of them (Scottish Widows and internal fund switches), only qualitative reasons (servicing, flexibility, fund availability). The file notes state Standard Life funds are 'more expensive' but no numerical comparison is provided to support this. The guard permits rejection of cheaper alternatives for non-cost reasons (fund range, access, servicing), and this applies here—but the cost justification is incomplete and internally contradictory. The charge comparisons lack aggregation across all cost layers, transaction costs are explicitly excluded, and ongoing adviser fees are not quantified beyond the platform facilitation reference. Without complete, consistent comparative cost data and reconciliation of the contradictory projections, the claim that alternatives provide better benefit cannot be fully supported.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts six RIY figures across three growth rate bases (Low, Mid, High) for both Existing and Recommended arrangements. For Existing: 0.3% at all three growth bases (Low, Mid, High). For Recommended: 2.1% (Low), 2.2% (Mid), 2.3% (High). Critical yields for Recommended are 1.9% (Low), 4.8% (Mid), 7.8% (High) per annum. Projected values at age 75 for Recommended: £97,600 (Low), £115,000 (Mid), £135,000 (High). Effect of switching: £10,400 or 9.6% decrease (Low), £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (Mid), £15,000 or 10.0% decrease (High). All figures stated as at page 18 of the suitability report, with growth rates of 0.00%, 2.95%, and 5.88% respectively.
    File:     Passage [P2] (pension switch report) presents an identical table structure with the same layout and growth rate bases. It shows: Existing RIY 0.3% at all three growth bases; Recommended RIY 2.1% (Low), 2.2% (Mid), 2.3% (High). Projected values at age 75: Recommended £97,600 (Low), £115,000 (Mid), £135,000 (High). Critical yields: 1.9% (Low), 4.8% (Mid), 7.8% (High). Effect of switching: £10,400 or 9.6% decrease (Low), £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (Mid), £15,000 or 10.0% decrease (High). Passage [P1] presents a different version of the same comparison, showing: Existing projected value £108,000 (Low), £127,000 (Mid), £150,000 (High); Recommended projected value £106,000 (Low), £125,000 (Mid), £147,000 (High); Recommended RIY 0.6% at all three growth bases; Critical yields 0.3% (Low), 3.2% (Mid), 6.0% (High); Effect of switching £2,000 or 1.9% decrease (Low), £2,000 or 1.6% decrease (Mid), £2,000 or 1.4% decrease (High). Passage [P17] (suitability report itself, category [I]) reproduces the [P2] figures exactly.
    Discrepancies:
      - Projected values at Low growth rate: Report [P17]/[P2] shows Existing £108,000 vs Recommended £97,600; Report [P1] shows Existing £108,000 vs Recommended £106,000 (£9,600 difference between [P1] and [P2]).
      - Projected values at Mid growth rate: Report [P17]/[P2] shows Existing £127,000 vs Recommended £115,000; Report [P1] shows Existing £127,000 vs Recommended £125,000 (£10,000 difference).
      - Projected values at High growth rate: Report [P17]/[P2] shows Existing £150,000 vs Recommended £135,000; Report [P1] shows Existing £150,000 vs Recommended £147,000 (£12,000 difference).
      - Recommended RIY: Report [P17]/[P2] shows 2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3% across Low, Mid, High; Report [P1] shows 0.6% at all three growth bases (1.5 percentage point difference at Low and Mid, 1.7 at High).
      - Critical yields: Report [P17]/[P2] shows 1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%; Report [P1] shows 0.3%, 3.2%, 6.0% (1.6, 1.6, and 1.8 percentage point differences respectively).
      - Effect of switching Low: Report [P17]/[P2] shows £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; Report [P1] shows £2,000 or 1.9% decrease (£8,400 difference, 7.7 percentage point difference).
      - Effect of switching Mid: Report [P17]/[P2] shows £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; Report [P1] shows £2,000 or 1.6% decrease (£10,000 difference, 7.8 percentage point difference).
      - Effect of switching High: Report [P17]/[P2] shows £15,000 or 10.0% decrease; Report [P1] shows £2,000 or 1.4% decrease (£13,000 difference, 8.6 percentage point difference).
    Two materially different versions of the reduction in yield, critical yield and projected value comparison exist within the evidence. Passage [P2] is a pension switch report that matches [P17], which is extracted from the suitability report itself (category [I]). Passage [P1] is also categorised as [G] (pension switch report) but presents substantially different figures. The differences are not attributable to rounding, different growth rate prescriptions, or different retirement ages — all three are stated consistently. The critical divergence is systematic: [P1] shows recommended projected values that are £9,600 to £13,000 higher than [P2] at each growth basis, recommended RIY approximately 1.5-1.7 percentage points lower, critical yields 1.6-1.8 percentage points lower, and switching costs £8,400 to £13,000 lower in absolute terms. [P2] and [P17] are aligned (the latter being extracted from the former). The suitability report asserts the figures from [P2]/[P17]. However, [P1] is also titled 'Pension switch report - updated.md' and appears to be a supporting document within the case file. The presence of two conflicting switch analyses in the evidence — one apparently 'updated' and one apparently superseded — creates material inconsistency. The [P1] figures would support a significantly less costly switch recommendation (lower RIY impact, lower critical yield requirement, smaller value loss), while [P2]/[P17] figures show materially worse switching economics. There is no annotation in the case materials indicating which is current, whether one corrects the other, or the reason for the divergence. The arithmetic on both is internally consistent within each version; the problem is that two different versions coexist. This is a data consistency problem: the report relies on one set of figures ([P2]), but the file contains another ([P1]) that contradicts them materially. A client reviewing both documents would see a fundamental discrepancy in whether the recommendation is financially justified. The canonical model correctly records what [P17] states; the issue is that another comparator table in the file says something substantially different, and the report does not acknowledge or explain this conflict.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.0% per annum. The report states this service includes: (1) Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable for you; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs. The report specifies the review frequency is unspecified in the canonical model. The report states 'Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year' and 'This fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly.'
    File:     The client agreement [P1] specifies 'ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' The client agreement [P8] describes the on-going advice and review service as including: 'Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include the following as part of this service; bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk. Statements and Performance Reports twice per year. Cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial. Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members for their ISA or pension set up on top of up to three hours advice for your children. Regular market updates. Quarterly e-magazine. On-line access for portfolio valuations. Access to your adviser should you need us in the meantime.' The client agreement [P8] states 'For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum).' The agreement [P2] confirms 'Should you choose to proceed with our recommendations, we will provide you with our Ongoing advice and review service, as described in the 'Important Information about our Services' document we have supplied to you, until either you, or we, consider this service to be no longer appropriate for your needs.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report does not specify a review frequency, whereas the client agreement states 'Review meeting at least once a year'
      - The suitability report lists five service components for the ongoing charge, while the client agreement specifies a more granular list including statements twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit provisions, quarterly e-magazine, online access, and specific service components
      - The suitability report does not mention the minimum fee of £100 per annum mentioned in the client agreement [P1] and [P8]
      - The suitability report does not disclose several service elements listed in the client agreement such as 'Statements and Performance Reports twice per year', 'Quarterly e-magazine', or 'Family benefit' provisions
    The suitability report asserts an ongoing annual charge of 1% and describes a service proposition comprising five components. The client agreement [P1, P8] corroborates the 1% charge rate and minimum fee of £100. However, the service proposition disclosed in the suitability report is less comprehensive than what is documented in the client agreement. The suitability report omits disclosure of: (1) the minimum fee of £100; (2) the stated review frequency of 'at least once a year' or annual review meeting; (3) statements and performance reports twice per year; (4) cash flow analysis when relevant; (5) family benefit provisions; (6) quarterly e-magazine; (7) online access for portfolio valuations. The report states the service includes items that are present in the agreement (risk review, portfolio suitability review, market updates, ad-hoc advice, holistic planning), but these are presented as the complete picture when the agreement contains materially more detail about what the service includes. The suitability report's disclosure of the service proposition is therefore incomplete. The service as described in the client agreement [P8] is more extensive than what the suitability report conveys to the client. Critically, where the client agreement specifies 'at least once a year' review meetings and twice-yearly statements, the suitability report provides no review frequency and no mention of statements. This represents a disclosure shortfall: the report should align with or explicitly reference the client agreement, or explain why certain elements are not included in the stated service. The charge amount (1%) is consistent, but the value proposition attached to it is incompletely disclosed in the report.

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P8)

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts eight benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs - 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension, meaning more of your savings remain invested for growth rather than being lost to charges.' (2) Lifestyling - existing funds reduce risk approaching retirement, moving outside agreed risk range, not recommended. (3) Greater Investment Choice - new scheme offers wider range of investment options, more tailored strategy, better suits risk profile and retirement goals. (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation - new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support. (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments - adviser will actively monitor and adjust investments as required. (6) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility - receiving scheme provides more flexible retirement options, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies. (7) Financial Strength - Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P, with £407bn AUM. (8) Choice - Over 6,900 funds and leading managers. The report also asserts two cost justifications: (1) Performance - 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings.' (2) Achievable Critical Yield - 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.'
    File:     The evidence pack contains product literature (Key Features, Policy Information, KIIDs, Terms and Conditions) for Aviva and existing arrangements. P4 [H] confirms Aviva aims to give flexibility to control retirement pension pot investment and manage benefits in retirement through multiple options (lump sum, drawdown, annuity). P8 [H] details flexi-access drawdown, single drawdown, self-select phased income drawdown, and capped drawdown options. P14 [E] confirms 24/7 MyAviva access and notes 'Pensions together in one place with Aviva could save money for your client and time and admin costs'. P19 [I] shows existing arrangements table with number of funds available: People's Pension 7, Scottish Widows 29, Aviva 80+, Zurich 60+, Standard Life 300. P5 [G] research table confirms Aviva Pension Portfolio offers FAD (flexi access drawdown) and full/partial UFPLS, matching the report's flexibility claims. P6 [E] confirms People's Pension offers 3 investment profiles (balanced, cautious, adventurous). No evidence is located in the pack substantiating: (1) that Aviva's overall cost structure is lower than the existing pension arrangements in aggregate; (2) that the recommended Aviva funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings; (3) that a 4.8% annual growth rate is achievable based on past performance of the recommended funds; (4) that adviser charging facilitation is a benefit specific to Aviva or material to the recommendation; (5) that ongoing monitoring and adjustments justify cost differences; (6) comparison of Aviva's financial strength or fund choice against alternatives the client was considering.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' but derived figures show: existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47; OngoingCharge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71; OngoingCharge £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45; NP1 total ongoing £484.81 at 0.44% implies £110,184.09; no aggregated all-in cost comparison table in evidence to support the 'lower overall cost structure' claim.
      - Report states recommended Aviva funds 'have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' but no performance comparison data is provided in evidence; P19 [I] shows existing Aviva holding (SP50163762) but no historical performance figures or comparison charts for that specific holding against recommended HSBC Global Strategy Balanced or CT Universal MAP funds are present.
      - Report asserts 4.8% annual growth required to match existing plan value is 'achievable' based on 'past performance of your recommended funds' but no historical performance data, return tables, or fund performance documentation for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc or CT Universal MAP Balanced C is provided in the evidence pack.
      - Report lists 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' as a benefit but evidence (P4, P14) shows adviser charging is mentioned as a feature of Aviva's Terms and Conditions, not as a differentiating benefit; no evidence shows existing arrangements prohibit adviser charging or that this is a material benefit justifying cost.
      - Report claims 'Greater Investment Choice' with 80+ funds available at Aviva, but P19 [I] shows Standard Life offers 300 funds, Zurich 60+, and Scottish Widows 29; Aviva does not offer the greatest choice among existing options, contradicting the positioning of this as a benefit justifying a switch.
      - Report states Enhanced Retirement Flexibility as a benefit, but P8 [H] and P4 [H] show Aviva's retirement options (FAD, UFPLS, annuity, capped drawdown) are standard across the industry; P5 [G] confirms People's Pension also offers FAD and UFPLS; no evidence shows these options are unique to Aviva or unavailable on existing arrangements.
      - No cost reconciliation provided between stated charges and actual fund values; derived charge arithmetic shows inconsistency: EA3 existing annual charge £18.72 at 0.5% matches Aviva's £3,744 value; EA4 £961.98 at 0.93% matches Zurich's £103,438.71; but aggregate values do not reconcile to a single 'overall cost structure' for comparison.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Historical performance data for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc funds, Historical performance data for existing Aviva SP50163762 holdings for direct comparison, Aggregated all-in cost comparison between existing arrangements (five pensions totalling £116,998.47) and recommended Aviva solution, including adviser fees, platform charges, fund charges, and transaction costs, Evidence that 4.8% annual growth rate is achievable based on documented past performance of recommended funds, Specification of adviser fee structure and how it is deducted from Aviva plan versus existing arrangements, Evidence supporting claim that Aviva's cost structure is lower than existing pensions when weighted by current fund values, Documentation of specific features unique to Aviva that justify higher costs if any
    The report asserts eight benefits and two cost justifications to support the recommendation to switch to Aviva. However, the comparison cannot be fully performed because critical evidentiary inputs are absent from the pack. The 'Lower Costs' benefit is the primary assertion but no aggregated cost comparison table or all-in cost illustration is present. Derived figures from the canonical model show charge percentages but these do not reconcile to a clear demonstration that the receiving scheme has a 'lower overall cost structure' — the report's own calculations show adviser fees of £484.81 at 0.44% on the recommended solution, implying a fund value of £110,184.09, but this conflicts with the stated transfer value of £110,185 and multiple other calculated values in the extraction metadata. The 'Lifestyling' benefit is supported by P2 [G] which confirms the client's existing Zurich pension invests in a lifestyle strategy, and P21 [I] confirms the report identifies that Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds. This benefit is thus supported. The 'Greater Investment Choice' benefit is contradicted by evidence: P19 [I] shows Standard Life offers 300 funds (not 80+ as Aviva), so this is not a genuine benefit of switching from all existing options. The 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' benefit describes standard market options (drawdown, lump sum, annuity) confirmed across all providers in P4, P5, P8, and P14; this is not specific to Aviva. The 'Financial Strength' and 'Choice' (6,900 funds) benefits are generic marketing claims not supported by comparisons to existing providers in the evidence. The 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' and 'Ongoing Monitoring' benefits lack evidence that these are unique to Aviva, that they justify cost increases, or that they represent something the client was not receiving before. The 'Performance Outlook' justification (stronger performance of recommended funds vs existing Aviva holdings) cannot be verified because no historical performance data for either the existing SP50163762 or recommended funds is in the evidence pack. The 'Achievable Critical Yield' justification (4.8% annual growth required) is stated to be achievable based on past performance, but no performance data is provided to support this assertion. The guards require that (1) a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify extra cost — this applies to Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, which is standard across plans; (2) consolidation convenience is a real benefit but not a financial one — the report does position consolidation as an outcome but primarily justifies on cost and flexibility, both of which are unsupported or contradicted; (3) a benefit the client will never use does not support cost — no evidence indicates whether the client will use adviser charging or ongoing monitoring. The requirement specifies that benefits must be 'supported by and consistent with the evidence provided.' Multiple benefits fail this test: Greater Investment Choice is inconsistent (Standard Life offers 300 funds, not fewer); Enhanced Retirement Flexibility is unsupported as a differentiator (standard across all providers); Adviser Charging Facilitation and Ongoing Monitoring are unsupported as cost justifications; Performance Outlook is unsupported (no data); Achievable Critical Yield is unsupported (no data). The Lower Costs assertion, the primary benefit, cannot be verified because no all-in cost comparison is provided and the derived figures show internal inconsistencies. The report does not disclose a comprehensive cost comparison table or reconciliation to support the central claim of lower costs.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended solution is cheaper than existing arrangements by 0.24% of fund value pa. It provides projected values at age 75 under three growth scenarios (Low 0%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88%), with corresponding reduction in yield (RIY) and critical yield figures. At mid-growth (2.95%), the new solution projects £115,000 vs existing £127,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. The report states the new solution would need to outperform the current plan by £4,672 in the first year and requires a critical yield of 4.8% pa to match the existing plan's value.
    File:     The pension switch report [P10] provides identical projected values and critical yield figures to the suitability report. It shows at mid-growth (2.95%): existing pension projects £127,000, new solution £115,000 (effect of switching £12,000 or 9.4% decrease). Critical yield is stated as 4.8%. The reduction in yield for the new solution is stated as 2.2% vs 0.3% for existing. [P3] and [P5] show comparable tables with similar critical yield and projected value decrements across different pension switching scenarios. [P1] shows the new solution day one value is £106,880.60 vs existing £110,185.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00 (the initial adviser charge). The switch report documents ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% pa included in projections [P3].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims recommended charges are 0.24% cheaper (RecommendedCheaper, differencePercentage -0.24), but the arithmetic check found this figure is not matched in any passage. The nearest figure found is 0%, which is 100% away from -0.24%.
      - The report states required outperformance of 4.24% pa (£4,672), but the arithmetic check found this figure is not matched. The nearest is 4 in [P1], only 5.7% away, suggesting derivation from fund value assumption.
      - The report's projected values and critical yields are internally consistent with [P10] and other switch report tables, but the cost comparison basis (the -0.24% figure) is not corroborated in the evidence. The switch report does not state an overall charge difference percentage; it presents individual product charges.
      - The suitability report states RIY percentages of 0.3%, 2.1%, and 2.2% but the arithmetic checks show these are not matched in passages. [P10] states RIY for new solution as 2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3% (matching the pattern) but the 0.3% for existing is not located in the evidence.
    The report asserts charges are 0.24% cheaper but provides projected outcomes showing significantly lower values for the new solution (£12,000 or 9.4% worse at mid-growth). This is internally consistent with including ongoing adviser charges of 1.00% pa in the new solution projections [P3]. The critical yield requirement of 4.8% pa is properly documented and reflects the higher cost burden of the new solution (including adviser fees) vs the existing arrangements which carry no adviser fees. The evidence [P10] corroborates the projected values and critical yields exactly. The cost comparison direction (RecommendedCheaper, -0.24%) cannot be verified against the evidence. The switch report [P1, P2, P5, P10] does not state an aggregate charge difference of -0.24%. Individual product charges are disclosed (e.g., 0.59%-0.6% for recommended vs varied existing), but no single -0.24% pa figure appears. The arithmetic check indicates the -0.24% figure does not reconcile with any fund value stated in the case. The required outperformance of £4,672 (4.24% pa) also cannot be located in the evidence; [P16] states 'need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year' but expresses this in monetary terms, not percentage terms, and derives it from unspecified assumptions about fund value. The guard permitting required outperformance under 0.5% pa to be treated as noise does not apply: 4.24% is material. The projections themselves are sound and consistently show the new solution underperforming the existing when subject to the same growth rate, which is correct given the adviser fees and higher product/platform charges included in the new solution. The evidence supports that switching to the recommended solution carries materially higher costs (including 1.00% ongoing adviser fees not present on existing plans) and produces lower projected outcomes unless the new solution outperforms at the critical yield rate. This is properly disclosed in the switch report tables but the aggregate cost comparison figure of -0.24% cheaper is unsupported.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report does not quantify the projected loss scenario from switching: the critical yield analysis in the Appendix (referenced in extraction) shows existing pension projected value at 75 of £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4% at mid-growth rate, but this material disadvantage is not stated in the main body where benefits are prominent. Report states ongoing service cost of 'circa £1,101.85 for the first year' but derived figures show OngoingCharge arithmetic produces conflicting fund values (£110,185.71, £115,195.45, £110,184.09) suggesting inconsistency in how ongoing charges are calculated or presented. Report does not clearly state that disinvestment on switching includes an additional cost of the greater of £10 or 10% of disinvestment value, as detailed in the Terms and Conditions [P13]. 10 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies the following material disadvantages: (1) Lower performance: Zurich funds are higher-risk and more volatile; recommended Aviva funds have outperformed existing Aviva holdings but not Zurich funds. (2) Access delay: 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking. (3) Ongoing service cost: circa £1,101.85 for the first year. (4) Long-term care costs not modelled in cashflow chart. (5) Projection uncertainty: small variance in assumptions compounds over time. (6) Discretionary payments: all payments subject to Aviva's discretion. (7) Cancellation limitation: previous provider not obliged to take plan back. (8) Limited fund choice: no Multi-Asset funds, limited range. (9) Risk misalignment: current fund does not align with agreed risk rating. (10) Tax implications: small pot withdrawal rules — 25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate. (11) Market timing risk: market movements during transfer period could disadvantage client. (12) No performance guarantee: no guarantee new investment will outperform existing arrangement. The report also states: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' An initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) is charged.
    File:     The Key Features document [P4, P7, P13] discloses multiple risks: (1) Value may go down as well as up; could get back less invested. (2) Income drawdown can deplete pension fund if investments don't grow to compensate; high income withdrawal reduces funds for dependants or annuity. (3) Tax position changes by HMRC could alter income payments. (4) Means-tested benefits may be affected by income drawdown. (5) Currency risk on foreign investments. (6) Property investments take time to sell; value subject to valuation opinion. (7) Fixed term products may not be accessible until term end; may receive significantly less than invested. (8) Projections not guaranteed; actual performance depends on investment performance and charges. (9) Delay in switching/selling may occur due to poor market conditions. (10) Cancellation within period may not return full original payment if single payment. The Pension Switch Report [P1, P2] shows critical yield analysis and reduction in yield calculations for each arrangement. The Terms and Conditions [P6, P8, P12, P13, P21] detail charges structure (initial adviser charge, ongoing adviser charge, DIM charge, fund manager charges), switching mechanics, and that disinvestment includes additional amount of greater of £10 or 10% of disinvestment value. The cannonical model notes internal inconsistencies in the report: pension fund value stated variously as £110,000, £106,000, and £110,185.47; charges comparison shows conflicting figures; critical yield section shows at mid-growth rate (2.95%), new solution projects £115,000 vs existing £127,000 at age 75, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report does not quantify the projected loss scenario from switching: the critical yield analysis in the Appendix (referenced in extraction) shows existing pension projected value at 75 of £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4% at mid-growth rate, but this material disadvantage is not stated in the main body where benefits are prominent.
      - Report states ongoing service cost of 'circa £1,101.85 for the first year' but derived figures show OngoingCharge arithmetic produces conflicting fund values (£110,185.71, £115,195.45, £110,184.09) suggesting inconsistency in how ongoing charges are calculated or presented.
      - Report does not clearly state that disinvestment on switching includes an additional cost of the greater of £10 or 10% of disinvestment value, as detailed in the Terms and Conditions [P13].
      - Report does not disclose the specific reduction in yield (RIY) figures from the Pension Switch Report for each existing arrangement being switched, which are material to understanding the cost impact.
      - Report does not address the risk that, depending on market conditions, the switching process itself may cause delays up to one month as disclosed in Terms and Conditions [P9].
      - Report does not state that means-tested benefits may be affected by income drawdown as disclosed in Key Features [P4].
      - Report does not quantify tax consequences: while small pot withdrawal rules are mentioned, no illustration of actual tax liability at marginal rate is provided, and no worked example shows client impact.
    The report identifies a reasonable range of generic disadvantages and some product-specific risks. However, the most material disadvantage — the projected reduction in pension value at retirement — is buried in the Appendix's critical yield section and not stated prominently in the main narrative where benefits and recommendations are discussed. The canonical model confirms this: at mid-growth rate (2.95%), the new solution is projected at £115,000 vs existing £127,000 at age 75, representing a 9.4% decrease or £12,000 loss. COBS 9.4.7R requires material disadvantages to be explained fairly and clearly; relegating a loss scenario of this magnitude to an appendix while the Performance Assessment section [P20] prominently compares historical performance without highlighting the projected underperformance represents a disclosure shortfall. The report does state 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' but does not connect this generic warning to the client's specific projected outcome. Disinvestment costs (10% or £10 minimum) disclosed in Terms and Conditions [P13] are not reflected in the report's cost narrative. Tax consequences are mentioned generically (small pot rules) but the actual tax liability at the client's marginal rate is not calculated or illustrated. Access delays are disclosed (10-15 working days) but the potential for up to one-month delays under poor market conditions is not mentioned. The vulnerability overlay is not material here: no documented support needs, communication requirements or vulnerability characteristics in the file affect whether these disadvantages should have been disclosed. The report does disclose risks consistent with product literature (discretionary payments, cancellation limitations, projection uncertainty, no performance guarantee) and sources them appropriately from Key Features and Terms and Conditions. The inconsistencies in pension fund valuation (£110,000 vs £106,000 vs £110,185.47) and charge calculations noted in the extraction do not affect disadvantage disclosure but do affect confidence in the figures underpinning the loss calculation.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states multiple product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) Limited withdrawal flexibility if transferring all pensions into the recommended plan, stated as client-specific and linked to objective OBJ2; (2) Lifestyling risk drift — existing funds automatically reduce risk as retirement approaches, moving outside agreed risk range, stated as client-specific and linked to OBJ3; (3) Timing risk — market movements between realisation and purchase of new funds could disadvantage the client; (4) Timing opportunity — conversely, market falls could advantage the client; (5) No outperformance guarantee — new investment return not guaranteed to exceed existing arrangement; (6) General investment risk — unit prices and pension fund value can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of original capital especially in early years; (7) Existing Zurich funds volatility — higher-risk funds more susceptible to market volatility resulting in greater fluctuations. These risks appear on pages 6–8 and 12–13 of the suitability report.
    File:     The case file contains product literature (KIIDs, Key Features, Fund Factsheets, Terms and Conditions, Zurich Policy Info, and Risk Profile Report). The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio KIID (P1, P3, P9) states the fund is rated category 4 (medium fluctuations), notes 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' (P9), and lists material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, liquidity risk, emerging markets risk, leverage risk, and fixed interest securities risk (P3, P11). The Aviva Key Features (P7, P14) describe pension portfolio risks including that 'the value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up' and 'you could get back less than has been invested'. The Zurich Policy Info (P16, P17) details investment risks including property fund valuation delays up to 12 months, high-yield bond fund risks, emerging market risks, smaller company risks, and derivative risks. The fund literature emphasises that projections are not guaranteed (P4) and that income drawdown can deplete the fund if investments do not grow sufficiently (P4). However, no passage explicitly addresses the specific risk of 'limited withdrawal flexibility' if transferring all pensions into the recommended plan, nor do the passages corroborate the framing of lifestyling risk as moving the portfolio outside the client's agreed risk range.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts that transferring all pensions into the recommended plan would 'limit how you can withdraw funds' and make the client 'unable to access this pension flexibly' — no product literature in the file corroborates this claim or explains what withdrawal restrictions apply to the HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio or Aviva Pension Portfolio as the recommended solutions.
      - The report states the existing lifestyling funds 'automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement' resulting in portfolio moving 'outside of your agreed risk range' — the product literature does not reference lifestyling mechanisms in the existing arrangements nor confirm that automatic de-risking would breach the client's agreed risk profile of 5.
      - The timing risk and timing opportunity statements in the report are generic (not client-specific) and appear in the suitability report as hypothetical scenarios, but no product literature discusses or validates the assumption that such timing gaps would materially affect this client's switching transaction.
      - The report emphasises investment risk (unit prices can fall, no guarantee of return of original capital) — while the product literature (P7, P14) contains the generic statement 'the value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up', the report's phrasing 'especially in the early years' is not echoed in the product literature.
    The report clearly articulates product-specific risks relevant to the recommendation. Three of the seven risk topics are corroborated by the product literature: (1) General investment risk (unit price volatility, capital not guaranteed) is stated generically in Aviva Key Features (P7, P14); (2) Timing risk and timing opportunity are standard switching risks presented in suitability reports, though not uniquely validated against this case's specific products; (3) Zurich volatility is stated in the report with reference to 'higher-risk funds' and supported indirectly by Zurich Policy Info (P17) which notes funds with high equity exposure offer 'greater potential for higher returns than a fund investing in cash deposits, but with it comes greater fluctuations in value'. However, two material assertions lack evidentiary support: First, the claim that the recommended plan limits withdrawal flexibility and prevents flexible access is unsupported — neither the HSBC KIID nor the Aviva Key Features nor any other document on file explicitly restricts withdrawal flexibility or explains why the new plan would reduce it compared to the existing arrangements. This is a material disadvantage that, if true, should be evidenced. Second, the lifestyling risk assertion (that existing funds will move outside the agreed risk range) is not corroborated — the extraction data notes the client's agreed risk level as 5, but no evidence confirms that the existing lifestyling mechanism will cause the portfolio to breach this ceiling. The product literature (P5, P15) describes asset allocations that adjust with market movements but does not confirm a lifestyling auto de-risking mechanism would violate the agreed risk 5 profile. These two gaps represent material disadvantages or risks identified in the report's rationale but not supported by the case file. The report does disclose that 'there is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater' (stated twice, on pages 8 and 12), which is corroborated by generic principle across the product literature. The volatility of existing Zurich funds is mentioned (page 13) and is consistent with Zurich Policy Info (P17). The broader investment risk disclosures (capital at risk, no guarantee of return) appear in the product literature but somewhat scattered — Aviva Key Features (P14) states 'you could get back less than has been invested' and 'the value of your investment and retirement benefits depends on the performance of your chosen investments'; HSBC KIID (P1) states 'the value of investments and any income from them can go down as well as up'. The report does not relegate all risk disclosure to product literature; it articulates key risks in the body of the report (pages 6–8, 12–13) rather than only in an appendix. However, the two unsupported assertions (withdrawal flexibility loss and lifestyling risk breach) represent a disclosure shortfall on material disadvantages. The vulnerability overlay does not apply: no driver of vulnerability is recorded in the evidence that would materially affect the assessment of risk disclosure adequacy.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states six tax consequences: (1) Death benefits before age 75 are tax-free to beneficiaries; (2) Death benefits after age 75 are taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate; (3) Nominees' withdrawals are tax-free if death occurs before 75, taxed at marginal rate if after 75; (4) Client understands pension freedoms and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown; (5) Flexi Access Drawdown withdrawals have no upper limit but are taxed at marginal rate of Income Tax; (6) Personal contributions benefit from tax relief (£80 net becomes £100 gross). Additional tax implications stated: Small pots rules apply with 25% tax-free cash and 75% taxed at marginal rate; Transfer is direct and triggers no immediate tax liability; From 6 April 2027, unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits will be included in taxable estate for IHT; Current IHT liability is £0.
    File:     The Key Features document [P2] states: 'You can normally withdraw up to 25% of your pension fund as a tax-free lump sum. You may have to pay income tax on any income payments and lump sum payments you take.' It notes HMRC limits on tax-free benefits (lump sum allowance and lump sum & death benefit allowance), and that income tax is payable on benefits taken above these amounts. It confirms tax-free relief on contributions and advises consulting adviser for personal circumstances. The Key Features [P4] confirms: death before 75 results in tax-free payment to beneficiaries (unless lump sum & death benefit allowance exceeded); death after 75 results in benefits taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate, with lump sum charge either at beneficiary's income tax rate or 45% if paid to trust. It notes beneficiaries may receive income drawdown and that within two years of death there is a tax charge. The SIPP research report [P18] confirms Risk level 5 and investment selections. The Pension switch report [P11] provides projected maturity values and yield analyses.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'each withdrawal is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax' with 'no upper limit to the amount the individual can withdraw as an income' [P6], but does not explain that withdrawals above the personal allowance will be taxed, or reconcile this with the client's expected retirement income of £1,700 net monthly or their household expenditure of £1,700 monthly and current State Pension of approximately £460 weekly (roughly £2,000 monthly), which together may exceed the personal allowance and trigger significant tax liability on drawdown withdrawals.
      - The report discloses the future IHT inclusion rule from 6 April 2027 [P5, P9] but does not explain the material consequence of this change for this client: with estimated pension funds of £110,000+, upon death after 6 April 2027 these funds would now be included in the taxable estate, potentially triggering inheritance tax at 40% on amounts above the nil rate band (£325,000 per person). The report states current IHT liability is £0 but does not explain how the 2027 change alters this outlook.
      - The report confirms small pots encashment of the Scottish Widows pension (£211.32) under small pots rules with 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate [P10], but does not state the client's current marginal tax rate or estimate the net proceeds, making it impossible to verify whether the advice to encash is consistent with the client's tax position.
      - The report states the transfer 'will not trigger any immediate tax liability' but does not disclose the critical yield analysis showing that at mid-growth (2.95%), the new solution projects £115,000 vs existing £127,000 at age 75—a £12,000 (9.4%) decrease—which assumes adviser fees are charged on the new solution despite no existing adviser fees being disclosed on the existing plans [P9, P23]. This represents a material cost consequence not explained in tax terms.
    The report states multiple tax consequences of the recommendation and these are largely consistent with the product literature and regulatory framework evident in the file. The Key Features [P2, P4] corroborate the report's statements on death benefits taxation and the 25% tax-free lump sum rule. However, several material consequences are either missing, inadequately explained, or not reconciled with the client's personal circumstances: (1) The report does not establish the client's marginal tax rate (see CHK-001 G1.2 guard). Without this, statements that withdrawals are taxed at 'marginal rate' are generic legislative explanation rather than client-specific consequence. The client's expected retirement income of £1,700 net monthly from household earnings and State Pension suggests significant income already; the threshold at which Income Tax begins on drawdown is not explained. (2) The 6 April 2027 IHT inclusion rule is disclosed but its consequence for this client is not explained. The report states current IHT liability is £0 and notes the rule change but does not quantify or explain that pensions will now be included in the taxable estate, potentially creating a material IHT liability of up to 40% on the pension fund if the combined estate exceeds the nil rate band. (3) The small pots encashment of £211.32 is advised but the tax cost is not estimated. The report says 25% is tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate but does not state the marginal rate or projected net proceeds. (4) The critical yield analysis [P9, P23] shows the new solution produces £12,000 less (9.4% decrease) at age 75 than the existing solution at mid-growth, yet this cost consequence is presented only in investment terms and not reconciled with the tax and charge implications. The adviser fee of 3% (£3,305.55) is stated [P7] but its tax treatment (whether paid gross or net, whether it reduces the transfer value) is not explained. The guard permits generic legislative explanation to differ from client-specific consequence; here, the report mixes the two without clearly distinguishing them or establishing the client's tax band. The tax consequences stated are not contradicted by the evidence, but material consequences are omitted or inadequately explained to support suitability.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts one loss scenario illustrated: 'Immediate market crash of -30% combined with high inflation rate of 6%'. The report states: 'I have completed a stress test based on if an immediate market crash were to occur in combination with the increased inflation level. This showed that if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' This is located on page 15 under 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests' section.
    File:     The case file contains multiple risk disclosures and scenarios. P2 (Key Features) sets out generic pension and income drawdown risks including that 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up' and 'You could get back less than has been invested.' P3 contains a critical yield table showing three growth rate scenarios (0.00%, 2.95%, and 5.88%) with corresponding projected values and 'Effect of switching' figures showing losses of £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), and £15,000 (10.0%) respectively at age 75. P22 (from the report itself, category [I]) mentions three stress tests: high inflation of 6%, a market crash of -30% combined with high inflation, and longevity assumptions based on ONS life expectancy. The illustration in P10 and P15 shows charges reducing growth and notes 'The value of your pension fund can go down or up and may be worth less than has been paid in.' P11 notes that 'This forecast represents just one possible future outcome and actual results may differ materially from these projections.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report explicitly states one loss scenario (market crash of -30% with 6% inflation) but the supporting critical yield analysis in P3 shows three distinct loss scenarios across different growth rates (0%, 2.95%, 5.88%) with material reductions in projected fund value (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000). These alternative scenarios are not presented as loss scenarios in the report's risk section.
      - The report's stress test for -30% market crash concludes 'there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used' but does not quantify the absolute loss in fund value or illustrate the financial impact to the client, unlike the critical yield analysis which shows specific cash losses.
      - The critical yield section (P3) showing effect of switching losses is presented as a cashflow calculation metric rather than as a loss scenario illustration for client understanding, despite representing material downside outcomes.
    The comparison can be performed. The report asserts that it has illustrated loss scenarios, specifically naming the -30% market crash with 6% inflation stress test. However, the manner and prominence of presentation differ materially between the report's risk section and the underlying evidence. The stress test in the report (P22) concludes there would be 'no shortfall' but provides no quantification of the fund value loss itself to the client. The critical yield table in P3, which is derived from the report's own cashflow modelling and presented in the Appendix, contains multiple loss scenarios showing fund decreases of 9-10% depending on growth rate assumptions. The guard states that 'A stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one in the risk section.' This means the critical yield analysis showing switching effects (losses of £10,400-£15,000) constitutes loss scenarios. The report has illustrated loss scenarios, but they appear scattered across different sections with different framing: the main risk section emphasizes the conclusion that stress tests show 'no shortfall', while the appendix contains numerical loss illustrations in technical format. The risk section does not explicitly frame the critical yield losses as consumer-facing loss scenarios, and the main stress test narrative focuses on absence of shortfall rather than magnitude of potential loss. The evidence supports that loss scenarios exist in the file but their presentation to the client may not be prominent, clear or consumer-focused in the risk section.

This showed that if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used."

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model indicates that risk warnings and disadvantages are scattered throughout the report in multiple sections (Other Considerations of Switching, Fund recommendation, Performance Assessment) on pages 8, 12, and 13, rather than being grouped in a single dedicated section. However, the extraction notes that they appear prominently in the main body text rather than only in appendices. The report presents: (1) Material disadvantages: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' [P5]; (2) Loss scenarios with specific figures across three growth rates — low (£10,400 or 9.6% decrease at age 75), mid (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease), and high (£15,000 or 10.0% decrease) [P1]; (3) Critical Yield requirement of 4.8% per year at mid-growth rate to match existing plan value [P2]; (4) Reduction in Yield impact of 2.2% at mid-growth rate [P1]; (5) Adviser charges of 1% per annum and initial £3,305 charge [P8]; (6) Risk profile confirmation as Risk Rating 5, a Balanced investor approach [P7]; (7) Capacity for loss explanation [P7]; (8) Specific risks in Other Considerations section including stock-market movements during pending transactions [P6].
    File:     The pension switch report (evidence category [G]) contains substantially identical loss scenario figures and critical yield analysis. It presents the same three-rate projection table with identical figures: low growth (£10,400 or 9.6% decrease), mid growth (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease), and high growth (£15,000 or 10.0% decrease) [P11]. The Zurich-only switch scenario shows different figures: low (£2,000 or 2.0% decrease), mid (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease) [P9]. The report notes an immediate fund shortfall of £3,305 from initial adviser charge [P15]. It also explains 'Reduction in Yield is the impact of product, platform and fund charges on the investment growth rate' [P11]. However, the suitability report itself does not dedicate a single prominent section to consolidating all disadvantages, risks and loss scenarios — they appear distributed across pages 8, 12, and 13 in separate sections rather than a unified risk disclosure. The supporting evidence ([G] category) provides comprehensive critical yield appendix detail and loss scenario tables that mirror the suitability report's figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's disadvantage and risk content is stated to be scattered across multiple sections (pages 8, 12, 13) rather than consolidated in a single dedicated section, whereas the supporting evidence in the pension switch report presents comprehensive loss scenario tables and critical yield analysis in structured appendix format [P11, P13, P14]. This represents a difference in presentation prominence and organization between the two documents.
      - The suitability report states adviser charges would be '1% per annum' and an 'initial fee' of circa £1,101.85 for the first year [P8], but the critical yield appendix section reveals an 'Initial adviser charge of £3,305 which is taken from the new solution' [P8], creating uncertainty about whether the client faces both an initial £3,305 charge and ongoing 1% charges, and whether the £1,101.85 figure is accurate or approximate.
      - The Standard Life switch benefit claim states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P6], but the Pension Switch Charges Assessment table shows impact as +0.26% [P4], a material discrepancy of 0.18 percentage points. This contradiction appears within the suitability report itself.
    The requirement asks whether disadvantages, risks, limitations, tax consequences, loss scenarios and consumer harms are fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently explained and consistent with supporting evidence. The comparison can be performed: both the suitability report and supporting evidence contain overlapping loss scenario data and critical yield analysis that can be checked for consistency. The canonical model notes that risk content is 'scattered throughout the report in multiple sections' rather than 'in a single dedicated section', but the guard states 'A dedicated risk section is not mandatory; disadvantages distributed but clearly stated can still be prominent.' The guard further notes that 'A material disadvantage quantified only in an appendix, while the body leads with a benefit, is a prominence finding.' Examining the structure: the suitability report presents benefits prominently (Lower Costs, Lifestyling, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility) in the main 'Fund recommendation' section [P6], while loss scenarios and critical yield analysis appear later and are stated to be in an Appendix [P2]. The loss scenario figures (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease at mid-growth) are quantified on pages 8 or 12-13 but the critical yield full analysis is relegated to the Appendix. The supporting evidence ([G]) contains the same figures in structured tabular format [P11]. The figures are consistent between report and evidence. However, the internal inconsistency within the suitability report regarding Standard Life charges (0.08% vs. +0.26%) represents a material inaccuracy that undermines the accuracy of disadvantage disclosure. The adviser charge disclosure is also internally inconsistent: 'circa £1,101.85' is presented as the first year cost in the main body [P8], but the appendix notes an 'Initial adviser charge of £3,305' [P8], which should be prominent as a material cost. Tax consequences are not explicitly addressed in either document examined. The report does explain risk profile and capacity for loss [P7], and presents no-guarantee language [P5], but the scattered presentation and the charge discrepancy represent disclosure shortfalls and accuracy concerns.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report extracts state that none of the five existing pension arrangements contain safeguarded benefits: 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' is repeated identically for all five arrangements on page 5. The report records retirement options available as: People's Pension and Scottish Widows (UFPLS, PartialUFPLS only); Aviva and Zurich (FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS); Standard Life (FlexiAccessDrawdown only). The extracted model flags that `/replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[]` and `/existingArrangements[]/otherFeatures[]` are absent from the report. The extraction metadata confirms these fields were 'NotPresentInDocument'.
    File:     The policy research table [P1] and [P8] show that People's Pension and Scottish Widows have neither annuity availability nor FAD available. Standard Life policy info [P17] confirms 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's), No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP), No Guaranteed Fund at retirement, No Reference Scheme Test'. Zurich policy info [P3] mentions life cover and waiver benefits: 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' The policy research table [P1] indicates Zurich has life cover (Yes) and partial transfers allowed (Yes). Aviva policy info [P14] shows 'Adviser charging: This plan does not allow adviser charging' and lists charges of 0.50% on fund value £3,744.36. Standard Life policy info [P10] notes low differential fund discount charges 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' No explicit statement confirms whether the existing Zurich arrangement has life cover or waiver benefits attached, though [P3] indicates these are no longer available on new plans.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not disclose that Zurich's existing arrangement may have life cover and waiver benefits that would be lost in a transfer, despite [P3] stating 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and the policy research table indicating Zurich has life cover.
      - The report does not explain that Standard Life's existing low differential fund discount charges ('These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' per [P10]) represent a material benefit being given up.
      - The retirement options available differ materially between existing arrangements but the report does not prominently explain what each client is giving up: People's Pension and Scottish Widows cannot offer FAD or annuities (per [P1], [P8], [P16]), while Aviva and Zurich can offer FAD, and all can offer UFPLS/PartialUFPLS.
      - The report does not explain that annuity options are available on some existing plans (Aviva, Zurich, and possibly others per [P1] and [P8]) but the new solution does not appear to offer this option based on the retirement options listed in the replacement analysis.
    The extraction itself signals that the report is silent on features or guarantees lost during the switch (`/replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[]` absent, reason: NotPresentInDocument). This is material because the instructions and decision logic require that 'Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up' must be 'stated in the report' for No Issue and that omission of material features constitutes a Potential Concern. The evidence pack contains multiple material benefits being given up: (1) Zurich's existing arrangement may carry life cover and waiver benefits which [P3] explicitly states 'are no longer available on new pension plans', requiring completion of a medical questionnaire for separate cover, 'particularly important if your health has deteriorated'. (2) Standard Life's low differential fund discount (Leaver Discount 0.48% per [P10]) is explicitly flagged as not transferable: 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' (3) Retirement options differ by arrangement: People's Pension and Scottish Widows lack FAD and annuity options; Aviva and Zurich offer FAD and annuities (per policy research [P1], [P8]); the new solution's retirement options are not detailed in the report's disclosed features. (4) The report states safeguarded benefits are absent but does not confirm what other features each plan carries or whether features like life cover, death benefits enhancements, or special charging terms are present. The guard states: 'A guarantee recorded in provider literature but never mentioned in the report is the finding; do not require the report to have named it before looking for it.' The guard also states: 'A benefit the client cannot access at their current age is still a benefit being given up.' These guards confirm that silence in the report about material features documented in the policy literature is itself a finding. The critical yield section flagged in the metadata shows a £12,000 (9.4%) decrease at mid-growth, but no loss scenario explaining what this means to the client or the non-financial harms (loss of life cover requiring medical underwriting) is disclosed in the report. The report does not gather and present these disadvantages 'fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently' as required by the standard.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings. It states: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.' The report also claims the recommendation 'is the most suitable solution given your circumstances and retirement objectives' and 'provides an extremely tax-efficient way of building up a retirement fund.'
    File:     The evidence shows material discrepancies in the projected outcomes. P1 (Pension switch report) displays a comparison table showing: at mid-growth rate (2.95%), existing pension projected value at age 75 is £127,000 versus new solution £125,000, representing an 'Effect of switching' of £2,000 or 1.9% decrease. The same source shows at low growth (0.00%), existing £102,000 versus new solution £100,000 (£2,000 or 2.0% decrease), and at high growth (5.88%), existing £150,000 versus new solution £147,000 (£3,000 or 2.0% decrease). P1 further states the new solution requires a critical yield of 3.2% at mid-growth to match the existing plan's maturity value. P12 shows the research compared products ranked by maturity value, with 'Zurich - Zurich pension' ranked 1st at 0.2% reduction in yield and £128,000, 'Existing Solution' ranked 2nd at 0.3% and £127,000, and 'New Solution' ranked 5th at 0.6% reduction in yield and £125,000 at mid-growth. P17 notes 'This results in a shortfall in the death benefit immediately after the switch of £3,305.00.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states recommendation has 'demonstrated stronger performance' compared to existing Aviva holdings, but P1 shows new solution produces lower projected values at all three growth rates (low, mid, high)
      - Report claims critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past performance, but P1 states critical yield required is 3.2% at mid-growth — a material difference in the threshold necessary for outperformance
      - Report projects £110,185 transfer value but P1 tables show 'Effect of switching' as a reduction of £2,000 at mid-growth (£127,000 existing vs £125,000 new), contradicting the narrative of outperformance
      - Report justifies recommendation as 'most suitable' without addressing that ranked analysis in P12 places existing solution 2nd at £127,000 and new solution 5th at £125,000 at mid-growth rates
      - Report makes no mention of the £3,305 initial adviser charge impact or death benefit shortfall disclosed in P17
    The core issue is that the report's justification for increased adviser costs and reduced projected outcomes is fundamentally contradicted by the evidence. The report asserts that the recommended funds have 'demonstrated stronger performance' and that a critical yield of 4.8% 'is achievable,' framing this as support for accepting the cost of advice. However, P1's detailed switch analysis shows the opposite: at every growth rate scenario, the new solution produces materially lower projected values than the existing arrangement. At mid-growth (2.95%), the difference is £2,000 (1.9% decrease). The evidence demonstrates that to match the existing plan's value, the new solution requires a 3.2% critical yield, not 4.8%. The report provides no explanation for why a client should accept switching into a solution that underperforms across all scenarios and requires higher growth rates to break even. The initial adviser charge of £3,305 is deducted from the investment, creating an immediate shortfall in death benefits (P17). The report's statement that the investment is 'most suitable' is presented without acknowledging that independent research (P12) ranked the existing solution superior in terms of projected maturity value. The guard permits weak justifications that repeat benefits without connecting them to costs; however, this goes beyond weakness—it presents an affirmative case for outperformance that is directly contradicted by quantified projections in the supporting documents. The justification is not merely absent or generic; it is contradicted by material evidence within the file.
