  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses: (1) Zurich investments susceptible to market volatility with greater fluctuations; Aviva advised funds have stronger performance than existing Aviva holdings but have not outperformed Zurich funds (LowerPerformance). (2) Access delays of 10-15 working days for fund withdrawal (AccessDelay). (3) Ongoing service cost of circa £1,101.85 for first year (OngoingServiceCost). (4) Cashflow chart does not model long-term care costs (LongTermCareCostsNotModelled). (5) Projections are uncertain and small variances compound over time (ProjectionUncertainty). (6) All payments subject to Aviva's discretion (DiscretionaryPayments). (7) Cancellation notice includes provision that previous provider not obliged to take plan back (CancellationLimitation). (8) Transferring all pensions would limit withdrawal flexibility and prevent flexible access (LimitedWithdrawalFlexibility). (9) Current lifestyling funds reduce risk as retirement approaches, portfolio will move outside agreed risk range (LifestylingRiskDrift).
    File:     The evidence identifies: (1) Pension switch report [P1, P3, P10] shows effect of switching at mid-growth (2.95%) is £2,000-£12,000 or 1.6%-9.4% decrease depending on arrangement; at low growth (0%) £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; at high growth (5.88%) £15,000 or 10% decrease. Critical yield requirements of 3.2%-7.8% depending on growth scenario. Reduction in yield for new solution is 0.6%-2.3% versus existing 0.3%. (2) Zurich Policy Info [P8] notes life cover and waiver benefits no longer available on new plans; medical questionnaire needed if seeking similar cover separately; health deterioration since taking existing benefits is important consideration. (3) Product literature [P11, P18, P19, P20] discloses material risks including counterparty, credit, default and derivatives risks; currency exchange risk; property liquidity constraints; fixed-term access restrictions; income drawdown depletes funds if growth insufficient; high income withdrawal reduces funds for dependants or future annuity; means-tested benefits may be affected by drawdown income. (4) Standard Life Policy Info [P15, P22, P23] notes plan does not facilitate UFPLS; has no Guaranteed Annuity Rates or Guaranteed Minimum Pension; does not permit partial transfers for investment purposes. (5) Aviva Policy Info [P16] notes plan does not allow adviser charging and has no transferring out charge. (6) KIID material risks [P18] include counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk; asset allocation fluctuates based on market movement. (7) Aviva Key Features [P20] discloses commitment to retain financial adviser; restrictions if no longer advised; various retirement options with tax implications; fund choice and flexibility features.
    Discrepancies:
      -  The report states Aviva advised funds 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' but does not quantify the shortfall or disclose the pension switch report finding that switching to Aviva would result in a decrease of £2,000-£15,000 (1.6%-10% depending on growth rate) at retirement versus retaining existing arrangements.
      -  The report discloses access delay of 10-15 working days but does not reference or quantify the switching cost impact: the switch itself involves an initial adviser charge of £3,305 [P3] plus ongoing 1% adviser charge, reducing initial investment and ongoing returns.
      -  The report states ongoing service cost of 'circa £1,101.85' but the pension switch report [P3] shows reduction in yield of 2.2% for new solution versus 0.3% for existing, representing a material ongoing cost differential not explicitly broken down in the report.
      -  The report discloses lifestyling risk drift but does not disclose that existing Zurich arrangement [P4] shows the client's current agreed risk level is 5, and the report does not explain what risk level the new solution achieves or how it differs from the existing arrangements' risk profiles.
      -  The report does not disclose that life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans [P8] and that a medical questionnaire would be required if seeking similar cover separately—a material loss of benefits and additional process burden.
      -  The report does not disclose that Standard Life plan does not permit partial transfers for investment purposes [P23], limiting flexibility compared to other arrangements.
      -  The report does not disclose that Standard Life plan has no Guaranteed Annuity Rates or Guaranteed Minimum Pension [P23], a material loss of protection features if switching away.
      -  The report does not disclose material investment risks documented in product literature [P11, P18, P19]: counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency exchange risk, property liquidity constraints, and income drawdown depletion scenarios are absent from the report's risk disclosure.
      -  The report does not disclose that means-tested benefits may be affected by income drawdown [P11], a material consequence for retirement planning.
      -  The report does not quantify or explain tax consequences: the Key Features document [P20] explains that drawdown provides flexibility to take up to 25% tax free and remaining pot subject to marginal tax rate, but the report provides no illustration of tax impact on the client's income.
      -  The report does not provide a loss scenario showing the at-retirement value under low-growth conditions: pension switch report [P10] shows at 0% growth the new solution would be £97,600 versus existing £108,000, a £10,400 or 9.6% loss scenario.
      -  The report cites an initial fee of £3,305 and 1% ongoing adviser charge [P3] but does not clearly disclose these separately or illustrate their cumulative impact compared to the stated 'circa £1,101.85' annual cost, which appears to exclude the 1% ongoing charge.
      -  The report does not disclose that Aviva plan does not allow adviser charging [P16], creating an inconsistency with the recommendation to pay 1% ongoing adviser charge on the new solution.
      -  The report does not disclose that previous providers are not obliged to accept cancellation and take plans back, limiting exit options if the client wishes to revert.
      -  The report does not disclose reduction in yield figures: new solution shows 0.6%-2.3% versus existing 0.3%, a material cost differential representing reduced growth compared to current arrangements.
    The comparison has been performed. Multiple material risks, disadvantages and limitations are identified in the supporting evidence but are absent from the report's disclosure. The pension switch report [P1, P3, P10] quantifies the effect of switching as resulting in projected decreases of £2,000-£15,000 (1.6%-10%) at retirement, which is material to assessing the disadvantage of the recommendation yet is not discussed in the report. The reduction in yield differential (0.6%-2.3% new vs 0.3% existing) represents an ongoing cost disadvantage not prominently disclosed. Product literature [P11, P18, P19, P20] discloses substantial investment risks (counterparty, credit, default, derivatives, currency, property liquidity) and income drawdown consequences (means-tested benefits impact, depletion if insufficient growth) that are absent from the report's risk section. The loss of life cover and waiver benefits [P8] and the requirement for a new medical questionnaire are material disadvantages not disclosed. The fact that Standard Life plan does not permit partial transfers and lacks Guaranteed Annuity Rates or Guaranteed Minimum Pension [P23] represents material loss of existing protections. The report does not provide an explicit at-retirement loss scenario under low-growth conditions (£97,600 vs £108,000 = £10,400 shortfall at 0% growth [P10]). Tax consequences are not illustrated despite Key Features [P20] explaining tax treatment of drawdown. The adviser charges (£3,305 initial + 1% ongoing) are not clearly separated or illustrated against the stated £1,101.85 annual cost figure. The discrepancy between Aviva plan not allowing adviser charging [P16] and the recommendation including 1% ongoing adviser charge is not explained. The report's statement that limitations 'do not consider long-term care costs' and projections are 'uncertain' are generic boilerplate; they lack product-specific explanation of how switching affects these limitations or what specific risks apply to the new solution versus existing arrangements. The disclosure of Zurich performance shortfall lacks quantification and fails to reference the material projected loss from switching. These omissions represent a pattern of material information from research, product literature and pension switching analysis that is absent from the primary report disclosure.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Charges comparison for Aviva: Report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (existing 0.50% vs recommended 0.44%), but the Charges dimension in the canonical model assesses this as 'Better' — this is internally consistent but requires evidence the 0.06% difference justifies switching. Charges comparison for Zurich: Report states -0.49% in quote field but canonical model shows existing 0.93% vs recommended 0.44%, a difference of 0.49% — mathematical consistency present but the critical yield analysis in [P19] shows this cost saving does not prevent an overall £12,000 (9.4%) decrease in projected retirement value. Performance comparison labelling inconsistency: [P6] shows two charts with conflicting fund labels — first chart labels A as Aviva and D as Zurich, but second volatility chart labels show A as Zurich and D as Aviva (reversed labelling), making performance comparison unreliable. 29 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts 10 separate comparisons across multiple dimensions (Charges, RiskAlignment, FundRange, AdviserServicing, RetirementOptions, Performance) for three existing arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) against a recommended new solution. For Charges: Aviva existing 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% (Better); Zurich existing 0.93% vs recommended 0.44% (Better). For RiskAlignment: Aviva existing Risk 2 (lifestyling) vs recommended Risk 5 (Better); Zurich existing Risk 7 vs recommended Risk 5 (Better). For FundRange: Aviva existing limited range vs recommended 6,900+ funds (Better); Zurich existing 60+ funds vs recommended 6,900+ (Better). For AdviserServicing: all existing arrangements offer no adviser servicing vs recommended allows direct deduction (Better). For RetirementOptions: Mixed assessments ranging from Equivalent to Better. Performance data provided for existing funds (Aviva 15.58%, Zurich 44.32%, SL 24.56%) but stated as not outperforming recommended arrangement overall.
    File:     Evidence pack contains product research from [P1] Policy Research table showing existing arrangements' features; [P3], [P4], [P5], [P9], [P17], [P20] Pension switch report showing alternative comparisons consolidating pensions with projected values; [P6] Performance analysis chart with labelling issues; [P18] Aviva policy documentation confirming lifestaging approach and no guaranteed rates; [P12] New solution investment details with two balanced multi-asset funds (Columbia Threadneedle and HSBC); [P19] Suitability Report critical yield analysis showing switching reduces projected value by £12,000 (9.4%) at mid-growth; [P16] Suitability Report Existing Arrangements table showing fund values and charges; [P21] Fund factsheet for Columbia Threadneedle fund; [P7] Key Features document detailing retirement options available. The pension switch report [P3]-[P5] shows detailed comparisons across all three existing pensions with projections, but these are presented in 'alternative comparisons' format rather than as primary recommendation support. The report [P19] explicitly states switching results in 9.4% decrease in projected value at mid-growth rate (£12,000 reduction from £127,000 to £115,000).
    Discrepancies:
      - Charges comparison for Aviva: Report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (existing 0.50% vs recommended 0.44%), but the Charges dimension in the canonical model assesses this as 'Better' — this is internally consistent but requires evidence the 0.06% difference justifies switching
      - Charges comparison for Zurich: Report states -0.49% in quote field but canonical model shows existing 0.93% vs recommended 0.44%, a difference of 0.49% — mathematical consistency present but the critical yield analysis in [P19] shows this cost saving does not prevent an overall £12,000 (9.4%) decrease in projected retirement value
      - Performance comparison labelling inconsistency: [P6] shows two charts with conflicting fund labels — first chart labels A as Aviva and D as Zurich, but second volatility chart labels show A as Zurich and D as Aviva (reversed labelling), making performance comparison unreliable
      - RiskAlignment for Zurich: Report states existing is 'Risk level 7 (Managed AP) — higher risk than agreed profile' and recommends Risk 5, but [P11] confirms Zurich fund is single fund at 'N/A' risk level per Defaqto methodology, not explicitly confirmed as Risk 7 in provider documentation
      - RetirementOptions comparison: Report compares Zurich existing 'Full and partial UFPLS only' as disadvantage against recommended 'Multiple flexible drawdown options', but [P1] provider research table and [P7] Key Features confirm both existing Zurich and recommended arrangement offer FAD and UFPLS — no clear difference established
      - FundRange for Aviva: Report states Aviva existing offers 'limited range' vs recommended 6,900+. [P18] Aviva policy documentation confirms maximum 50 funds possible simultaneously but does not state total available fund range as limited relative to market
      - Adviser Servicing comparison: Report contrasts existing 'No adviser servicing' with recommended 'Allows adviser charges to be deducted directly'. However, [P1] Policy Research table shows existing Zurich 'Can adviser charging be applied: No' but existing Aviva 'Can adviser charging be applied: No', and new solution 'Can adviser charging be applied: Yes' — the comparison is supported but [P8] shows existing arrangements explicitly state 'Current Adviser charge: No'. The comparison asserts this is an advantage, but [P19] critical yield projections incorporate 1.00% ongoing adviser charge and £3,305 initial charge into new solution, resulting in significantly worse outcomes
      - Projected retirement values: Report states at mid-growth all existing arrangements will be switched to new solution. [P19] shows existing pensions total £127,000 at age 75 vs new solution £115,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. This contradicts the narrative assertion that switching produces 'Better' outcomes across multiple dimensions. [P17] for all pensions combined shows identical reduction
      - Missing safeguarded benefits analysis: Report canonical model shows no /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost field populated. [P16] Suitability Report table states 'Safeguarded Features incl. TFC/GARs/MIG: This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' for all five existing arrangements, but the comparison does not explicitly address what is lost by switching or confirm none exist on new solution
    Comparison is performed across all stated dimensions with provider documentation, performance data and switching analysis available. However, critical inconsistencies emerge: (1) Charges comparison: The report asserts cost savings of 0.06% (Aviva) and 0.49% (Zurich) but the critical yield analysis in [P19] demonstrates these savings are more than offset by adviser charges (initial £3,305 and 1.00% ongoing), resulting in projected retirement values that are 9.4% lower (£12,000 less) even before adviser charges were explicitly disclosed to the client in the comparison sections [P3]-[P5]. The comparison shows raw charge differences but does not reconcile with the actual financial outcome analysis which reveals switching is detrimental. (2) Performance labelling error in [P6]: The two performance charts use inconsistent fund labelling (A switches between Aviva and Zurich, D switches between Zurich and Aviva across the two charts), undermining the reliability of the performance comparison presented. (3) RiskAlignment: While the report asserts Zurich is Risk 7 and existing Aviva is Risk 2, provider documentation [P18] confirms Aviva uses 'Lifestage Investment Approach' with auto-switching, and Zurich documentation [P11] does not confirm Risk 7 classification in provider terms. The report's risk classification appears to be adviser-derived rather than confirmed by provider documentation. (4) RetirementOptions: The comparison characterises Zurich as offering only 'Full and partial UFPLS' but [P7] Key Features and [P1] research table both show FAD is available on Zurich, making this comparison incomplete or incorrect. (5) AdviserServicing: The report presents adviser charging facilitation as a 'Better' outcome, but the underlying critical yield analysis reveals adviser charges (£3,305 initial + 1.00% ongoing) are the primary driver of the 9.4% reduction in projected value. This represents a fundamental contradiction: adviser servicing is presented as a benefit while the financials show it materially worsens the client's position. (6) Safeguarded benefits: The report confirms no safeguarded benefits exist on existing arrangements but does not compare against new solution or address what is retained/lost. (7) The switching analysis in [P3], [P4], [P5] presents alternative comparisons showing the new solution underperforms across all three existing arrangements when considered individually or consolidated, yet the recommendation proceeds. The comparison is performed but the recommendation contradicts the comparative financial analysis. No vulnerability overlay issues identified in the evidence pack that would materially affect this comparison.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts multiple benefits of switching: lower costs (receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure); lifestyling risk (funds automatically reduce risk approaching retirement, moving outside agreed risk range); greater investment choice (wider range of options for tailored strategy); adviser charging facilitation (charges deducted directly); ongoing monitoring and adjustments (ability to actively monitor and adjust). For costs, the report states: no exit penalties (£0); initial advice fee of £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested); annual charge differences vary by plan — Aviva shows -0.06% (-£2.25), Zurich shows -0.49% (-£506.85). The report claims a critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth (2.95%) to match existing plan value. The report states these benefits would not detract from financial retirement plan and could enable tax-efficient withdrawal within personal allowance.
    File:     The pension switch report (P2, P3, P6, P12) provides detailed comparisons. For the overall three-plan consolidation at mid-growth (2.95%), existing projected value is £127,000 versus new solution £125,000, showing a decrease of £2,000 or 1.6% (P6). The standalone Zurich-only comparison (P3) at mid-growth shows existing £127,000 versus new £125,000, effect of -£2,000 or 1.6% decrease. The critical yield required is shown as 3.2% (P6 overall) not 4.8%. Notably, P12 shows a different scenario where at mid-growth the existing pension value is £127,000 and new solution is £115,000, representing a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease, with critical yield of 4.8%. The switch charges analysis (P15) notes advisor fees of 3% (matching report's £3,305.55 on £110,185.60 transfer value). P7 shows immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305.00 post-switch. Zurich policy (P9) confirms no fund switch charge and no transfer value charges. The evidence does not provide explicit corroboration of claimed advantages (lifestyling benefits, fund choice superiority, monitoring capability).
    Discrepancies:
      - Critical yield figure: report states 4.8% at mid-growth, but P6 switch report shows 3.2% for overall three-plan consolidation at same growth rate
      - Effect of switching values: report implies benefits justify higher costs but P6 shows £2,000 or 1.6% decrease in projected maturity at mid-growth; P12 shows significantly worse outcome of £12,000 or 9.4% decrease at mid-growth depending on which plans are included
      - Charge comparison consistency: report claims Aviva annual charge difference of -0.06% but the arithmetic checks show no corroborating exact figure in passages; Zurich shows -0.49% charge difference but again no exact passage match
      - Death benefit impact: P7 shows immediate shortfall of £3,305.00 (exactly matching advisor fee amount) but report does not disclose this material loss of fund value at point of switch
      - Benefit claims lacking substantiation: report asserts lifestyling advantage, greater fund choice, and monitoring capability but these are not evidenced by comparison data or fund documentation in passages E and G
    The report's headline benefit claims rest on two foundations: cost savings and non-cost advantages. On costs: the report correctly identifies zero exit penalties and the 3% initial advice fee. However, the annual charge comparison creates ambiguity. The report's canonical model states Aviva at -0.06% and Zurich at -0.49%, but the switch report (P6, P12) presents these plans within comparison tables that show projected maturity values. At mid-growth 2.95%, the overall consolidation produces not a saving but a loss: £127,000 existing to £125,000 new, a 1.6% decrease (P6). A different combination shown in P12 produces an even worse outcome: £127,000 to £115,000, a 9.4% decrease. The critical yield figure exemplifies this inconsistency: report states 4.8% but P6 shows 3.2% for the three-plan consolidation at identical mid-growth assumption. This is material because critical yield is the growth rate the new solution must achieve to match the existing plan — a higher critical yield requirement works against the switching case. The report claims benefits would not detract from retirement plan, but the effect-of-switching data contradicts this at mid-growth rates. P7 reveals a death benefit shortfall of exactly £3,305, matching the advisor fee, showing immediate capital erosion. The report asserts lifestyling risk as a driver (existing funds drift outside agreed risk range), greater fund choice, and superior ongoing monitoring, but provides no evidence these advantages exist or have been quantified. The switch report (P15) contains fund information but does not evidence that Aviva offers meaningfully greater choice than Zurich or that the new funds are superior. No sensitivity analysis is provided linking the cost increases (2.2% reduction in yield for new solution at mid-growth per P12) to claimed non-cost benefits. The claim that benefits outweigh higher costs is not supported by the charge analysis or the projected values, which show consistent underperformance of the new solution.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that five existing arrangements have no safeguarded benefits: 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' (repeated for each of the 5 plans on page 5 of the report). The report also states that employer contributions are not lost on any of the four switching/transfer recommendations (employerContributionsLost = false for all four). The report recommends: (1) Retain People's Pension; (2) Encash Scottish Widows small pot £200; (3) Switch Aviva plan; (4) Switch Zurich plan; (5) Switch Standard Life plan.
    File:     Provider documentation confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits across all plans: Aviva (SP50163762) states 'This plan doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'The plan doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension' [P17]; Scottish Widows plan confirms 'Guaranteed Annuity Rates: No', 'Guaranteed Minimum Pension: No', 'Guaranteed Minimum Fund: No', 'Waiver of premium: No', 'Life cover: No' [P2, P10, P21]; Standard Life states 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement' [P4, P23]; Zurich confirms 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan' [P15]; People's Pension documentation makes no mention of safeguarded benefits [P8]. However, Zurich policy information identifies that 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' [P1], indicating these are features being lost. Standard Life documentation states the plan 'do[es] not facilitate adviser charging' [P4, P24], meaning adviser management available on the new solution is not available on the existing plan. The Scottish Widows plan 'does not facilitate adviser charging' [P10, P24] and 'Money4Life Plans do not include life cover, the member, if still employed, may have death in service benefits through their employer' [P10].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts no safeguarded benefits exist on all five plans; evidence confirms this is technically accurate but obscures a material distinction: life cover and waiver of premium exist on some existing plans but are not available on new plans, and adviser management is not available on existing Scottish Widows and Standard Life plans but is available on the recommended Aviva Platform solution
      - Report does not disclose loss of life cover between existing Zurich arrangement and recommended new solution, despite Zurich documentation explicitly stating 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' [P1]
      - Report does not disclose loss of waiver of premium between existing Zurich arrangement and recommended new solution, despite Zurich documentation explicitly stating this benefit is unavailable on new plans [P1]
      - Report does not address the gain in adviser management capability on the new Aviva Platform solution compared to Scottish Widows and Standard Life existing arrangements, which explicitly do not facilitate adviser charging [P10, P24]
      - The critical yield analysis references adviser fees being paid on the new solution but does not clearly identify what features or capabilities justify these fees compared to existing arrangements
      - Report does not address whether Scottish Widows small pot encashment (£200 at 25% tax-free) is the appropriate course given the member's circumstances; the report states 'We estimate you would receive £170 net' [P16] but does not compare this to alternative withdrawal strategies
    The report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits are being lost is technically supported by provider documentation. Every plan—Aviva, Scottish Widows, Standard Life, Zurich, and People's Pension—confirms via their own policy information that they hold no guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed minimum pensions, guaranteed minimum funds, or similar safeguarded benefits. This is corroborated across multiple sources [P2, P4, P10, P15, P17, P21, P23]. However, the report's silence on life cover and waiver of premium creates a material gap. Zurich's own policy information explicitly states 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' [P1]. The report does not address whether the existing Zurich plan held these benefits or what their value was; Zurich's wording suggests they are features of the existing Zurich arrangement that will be lost upon transfer. The report also does not address adviser management differences: existing Scottish Widows and Standard Life plans 'do not facilitate adviser charging' [P10, P24], whilst the recommended Aviva Platform solution explicitly does. The report frames the switch to Aviva Platform partly on cost and partly on investment flexibility, but does not disclose that adviser management is being added as a new feature that will incur cost. The critical yield section (referenced in the extraction's internal inconsistencies but not provided in full) appears to account for adviser fees on the new solution but the report does not articulate clearly what capability or service justifies these fees relative to existing arrangements. The Scottish Widows encashment recommendation lacks proper analysis: the report states a £200 pension would net £170 after 25% tax-free cash and income tax, but does not compare this to the alternative of leaving it invested or the consequences of losing this small pot entirely. The extraction metadata confirms that /replacementAnalysis/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[] is absent from the extracted model ('NotPresentInDocument'), and /replacementAnalysis/criticalYieldExplanation is 'PresentButUnparseable', suggesting the report either does not fully disclose lost features or the analysis is unclear. Against the requirement's decision logic, the report does address safeguarded benefits (confirming their absence) and does address alternatives to some extent (e.g., noting Scottish Widows consolidation is not recommended because it lacks a risk-5 fund and does not allow adviser management), but it fails to disclose non-safeguarded features that are material to the switching decision: life cover, waiver of premium, and the addition of adviser charging. These are not safeguarded benefits, but they are features or costs that affect the comparison between existing and recommended arrangements.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that four alternatives were considered: (1) Leave Scottish Widows pension in place — rejected because it does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned to risk rating 5 (would require combining two funds with one in cash, leaving 20-30% out of market) and does not allow adviser management; (2) Leave the existing pension scheme untouched — considered but no rejection reasons stated; (3) Leave existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds — considered but no rejection reasons stated; (4) Leave existing Zurich pension scheme untouched — considered but no rejection reasons stated; (5) Internal fund switch within Zurich — rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds (would require blend of risk 7 and risk 2 including Interest Deposits, reducing diversification), blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options, and consolidation better serves client's main priority of consolidating pensions and gaining better control with all available drawdown options.
    File:     The evidence pack contains the suitability report itself [P1, P6] which repeats these same assertions, and a pension switching report [P9, P22] which states 'Analysis was undertaken on 23 October 2025 to investigate the benefits of switching your current pension solution to a new solution.' The switching report [P9, P22] provides a comparison summary and comparison tables, but does not explicitly document consideration of the specific alternatives the suitability report claims were considered. The provider documentation [P4, P8, P15] confirms product features (Scottish Widows and Zurich do not allow adviser charging; fund ranges are as stated), but does not evidence that these alternatives were systematically considered during the advice process. No separate analysis document, meeting notes, fact-find or adviser work file entries are present that independently corroborate the consideration and rejection reasoning for each alternative.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report claims four alternatives were considered and evaluated, but only two have rejection reasoning explicitly documented in the report: Scottish Widows (risk alignment and adviser management) and Zurich internal switch (risk alignment, performance, consolidation objective). Two alternatives are listed as 'considered' with empty rejection reason arrays, suggesting they were noted but not analysed or their analysis is not captured in the extracted model.
      - The switching report [P9] documents a comparison between existing pensions and new solution but does not separately document or cross-reference the alternative scenarios (do-nothing, in-situ switch) that the suitability report claims were evaluated.
      - The report states consolidation 'achieves better than an internal fund switch' as a reason to reject the Zurich internal switch, but the switching report shows projections only for the three ceding plans being consolidated into the new solution, not a side-by-side comparison of Zurich-only retention versus Zurich-only internal switch.
    The requirement is whether alternatives to switching were considered and whether the reasoning for rejecting them is supported by evidence. The suitability report [P1, P6] explicitly names four alternatives and provides rejection reasoning for two of them. Scottish Widows rejection reasons (no risk 5 multi-asset fund, no adviser management) are supported by provider documentation [P8] which confirms Scottish Widows does not allow adviser charging. Zurich rejection reasons cite lack of risk 5 funds, poor performance of blends, and consolidation objective. Provider documentation [P4, P8] confirms Zurich's fund range (the report states Zurich offers risk 2 and 7 but not 5, consistent with [P8] and [P10] which show Zurich fund details). The performance comparison claim ('blended funds have not performed as well as options available through Aviva') is stated in [P1, P6] but the switching report [P9, P19] shows comparative projections where Zurich-only switch to new solution performs worse than existing Zurich at mid growth: existing £127,000 vs new £115,000 (9.4% decrease). This appears to reference the Managed AP (existing) versus recommended fund comparison, but does not directly compare the rejected blended in-house option to Aviva. Two alternatives (retaining existing scheme untouched, in-situ fund switch within existing scheme) are listed as considered in the extracted model with empty rejection reasons. The report text at [P1] and [P6] does reference 'Leave the existing pension scheme untouched' and 'Leave existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds' but provides no rejection reasoning for these. The guard 'Options assessed once for the whole case may legitimately cover several plans — check whether rejection reasoning is plan-specific where plans differ materially' applies: the two Scottish Widows and Zurich rejection reasons are plan-specific, but the two generic alternatives (do nothing, in-situ switch within existing) lack plan-specific reasoning. For Scottish Widows, the reasoning is directly supported (no adviser management is confirmed by [P8], risk alignment issue is documented). For Zurich internal switch, the risk alignment and consolidation rationale are supported, but the performance comparison to Aviva is stated without quantified evidence comparing the specific rejected blend option to the recommended Aviva fund. The do-nothing alternatives lack articulated rejection reasoning in the documents provided, meaning the case file does not evidence why they were rejected or whether they were genuinely evaluated versus merely listed.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts switching costs of zero exit penalties across all four replacement analyses, with initial advice fees of £3,305.55 charged as 3% of transfer value. For the Aviva-SP50163762 switch, it claims an annual charge difference of -0.06% (saving £2.25 pa). For the Zurich switch, it claims -0.49% pa (saving £506.85 pa). The report states critical yields of 1.9%, 4.8%, and 7.8% per annum. Benefits cited include lower costs, lifestyling risk avoidance, greater fund choice, and adviser charging facilitation. The report asserts these benefits justify the recommendation despite the critical yield analysis showing at mid-growth rate (2.95%), the new solution would achieve £115,000 vs existing £127,000 at age 75, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%.
    File:     The pension switch report evidence shows three alternative comparison scenarios. For Zurich pension only: existing value £102,000 (low growth) vs new £100,000, with -2% effect at low growth; at mid-growth (2.95%) existing £127,000 vs new £115,000, with -£12,000 or -9.4% effect; at high growth £150,000 vs £135,000, with -£15,000 or -10.0% effect. For Standard Life pension only: at low growth £2,910 vs £2,900 (-0.3%); at mid-growth £4,010 vs £4,010 (no change shown); at high growth £4,020 vs £4,010 (-0.2%). The evidence shows an immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305.00 following the switch (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60). The evidence lists alternative comparisons considered but does not show a quantified cost-benefit reconciliation that demonstrates benefits exceed the stated costs and negative projected outcomes.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts annual charge difference of -0.06% for Aviva switch; evidence shows no such figure in supporting documentation
      - Report asserts annual charge difference of -0.49% for Zurich switch; evidence shows no such figure in supporting documentation
      - Report claims -£2.25 annual saving; evidence does not corroborate this figure
      - Report claims -£506.85 annual saving; evidence does not corroborate this figure
      - Report states critical yields of 1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8% per annum; evidence in [P7] shows identical figures (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) but these are labeled as the growth rates required from the new solution to match existing pension maturity values, not presented with reconciliation to actual expected returns
      - Report projects switching leads to benefits justifying the recommendation, yet evidence shows at mid-growth (2.95%), switching results in £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected maturity value at age 75 for the main Zurich comparison
      - Report claims switching benefits include lower costs, yet the cost comparison data in evidence shows the new solution has higher reduction in yield (2.2% vs 0.3% at mid-growth for Zurich), directly contradicting the 'lower costs' benefit assertion
      - Evidence shows immediate death benefit loss of £3,305.00 from advice fee deduction; this is not explicitly addressed as a cost in the switching cost analysis, which lists only zero exit penalties and the initial advice fee
    The report asserts that switching costs are justified by benefits, but the evidence reveals material contradictions. First, the charge comparison figures the report cites (-0.06%, -0.49%, -£2.25, -£506.85) do not appear in the supporting switch report evidence provided. The evidence shows the new solution actually has HIGHER reduction in yield at mid-growth (2.2% vs 0.3%), meaning higher drag on returns, not lower costs. Second, the critical yield analysis in the evidence [P7] shows at the most realistic growth scenario (2.95%), switching results in £12,000 or 9.4% loss in projected maturity value. This is a material adverse financial outcome. The report's stated benefits (lower costs, greater choice, adviser charging) are presented generically without quantified analysis of whether they offset this £12,000 projected loss. Third, the immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305.00 is evidenced [P2] but not framed as a switching cost in the cost analysis; the switching cost analysis lists only exit penalties (£0) and initial advice fee (£3,305.55), creating internal inconsistency about what costs are included. Fourth, while the evidence shows alternative comparisons were done (Zurich only, Standard Life only, Aviva only), these comparisons also show negative effects of switching, yet the evidence does not show analysis of why retaining existing pensions was rejected. The guard permitting non-financial benefits to justify cost increases applies only where 'the report argues it explicitly'—the report states these benefits generically but provides no explicit quantified argument that they offset the £12,000 projected loss. The extraction metadata notes the critical yield explanation is 'PresentButUnparseable', suggesting the numerical support for the switching recommendation may not be transparently presented. Taken together, the report recommends switching despite evidence showing material projected value loss at the central growth scenario, without adequate quantified justification that non-financial benefits compensate for this loss.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report considers five main alternatives: (1) Leave Scottish Widows pension in place — rejected because it does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5, would require combining two funds with 20-30% in cash, and does not allow adviser management; (2) Leave existing pension scheme untouched — considered but rejected; (3) Leave existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds — considered but rejected; (4) Leave existing Zurich pension untouched — considered but rejected; (5) Internal fund switch within Zurich — rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, a blend would reduce diversification, blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options, and consolidation better serves client objectives. The report also considers (6) Switch schemes into employer's pension (rejected: funds don't meet risk profile, plan 0.06% more expensive, £4.50 annual admin charge, limited fund range). The recommended option is to set up a new pension plan (Aviva) to consolidate existing pensions. People's Pension is retained as the current workplace scheme with ongoing contributions.
    File:     The evidence [P1] shows comparative projections for Overall position across all arrangements: existing pensions project to £141,000 at age 75 with 0.6% reduction in yield, while new solution projects £138,000 with 0.6% reduction in yield, representing a £3,000 or 2.1% decrease. For Zurich pension only [P3], existing projects to £127,000 (0.3% RIY) versus new solution £117,000 (0.6% RIY), effect of switching -£10,000 or 7.9% decrease. For Aviva plan only [P5], existing £4,280 versus new £4,260, effect -£20 or 0.5% decrease. For Standard Life only [P8], existing £3,440 versus new £3,430, effect -£10 or 0.3% decrease. Critical yield analysis [P13] at mid growth rate (2.95%) shows existing £127,000 versus new solution £115,000, effect -£12,000 or 9.4% decrease, with new solution requiring 4.8% critical yield. The report states charges for Standard Life as 0.08% more expensive [P17] but the comparison table shows impact. [P15] shows performance chart comparing Aviva existing (A) and Zurich existing (D) against recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) over 5 years: A Aviva achieved 15.58%, B Aviva Risk 5 achieved 21.48%, D Zurich not fully visible in excerpt. The research document [P18] provides a detailed comparison table of all five existing schemes showing retirement options, number of funds, adviser servicing capability. [P19] confirms the five existing arrangements and the recommendation to switch into new Aviva arrangement while retaining People's Pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the new solution will provide better outcomes through 'lower costs' and 'greater investment choice', but the switching analysis [P1][P3][P13] shows the new solution projects to lower maturity values: overall £3,000 decrease (2.1%), Zurich-only £10,000 decrease (7.9%), and at mid-growth critical yield scenario £12,000 decrease (9.4%)
      - The report asserts that Aviva options have 'performed as well as' or better than Zurich (stating 'blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva'), but the 5-year performance chart [P15] shows Aviva Consolidation fund (A) at 15.58% versus Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) at 21.48%, which does not directly compare existing Zurich performance to new solution
      - The report claims Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P17] but provides no explicit cost comparison table to corroborate this specific figure against the new solution charges
      - The report identifies that Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds and requires a blend including Interest Deposits (a single-asset fund), stating this 'reduces diversification', yet the new solution [P4] is constructed from two multi-asset funds (Columbia Threadneedle 50%, HSBC 50%), which directly addresses this diversification concern
    The requirement asks whether 'other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client'. The report considers multiple alternatives and recommends consolidation into Aviva, but the evidence reveals a material tension. The report's stated rationale emphasises lower costs and better investment choice, yet the switching analysis provided by the same pension switch report [P1][P13] projects that the new solution will deliver lower maturity values than retaining existing arrangements. At mid-growth rate (2.95%), the analysis shows consolidating Zurich into the new solution reduces projected value by £10,000 (7.9%) on that single pot alone, and consolidating all arrangements reduces overall value by £12,000 (9.4%) at the same growth rate [P13]. The critical yield required from the new solution is 4.8% to match the existing arrangement — a hurdle the existing solution does not face. The report does not reconcile this contradiction: it claims the switch is justified by cost savings and better alignment with risk profile, but the quantified analysis shows the client receives less at retirement. The performance chart [P15] shows the Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (the recommended fund) outperformed the existing Aviva Consolidation fund over 5 years (21.48% vs 15.58%), but this does not address the comparison with Zurich, where the new consolidated solution still projects lower maturity value. The report acknowledges alternatives (do nothing, in-situ switches, employer scheme) and provides reasoning for their rejection: Scottish Widows lacks multi-asset funds and adviser management; Zurich internal switch reduces diversification and has lower performance; employer scheme has limited funds and higher costs. These reasons are internally consistent and client-specific. However, the core issue is that the primary recommendation (full consolidation into Aviva) is not supported by the maturity value analysis. The report presents a 'cost saves money/better alignment' narrative without explaining why the client should accept lower projected pension at retirement. Consolidation itself is a stated client objective (evidenced in the alternatives analysis), which is a valid guard — the client did ask for consolidation. The retention of People's Pension (current workplace scheme) is appropriate and recommended. But the consolidation of Zurich, Aviva, and Standard Life into a new Aviva plan is justified on cost and choice grounds that the switching analysis does not support in terms of retirement outcome. The alternatives considered are genuine and reasons given for rejection are sound, but the recommended option's justification does not align with its quantified consequences.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report claims multiple benefits justify the switch to the new Aviva Platform solution despite higher overall costs including adviser fees. Key assertions: (1) 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' (page 7); (2) 'Lifestyling' risk requires exit from existing funds which will move outside agreed risk range; (3) 'Greater Investment Choice' offering wider options better suited to risk profile; (4) 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' enabling ongoing support; (5) 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' capability; (6) Tax efficiency benefits; (7) Enhanced retirement flexibility. Crucially, the report also states: 'Including adviser fees, Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans. This means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year' and 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan.'
    File:     The pension switching report [P1-P4, P6-P8, P11-P13] provides detailed comparative analysis. At mid-growth rate (2.95%), the effect of switching the Zurich pension alone shows the new solution would achieve £115,000 vs existing £127,000, a 9.4% decrease [P4]. The switching report summary [P13] recommends consolidating existing pensions into the new Aviva solution. The report itself acknowledges in Appendix that adviser fees totalling £3,305 initial plus 1% ongoing are included in projections [P17-P18]. The comparison tables show the new solution ranked 5th out of top performers at projected value of £125,000 versus existing solution at £127,000 [P8]. The Zurich policy documentation [P9] lists available features including fund switches at no charge, flexible access options, and death benefits. No evidence is provided in the file that the new Aviva solution offers materially expanded fund choice compared to what was available on existing plans, nor that lifestyling was unavoidable on existing arrangements through internal fund switches without incurring adviser fees.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' as a benefit while simultaneously stating the plan is 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees
      - Report asserts 'Lower Costs' benefit when at mid-growth rates the new solution underperforms by £12,000 or 9.4% over the term to age 75, contradicting the cost advantage narrative
      - Report does not disclose that Zurich policy allows fund switches at no charge [P5, P9], yet cites lifestyling as justification for switching providers entirely rather than switching funds within the existing plan
      - Report claims 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit but provides no comparative fund count or evidence that existing plans lacked suitable fund options for the client's risk profile
      - Report recommends switching despite ranking analysis showing existing Zurich solution (ranked 1st at £128,000) and existing overall position (ranked 2nd at £127,000) outperforming the new solution (ranked 5th at £125,000) at mid-growth rates [P8]
      - Report states adviser fees enable 'ongoing financial planning support' as a benefit, but existing arrangements could have accommodated ongoing advice and monitoring without requiring a full provider switch
    The requirement asks whether evidence supports claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits. The report makes a fundamental contradiction: it asserts 'Lower Costs' as a primary benefit while explicitly acknowledging the plan is 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees are included. This creates an internal inconsistency. The report's own arithmetic shows that at mid-growth rates (2.95%), the new solution would produce £115,000 versus £127,000 on the Zurich pension alone—a 9.4% shortfall requiring 4.8% annual outperformance to break even [P4, P18]. The guard 'where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite' does not apply here; the plan is measurably more expensive. The claimed benefits do not align with the evidence: (1) Lower Costs is contradicted by the report's own cost comparison. (2) Lifestyling avoidance could have been achieved by internal fund switching on the existing Zurich plan at no charge [P5, P9], not requiring a full switch to a new provider. (3) Greater Investment Choice is asserted without comparative evidence of fund count or client-specific needs justifying access to additional funds. (4) The ranking analysis [P8] shows the existing solution outperforms the new solution, undermining the overall rationale. (5) Adviser charging facilitation is presented as a benefit, but adviser fees increase costs rather than reduce them, and ongoing advice was available without switching. The report does not adequately evidence why these specific benefits justify accepting a 4.24% cost increase and a 9.4% reduction in projected retirement value at mid-growth rates. The critical yield analysis acknowledges the drag but does not explain why the client should accept it.

        "The costs for this service are 1% per annum. Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year."

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts multiple rationales for consolidating existing pensions (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) into an Aviva Platform SIPP: (1) Small pot encashment of £200 would yield £170 net after tax; (2) Aviva platform is 0.24% less expensive than current plans like-for-like, allowing underperformance of £264 in year one; (3) Including adviser fees, Aviva is 4.24% more expensive, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in year one; (4) Based on mid-growth rate of 2.94% and adviser fees, recommended plan needs 4.8% annual growth to age 75 to match existing value, claimed as achievable based on past fund performance; (5) Zurich investments are in higher-risk funds with more volatility, while Aviva funds align with risk tolerance and deliver comparable performance with reduced volatility; (6) Consolidation offers better control and unified management; (7) Workplace scheme (People's Pension) should be retained because employer contributions continue; (8) Scottish Widows should be retained as it allows servicing and flexibility; (9) Lifestyle funds in existing arrangements will move outside agreed risk range approaching retirement, making them unsuitable; (10) New scheme offers flexible retirement options and Flexi Access Drawdown aligned with client preference.
    File:     The Pension Switch Report (P5, P6, P20) provides comparative analysis: At medium growth rate, existing solution projects £127,000 at age 75 versus new solution £125,000 (a decrease of £2,000 or 1.6%). The Zurich pension alone projects £128,000, outperforming both existing and new solutions. The switch report ranks existing solution as 2nd at 0.3% charge, new solution as 5th at 0.6% charge, below Zurich at 0.2%. Provider documents (P8, P9, P14) confirm Aviva plan is in 'Lifestage Investment Approach' with auto-switching; Zurich has no guarantees, bonuses or safeguards; Standard Life offers annuity options. File notes (P16, P17) record that Zurich was discounted because 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used' and 'wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types.' Aviva alternative was discounted 'as only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.' Standard Life was discounted as 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive.' Provider literature (P2, P4) states transfers mean loss of features/guarantees and creates 'out of market' period without investment benefit.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Aviva platform is 0.24% less expensive than current plans like-for-like; switch report shows new solution at 0.6% vs existing solution at 0.3%, a difference of +0.3% (more expensive, not less)
      - Report claims Aviva plan can underperform by £264 in first year based on 0.24% cost advantage; derived figures show 0.24% on £110,185 fund value implies £264.44 possible underperformance, but this contradicts the switch report showing new solution more expensive at 0.6% vs 0.3%
      - Report states at mid-growth 2.94% the recommended plan would achieve same value as current plan with 4.8% annual growth; switch report projects new solution at £125,000 vs existing at £127,000 at age 75, showing new solution underperforms by £2,000 (1.6%) even accounting for assumed growth rates, contradicting achievability claim
      - Report asserts Aviva funds deliver comparable performance with reduced volatility vs Zurich; switch report ranking shows Zurich pension alone at £128,000 (rank 1) outperforming new solution at £125,000 (rank 5) and existing £127,000 (rank 2)
      - Report claims 4.24% cost disadvantage including adviser fees requiring £4,672 outperformance; derived figure shows 4.24% on £110,188 implies £4,672 calculation is internally consistent but contradicts switch report showing new solution projected to underperform by £2,000, not outperform by £4,672
      - Report states Zurich investments in higher-risk funds are unsuitable; file notes confirm Zurich is risk 7 (not risk 5) but recommend against internal switch to Zurich because 'main priority is to consolidate' not because Zurich funds themselves are unsuitable—rationale conflates fund choice with consolidation benefit
      - Report recommends consolidation for better control and unified management without addressing that file notes show alternatives (Standard Life, Zurich blends) were discounted purely on cost/fund availability grounds, not on consolidation benefit analysis
      - Report states lifestyle funds will move outside risk range; evidence (P8) confirms Aviva plan has 'Lifestage Investment Approach' (auto-switching) but report does not clarify whether new Aviva solution eliminates this problem or merely replaces one auto-switching fund with another
    The report's replacement rationale rests primarily on cost savings and risk alignment. The switch report directly contradicts the cost-saving claim: it ranks the new solution (0.6% charge) below the existing solution (0.3% charge) and projects underperformance of £2,000 by age 75, not the claimed outperformance. The 0.24% 'like-for-like' cost advantage asserted in the report is not supported by the switch report data; instead the switch shows new solution as more expensive. The critical yield assertion (4.8% required, claimed achievable) is contradicted by the switch report's own projections showing the new solution underperforms even with assumed mid-growth rates. The volatility and risk rationale is contradicted by the Zurich pension's superior projected performance (£128,000) over the recommended new solution (£125,000). File notes show alternatives were rejected on cost/platform grounds, not on fundamental unsuitability, yet the report does not acknowledge that the chosen solution projects to underperform available alternatives. The lifestyle fund risk issue is acknowledged but the report does not clarify whether the new Aviva solution (also with Lifestage approach per P8) solves this problem or merely transfers it. The 'better control' and 'consolidation' rationales are stated but unsupported by comparative analysis of administrative or flexibility benefits. The rationale for retaining People's Pension rests on ongoing employer contributions (supported by P21 showing workplace scheme status) but the report does not quantify the contribution value being protected. The small pot encashment rationale (£170 net on £200) is mathematically sound but the £200 figure itself is not evidenced in the documents provided. The recommendation is presented as consistent with risk tolerance but the switch report shows it as more expensive and underperforming, and file notes record that alternatives at better cost were actively rejected. The guard permits rationales resting on charges to be evaluated against charges schedules; here the charges comparison directly contradicts the report's stated cost advantage.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that no employer contributions are lost across four replacement analyses (employerContributionsLost: false, false, false, false). The report states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for all five existing arrangements, quoted from page 5 of the Suitability Report. The report presents a critical yield analysis showing the effect of switching at mid-growth rate (2.95%) as '£12,000 or 9.4% decrease' in projected value at age 75.
    File:     The Peoples Pension Policy Info documents (passages P1-P10) show regular employer contributions being made across multiple periods from 2021 to 2023, with employer contribution entries clearly documented. The Zurich Policy Info (P11) states: 'If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred. This exit charge is a standard term of the contract. The charge is taken in order to recover the charges, which would be due, if the fund were to remain invested with Zurich until the customer has reached their selected retirement age (SRA). The charge is taken by reducing the number of capital units paid out; the charge reduces by around 0.3%-0.4% a month until SRA where there is no charge. The capped at 1% from age 55.' The Pension Switch Report (P22) shows at mid-growth rate projected value for existing pension at £127,000 vs new solution at £115,000, representing a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. However, it does not disclose whether employer contributions will cease or continue post-transfer.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states no exit penalties ('No charges for transfer and/or switching') for all arrangements, but Zurich policy explicitly describes exit charges that apply on transfer: charges reducing by 0.3%-0.4% per month until SRA, capped at 1% from age 55.
      - Report asserts employer contributions not lost (employerContributionsLost: false across all four analyses) but does not disclose whether contributions are active/ongoing or whether they will cease upon transfer from these apparent workplace schemes.
      - Report presents switching cost analysis but does not explicitly state whether the critical yield and effect-of-switching figures account for future employer contributions or assume cessation.
      - Extraction notes indicate /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost marked as 'NotPresentInDocument', suggesting safeguarded benefits are not addressed in the report.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Confirmation from provider documentation that the Zurich exit charge does not apply (guard: 'A nil exit penalty confirmed by the provider and left out of the report is not a finding; an unmentioned penalty that exists is'); only the Zurich policy document confirms charges exist., Explicit disclosure of whether Peoples Pension employer contributions are active/ongoing at time of advice or will cease upon transfer., Evidence whether the client is still an active member of any workplace scheme (guard: 'An employer contribution that stops on leaving service is not lost by the transfer; check whether the client is still an active member')., Documentation of any safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features that may be lost on the transfers recommended., Clarification of whether critical yield and switching cost projections assume continuation or cessation of employer contributions.
    The comparison cannot be completed safely because three material inputs are absent. First, the Zurich Policy Info plainly states exit charges apply on transfer, but the report discloses 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. The guard states an unmentioned penalty that exists is a finding; this is an unmentioned charge that exists in the file. Second, the Peoples Pension records show employer contributions but the report does not state whether the client is an active member whose contributions would cease, or whether they would continue post-transfer. The guard requires checking active membership status; this is not evidenced. Third, the extraction flags that features or guarantees lost are not present in the document, yet Zurich's documentation describes specific features (the exit charge structure tied to SRA) which may be material. The report cannot be said to have addressed safeguarded benefits if the extraction could not parse them. The switching cost analysis in the report (critical yield, effect of switching) does not disclose whether projections include or exclude future employer contributions—material to the justification. The Zurich exit charge is plainest: the report contradicts the provider document by asserting no transfer charges when Zurich documentation establishes they exist and reduce over time. This is not explained by any document in the pack. Under the decision logic, material replacement-related information is omitted: the Zurich charges are not disclosed, the status and fate of employer contributions is not explained, and safeguarded features or guarantees are not addressed.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The canonical model asserts isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=false but no document explicitly confirms or denies whether a Power of Attorney is currently in force; the agreement language is permissive (allowing future arrangement) rather than declarative of current status. The report does not assert or describe any support needs, adjustments, or third-party arrangements in the extracted fields, but the file contains questionnaire responses indicating lack of knowledge in core investment and pension areas; no discussion of how this knowledge gap was addressed is recorded. Risk profile and fact find both record 'No additional notes' on key support topics despite the client's stated lack of understanding on fundamental investment concepts. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.2] Communication preferences and formats
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model contains no extracted value at /vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences. The suitability report itself is silent on the client's communication preferences or any formats, adjustments, or accessibility considerations specific to this client.
    File:     The case file contains multiple references to communication preferences and methods: [P9] records 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference' in the Fact Find. [P24] describes Aviva's general policy that clients may receive communications via email, post, or other means, and states 'You may change Your address, email and other personal details either by contacting Us or through Our Adviser.' [P3] confirms the adviser accepts third-party instructions via Power of Attorney. [P1] and [P5] set out the general Client Agreement framework but do not record this specific client's preferences. The Fact Find at [P9] shows a checkbox field for 'Preferred delivery method' marked as 'No Preference,' indicating the question was asked and answered.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not reference or discuss the client's communication preference recorded in the Fact Find.
      - The report does not acknowledge or act upon the 'No Preference' response recorded at [P9].
      - No communication format, accessibility requirement, or delivery method is discussed in the suitability report despite the Fact Find having captured this information.
    The Fact Find [P9] records that the client was asked about preferred delivery method and selected 'No Preference.' This is a direct answer to a communication preference question, captured as part of the client intake process. The suitability report is entirely silent on this. The absence of a discussion about communication preferences in the report does not establish that none exist; rather, it shows that information captured in the client's file—the Fact Find—was not carried through to or reflected in the suitability report's narrative. The guard noting that 'a standard contact preference is not a vulnerability adjustment' is applicable here: 'No Preference' is a standard preference, not an indicator of vulnerability requiring special adjustments. However, the requirement is about consistency—whether information in the report is supported by the file, and conversely, whether material information in the file is reflected in the report. The report's silence on a captured preference is not a contradiction, but it indicates the preference was not carried forward into the adviser's reasoning or recommendations. This is a process risk: the client's stated preference was recorded but not documented as having been considered in the advice. The critical issue is whether the report should have acknowledged receipt and consideration of this preference, particularly given FG21/1's emphasis on communication suitable to the client's needs. 'No Preference' may mean the client is content with any method, but it should still have been noted as considered.

    The requirement asks whether adjustments made to support client comprehension are fully supported by evidence. However, the absence of a record of adjustments made is not the same as evidence that no adjustments were needed or that comprehension was adequately supported. The report does not explain how it ensured the client understood complex concepts (flexi-access drawdown, critical yield, capacity for loss) before proceeding. The discussions documented (file notes [P11], capacity for loss section [P5]) show that topics were covered and agreement reached, but not that adjustments were made to support understanding—for example, whether simplified explanations were provided, whether the client was given time to ask questions, whether documents were explained section-by-section, or whether any third-party (spouse Kim, who is named and contributes income) was involved in explaining the advice. The client's spouse Kim is mentioned multiple times in income and capacity discussions but the report does not state whether she attended meetings, received explanations, or was part of the comprehension process. The report confirms the client confirmed understanding ('You confirmed') but does not evidence how that confirmation was validated or what adjustments supported it.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report makes no assertion about vulnerability, support needs, communication adjustments, or their impact on the recommendation. No statement appears in the canonical model at paths /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] or /vulnerability/foreseeableHarms[]. The report does not claim to have assessed vulnerability or to have tailored the recommendation based on any vulnerability characteristics.
    File:     The case file contains multiple indicators that would typically trigger vulnerability assessment under FCA FG21/1: (1) Risk profile responses [P1] show the client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning. (2) The client's investment experience is limited to Current Accounts and Savings Accounts, managed 'by myself' [P1]. (3) The client has no prior experience with Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products in the last 5 years [P1]. (4) Capacity for Loss questionnaire section [P2] records 'No additional notes were recorded' despite the responses above. (5) The fact find [P6] records the client 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has.' This suggests limited comprehension and need for simplification. (6) Current monthly cash flow [P12] shows total disposable income of £-288.00, indicating financial pressure. (7) The suitability report [P14] states 'John, you are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' This acknowledges limited experience. (8) The report [P14] records the client was 'shown a projection' and risk level was altered 'until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved', suggesting the initial questionnaire result (Risk 4) was not the client's true preference and required discussion to adjust to Risk 5. (9) The fact find [P6] identifies the client as 'ATR 5' (Attitude to Risk 5) but also notes he 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years', placing him in a very short timeframe for pension consolidation with elevated risk exposure.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no vulnerability assessment or impact on the recommendation, yet the file contains indicators that would normally trigger such assessment: limited product knowledge (stocks, shares, pensions), limited investment experience, and expressed need for help understanding existing arrangements.
      - The report does not reference or discuss the client's knowledge limitations acknowledged in [P1] where he rated 'No understanding / knowledge' for three product categories.
      - The report does not document or reference the fact find note [P6] that the client 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has', which indicates comprehension support needs.
      - The report does not address the negative monthly disposable income (£-288.00) recorded in [P12], which indicates financial pressure and potential capacity for loss concerns.
      - Risk rating adjustment from 4 to 5 is recorded in [P14] but no formal vulnerability or capability assessment explaining why the client's initial questionnaire answer was overridden is documented.
      - The Capacity for Loss questionnaire section [P2] records 'No additional notes were recorded', leaving no evidence of adviser discussion or assessment of the client's true capacity in light of limited experience.
    The trigger for this check indicates that checkTriggers.hasVulnerabilityIndicators = false, meaning the extraction did not identify a formal vulnerability flag in the report. However, the check is applied to every case to assess whether vulnerability was considered and addressed. The task instructs: 'Do not read the missing trigger as evidence that the check's subject is absent: whether it was ever considered is part of what you are assessing.' The evidence shows multiple FG21/1 drivers present in the file: the client demonstrates very limited understanding of investment products (stocks, shares, pensions, EIS/VCTs) [P1], limited experience (only cash/savings products managed by himself) [P1], and explicitly seeks help to 'get his head around what he has' [P6]. The client's risk rating was adjusted upward from the questionnaire result (4 to 5) [P14], but no documented assessment explains how this decision accounts for his limited knowledge and comprehension needs. The Capacity for Loss questionnaire section [P2] records no adviser notes despite these knowledge gaps. The negative monthly disposable income [P12] indicates financial pressure. The report acknowledges 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' [P14] but does not discuss how this limitation affects suitability of the recommendation or what adjustments were made to support understanding. The fact find note that the client 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' is not reflected in the report's discussion of communication methods, advice delivery, or post-recommendation support. Under PRIN 2A.2.1R and FCA FG21/1, where a client has demonstrated low capability (limited product knowledge, limited experience) and expressed comprehension support needs, the report should explicitly address whether the recommended Risk 5 balanced portfolio in a 2-year retirement timeframe is appropriate given those characteristics, and what steps were taken to ensure informed decision-making. The absence of this assessment—despite the presence of clear indicators—constitutes an unsupported claim that the recommendation is suitable for this client.
